substances, activate transmission factors (CREB). PKA

can sometimes also involve the activation of ion

channels, but generally it acts as an intermediary that
delays the activation or signalling process.

**PKA** is composed of 4 subunits, 2 homodimers: 2
regulatory subunits, and 2 catalytic subunits which have
kinase activity.

**Alteration of GPCR** in the intestine and respiratory system by Cholera toxin and Bordetella
Pertussis (here we can see the importance of shutting off signalling):

   - **Cholera toxin** is going to activate alphaG
subunit, so the protein kinase A will be
constantly activated. It binds to a stimulatory
G subunit, and induces an activation of the
G protein. It stimulates the **opening of ion**

**channels** and release of Cl-. Water exits

enterocytes to equilibrate osmotic

concentration and results in diarrhea.

   - The **Bordetella toxin** will act by inhibiting
the inhibition, thus increasing PKA activity.

**b) Phospholipase C-beta** : also found in the membrane, has to be bound to G-GTP protein
to become activated. It results in the production of **inositol-3-phosphate and DAG. I3P** is a
second messenger that induces the **release of calcium** ; **DAG** will stimulate the release of
**PKC** and the production of arachidonic acid, a precursor of eicosanoids. Basically,
phospholipase liberates secondary messengers that will have different effects on the cell.

*[PDF p. 83]*

**c) Guanylate cyclase** : Once activated through G proteins, it generates **cyclic GMP,** which
comes from GTP. This cGMP is activated through **NO** . In muscles, high cGMP levels induce
smooth muscle relaxation. It functions for example in the process of sight, how we
distinguish dark from light. Brain detects dark when cGMP is high. The rapid entry of ions
generates a depolarization of the plasma membrane which results in a rapid nerve impulse
to the brain. This stimulus indicates the absence of light. When the GPCR is activated,

cGMP is converted to GTP and the ion channels are closed. The membrane becomes

hyperpolarized, and the brain senses this stimulus as the presence of light.

**Example of GPCR signalling:** release of **glucagon**
**and insulin** ( _beta cells=insulin, alpha-cells=glucagon)._

➢ Regulation of glycemia:

When there is high glucose in blood, beta cells
induce the production of insulin which reduces
glucose blood levels. It stimulates the glycogen
storage in the liver (can also happen in the
muscle by forming saccharide-bound proteins,
or in fat by forming lipidic droplets). If blood
levels are low, the body releases glucagon and
these processes are reverted.

**Signal amplification process:** one molecule that stimulates receptors can stimulate the
activation of thousands of signalling processes.

*[PDF p. 84]*

**Lecture 4.3. Signalling through ion channels**

**Relevance and main characteristics**

Ion channels are present in every cell, but they are particularly important in electrically
excitable cells (neurons, muscles,...)

**Characteristics:**

-Ions move **down the concentration gradient** (from less to more)
-They have a very **high rate of transport**, no need to interact with receptors.
-Two major types of ion channel receptors:

   **voltage gated:** the channel is regulated by **changes in**
**membrane potential**, it will open or close depending on that.
In the resting situation, membrane potential is such that there
is a negative charge in the inner membrane. A depolarization
involves a positive charge inside the membrane and negative
outside. This promotes that the protein channels open and ions

can enter the cell.

                   **ligand- gated:** channels open or close depending on the
**binding of** one **specific molecule** like acetylcholine. In the case of
Ach and Na+: two acetylcholine molecules bind to the channel,
which opens and allows sodium to enter the cell.

-Main ions involved: Na+, K+, Cl- and Ca 2+

**Acetylcholine and its receptors**

There are many receptors for **Acetylcholine** . It’s a very plastic molecule, it can act through
different types of receptors and have a very different outcome, depending on the receptor
and the cell. We can differentiate two types of receptors:

   - **Nicotinic:** Called nicotinic because this channel can also be activated by nicotine.
That’s why nicotine is an agonist of Ach. Ach binds to an ion channel receptor.
   - **Muscarinic:** Ach binds to a G Protein Coupled receptor (GPCR). Called muscarinic
because it also binds to muscarine, a toxin from mushrooms.

**How acetylcholine acts in neuromuscular junction:**

First, there is a **depolarization** of the membrane. This depolarization causes the **release of**
**calcium** into the cell ( _the presence of Ca2+ in a cell generally promotes exocytosis of_
_vesicles_ ), which promotes the **exocytosis** of **vesicles with Ach** into the synaptic space. It
then **binds** to its receptor in the **muscle fiber** . The **channel opens** and **Na+ enters** .

*[PDF p. 85]*

This propagates the stimulus in the muscle fiber: the axon potential propagates through the
membrane invaginations ( **T tubules**, because they are in a T shape). These tubules, at
some point, contact with the **sarcoplasmic reticulum** / SER in muscle fibers. The
depolarization and the close contact between membrane and reticulum **opens Ca2+**
**channels**, promoting its release into the cytosol. This will then cause **muscle contraction**,
as Ca2+ binds to troponin and tropomyosin, leaving the actin’s active site free for myosin

heads to bind to.

To stop contraction, Ca2+ must be
removed from the cytosol. For this
there are **calcium pumps** that actively
(with energy consumption) move

calcium back into the SER.

To stop the whole process, acetylcholine is either
**diffused** or **broken down** by an enzyme found in
the synaptic cleft **(** _**acetylcholine-esterase**_ **)** into
acetic acid and choline, that is reabsorbed to

generate more acetylcholine.
_(Sarcolemma=membrane of a muscle fiber)_

**Related pathologies. Alteration of the signaling process:** **MYASTHENIA GRAVIS**

It's an autoimmune disease where

**antibodies** are produced **against**
**acetylcholine receptors.**
They bind to the receptors and block
them, so Ach cannot bind properly to the
receptor in the channel. It isn’t a
complete blockage, otherwise the patient

would die in a few minutes. There is

some **muscle contraction**, but it's **impaired** .
Antibodies bound to the receptors are also then incorporated through endocytosis into the
cell, because the antibodies stimulate this process. The **channel and the antibody** are
**swallowed** and destroyed by lysosomes, so as a result there is a lesser amount of receptors
and channels. There is less signalling but also less receptors. The effect of this is muscle
weakness (ex: dropped eyelids/ptosis, lacking facial expressions,...), extreme fatigue,...

*[PDF p. 86]*

The treatment: using immunosuppressors (corticosteroids) to block antibody production, or

the use of **AchE inhibitors:** it blocks the activity of
_acetylcholinesterase_, and keeps the signalling going for longer.
Some AchE inhibitors may be fatal, but used in small doses with this
pathology can improve movements.

**Toxins that interfere with signalling process**

   - **Tetanus toxin:** produced by _clostridium tetani_, which causes hyperactivation
of signalling. Too much Ach is released, causing muscle rigidity, spasms and
paralysis due to hyperactivity.
   - **Botulinic toxin:** produced by _clostridium botulinum_ . The bacteria blocks the
exocitosis process that relesases Ach, and therefore there is no signalling.
As a result there is muscle inactivity, which can also be fatal (hypoactivation).
Nowadays it can be used for the treatment of migraines, muscle spasms or
hyperhidrosis (increased sweating).

*[PDF p. 87]*

**TBL 4.1. Signalling through tyrosine kinases**

Cellular communication is possible thanks to signals that stimulate receptors. We find
intracellular receptors, and membrane receptors that we will study today.

**SIGNALING THROUGH MEMBRANE RECEPTORS**

**Membrane receptors** are **amphipathic** molecules that collect extracellular signals and
transmit them to the interior of the cell, activating a protein cascade that will result in gene
transcription and the production of a protein.
There are 3 types of membrane proteins:

   Ligand gated ion channels

   G protein coupled receptors

   Enzymatic or catalytic receptors. We will focus on these last ones:

**Enzymatic or catalytic membrane receptors** are enzymes that once activated, due to
ligand bind, enhance the signal transmission through cytoplasm proteins.They have an
extracellular domain (where ligand binding occurs), a transmembrane domain and an
intracellular domain (which confers the catalytic activity).

This catalytic activity can be a **kinase activity: kinases** are
enzymes that catalyze the **transfer of a phosphate** group
from a donor ATP to a substrate. This process regulates
many intracellular signalling cascades.

**Tyrosine-kinase receptors (RTK)**

Like all membrane receptors, they possess an extracellular
domain, cross-membrane domain and intracellular domain. Once
activated by a ligand, they enhance the **phosphorylation of**
**tyrosine residues**, which will lead to a cellular response.
Tyrosine-kinase is a dimer. The ligand binds to either one or two
subunits. When in **monomeric form,** they are **inactive** . After the
binding of the signal molecule, the monomers join and form the dimer. In order to get
activated, a **cross-phosphorylation** has to occur by consuming ATP.
Active form = phosphorylated dimer.

An example of a tyrosine
kinase signalling is the RAS
pathway. **RAS** is a membrane
protein that belongs to a
small GTPase protein family,

which carries out the

**RAS-RAF-MEK-ERK**

**pathway.** At the end of the
process, ERK suppresses
upstream pathway signalling.

*[PDF p. 88]*

**30%** of human **cancers** are related to a **hyperstimulation** of this
pathway. This hyperstimulation stimulates uncontrolled growth and
tumor appearance.
Action mechanism: a ligand binds to the receptor, and the second
subunit binds to the 1st RTK. Then cross-phosphorylation occurs.
Now activated, they can recruit the intracellular proteins. **RAS** is
activated by **GEF** and stays attached to the membrane. Many **RAS**
proteins are activated = signal amplification. Then **RAF** is activated,
joining RAS and forming an inner structure. Then **MEK** and **ERK** are activated. Activated
ERK translocates to the nucleus and stimulates many processes that end in gene
transcription and a cellular response.
The **medical relevance of tyrosine-kinase receptors** is that many drugs developed to treat
cancer act on RTK, either targeting ATP binding sites, interfering with RKT activation or
targetting RTK-expressing cells for destruction by the immune system.

**Ovarian cancer**

Known as a silent killer. It doesn’t show specific symptoms, and these appear at a late stage,
when there has already been a metastasis. Therefore it’s difficult to treat and cure. Increased
**angiogenesis** stimulates the **formation of new blood vessels** around the cell. The tumor
enhances angiogenesis by taking advantage of EGF (growth factor). Therefore angiogenesis
is targeted to stop tumor growth. Angiogenesis can be stopped by blocking EGF.

**RTK-Insulin receptor**

Another RTK receptor is the **insulin receptor** . It’s a receptor that is very abundant in cells of
the **liver**, **adipose tissue** and **skeletal muscles.** It’s a dimer made up of two extracellular

subunits and two intracellular subunits. When

**insulin** binds, intracellular tyrosine domains

become activated and there is a downstream

cascade that leads to the **translocation of**

**GLUT4 transporters**, which allows the
transport of glucose into the interior of the
cell. If glucose in the bloodstream decreases,
the GLUT4 transmembrane proteins are

stored in vesicles and removed from the

membrane.

*[PDF p. 89]*

When insulin receptor does not work properly, it leads to **insulin resistance** (a stage where
cells cannot respond to insulin and therefore can’t take up glucose from the blood. It implies
a higher-than-normal insulin concentration to maintain normal glucose levels) and **type II**
**diabetes.** Overweight and people with obesity, 45 or older, certain ethnicities, high
cholesterol levels, are all factors that can lead to this disease. Molecular mechanisms that
lead to insulin resistance are still unknown. It can be due to mutations and dysfunctions in
proteins, mitochondrial dysfunction,….

**What is DIABETES?**

It’s a chronic disease where pancreas can’t produce enough insulin or the body cannot use
the insulin it produces.

**Type I diabetes** is an **autoimmune disease** where the body cannot produce enough insulin
because **autoantibodies** attack **beta-cells in the pancreas** (those in charge of insulin
production).

**Type II diabetes** is a **metabolic disease** which prevents the **little insulin** which the body
can produce (it’s produced, although not enough) from working properly. It **can’t be taken**
**up** by cells, and therefore the body doesn't recognize high glucose levels in the blood.
Factors that can impact type II diabetes: mutations in genes resulting in mutated proteins,
environmental factors such as weight and diet,...

*[PDF p. 90]*

**Lecture 5.1. Bioenergetics**

**Forms of obtaining carbon and energy:**

**Metabolic pathways** are a sequence of reactions that, through the continuous modification
of a precursor, transform it into a product. These reactions are catalyzed by enzymes.

**Types of chemical reactions in metabolism:**

-Transfer of functional groups

-Oxidation and reduction

-Isomerization

-Hydrolysis/condensation
-Formation or cleavage of C-C bonds

**Metabolic phases**

  - **Catabolism:** degradative phase in which nutrients are degraded into their simpler
subunits, to obtain precursors (CO2, H2O, NH3). Two main goals: to **obtain energy**
and to obtain **precursors** . The main strategy to do this is the **oxidation of the**
**carbon atoms**, by removing electrons. This produces energy, which is stored as
**ATP**, and the released electrons are collected and stored in molecules called
**electron carriers.** They collect e- from the carbon molecules which are oxidized.

  - **Anabolism:** It’s a process in which precursors obtained from catabolism are used,
through ATP consumption, to generate macromolecules that our body needs to
function (lipids, proteins,...).

**Types of metabolic pathways**

The body’s interest in making the most of its resources
is reflected in the metabolic pathways. All nutrients are
converged towards a common element (normally
**Acetyl-CoA** ) so the same enzymes can be used at
the end of the catabolic pathway.
**Catabolism** is **convergent**, and **anabolism** is
**divergent** .

*[PDF p. 91]*

**Catabolic stages**

   **1st stage:** big molecules are degraded into small molecules: fats into fatty acids and
glycerol, polysaccharides into glucose and other sugars, and proteins into amino
acids. This occurs by **hydrolysis**, which **doesn’t require energy.**

   **2nd stage:** small molecules are further broken down to finally obtain
**Acetyl-CoA,** which is the main goal of this phase (although some energy
is released). It’s a convergent phase.

   **3rd stage:** Acetyl-CoA undergoes a series of reactions (Krebs cycle/citric
acid cycle). Most of the ATP is generated in this phase.

**Regulatory mechanisms in metabolism**

   - Control of **enzyme concentration**
   - Control of **enzyme activity:** either by allosteric
control or covalent modification (such as
phosphorylation)

   **Compartmentalization** of enzymes and
substrates (ex: cellular respiration only happens
in the mitochondria)

Pathways can be regulated by the own products of the pathway.

**ATP**

**Adenosine triphosphate is broken** down to produce **ADP** or **AMP**, by hydrolysis, which
releases a big amount of energy that is used by our body.

*[PDF p. 92]*

**Lecture 5.2. Electron transport**

**Gibbs free energy change** lets us know about the direction of a reaction. The direction in
which the **free energy change** is **negative** will be the **spontaneous reaction**, and
therefore, the direction in which the process will take place.
The free energy change is not a fixed number, it changes towards equilibrium.
Gibbs free energy provides NO info about the reaction rate. That depends on the activation
energy and the transition state.

How to calculate the free energy change of a reaction knowing the conditions:
(R= gas constant: 8.3 J/mol) )

Example: Glucose 6P to FRuctose 6P

It 's positive. How come this process occurs in our cell?
Depending on the concentration of the molecules, the free energy rate will be positive or
negative in one direction.
The **direction** will therefore be **determined by the concentration of molecules.** The free
energy change changes with the molecule concentration.

What is the ratio in the equilibrium? We equal the equation to 0. This ratio will determine if a
reaction is **reversible or irreversible** . If it’s too high, it’s not possible in physiological
conditions, and therefore won't happen.

In conclusion: **all processes that take place in a cell are exergonic**

**Degradation** and **synthesis** follow different processes, because not all reactions are
reversible. This allows the cell to regulate the processes independently, avoiding reactions
which would be useless. It allows **coordinate and reciprocal regulation.**

**Coupling reactions** allows to establish a negative free energy change, permitting
irreversible reactions to take place. This can be done by breaking ATP bonds.

There are two main strategies to obtain ATP: **Substrate**
**level phosphorylation** (because the element we are using
has a high phosphorylation potential) and **oxidative**
**phosphorylation.**

*[PDF p. 93]*

**Carbon oxidation releases energy**

Malate to oxaloacetate = oxidation. Electrons released are collected by NAD+, which
becomes NADH (its reduced form). When there is an oxidation, there also has to be a

reduction.

**Electron carriers in metabolism:** NAD +, NADP +, FAD and FMN.
**NAD+ and NADP+**, made up of a nicotinamide ring which is the active site of the molecule.
NADP is more frequently used in anabolism.
**FAD** (flavin adenine diphosphate) and **FMN** (... mononucleotide). They typically participate in
reactions in which double bounds are produced.

*[PDF p. 94]*

**Lecture 5.3 and 5.4. Generation of ATP from glucose and other sugars**

**Glucose** is an excellent fuel. Nervous tissue and erythrocytes preferably use glucose and
carbohydrates as their main fuel.
-Its oxidation yields **-2840 kJ/mol glucose** after the full oxidation of backbone carbons. It’s
highly reduced and contains a high amount of electrons, which will allow to obtain ATP
through oxidative phosphorylation.
-It can be efficiently **stored** in polymeric form ( **glycogen** ).
-Many tissues (neurons, erythrocytes,...) can meet their energy needs on glucose only.
-It’s a versatile **biochemical precursor**, as it can be used to generate all amino-acids,
membrane lipids,nucleotides,...

We can use glucose to obtain energy through the oxidation of the full carbon backbone.

**GLYCOLYSIS**

**Glycolysis** is a series of enzyme-catalyzed reactions where glucose (6C) is broken down
into two pyruvate molecules (3C).The most of the energy released during glycolysis remains
in the form of **NADH** and **pyruvate** . Not much energy is released during glycolysis. Pyruvate
can be further oxidized or used as a precursor for biosynthesis.
It takes place in the **cytoplasm** and **doesn’t need O2** : it can be done with or without
oxygen. Some tissues in our body carry out glycolysis aways in anaerobic conditions, like
erythrocytes. They contain hemoglobin with O2, but they can’t use it as they lack
mitochondria and so they can’t carry out cellular respiration. When glycolysis happens
without O2, the last step is fermentation. This process also happens in skeletal muscles,
when their activity is higher than the mitochondrias possibility to reoxidize NADH.
We can differentiate two steps in glycolysis: the **preparatory phase** (glucose is prepared for
breakdown. ATP is consumed) and **payoff phase** (from which we are able to obtain energy).

**-**
**Preparatory phase:** in this phase, we need
energy, so **2 ATPs are consumed**, but this will
be compensated for in the next phase. First,
**glucose undergoes phosphorylation**,
avoiding the possibility for it to be released out
of the cell because of the negative charge
present on the phosphate. Also this negative
charge allows the substrate to establish many
interactions with enzyme active sites.

**1.** **Step 1: Glucose phosphorylation** (usually catalyzed by kinases: they
transfer one phosphate from ATP). This step is catalyzed by _**hexokinase**_,

which can also work on fructose and

mannose.

The **liver** is a carbohydrate-storage organ. It
doesn’t express hexokinase, but a different
isozyme, _**glucokinase**_ . The only difference

*[PDF p. 95]*

between them is their affinity for glucose ( _hexokinase_ more than _glucokinase_ ).
This is so the liver, because it stores glucose, doesn’t consume all the
glucose and fill the blood with sugars.
If not, we would always be in hypoglycemia. For the liver, the main
energy-obtaining pathway is beta-oxidation of fatty acids.

**2.** **Step 2: Glucose-6-phosphate isomerization:** it’s
isomerized into **fructose-6-phosphate** by
_**phosphohexose isomerase.**_ This step is

reversible.

**3.** **Step 3: 2nd priming phosphorylation** :
_**Phosphofructokinase-1**_ (PFK-1) phosphorylates
carbon 1. It’s a critical step in glucose degradation
regulation. It’s regulated by the increase of cAMP
and ADP (which indicate low energy). It generates
**fructose-1,6-bisphosphate.**

**4.** **Step 4: Aldol cleavage:** fructose-1,6-bisphosphate is broken down by an
_**aldolase**_ between carbon 3 and 4. Carbons1,2,3 become a ketone,
**dihydroxyacetone-phosphate**, and carbons 4,5,6 become
**glyceraldehyde-3-phosphate** (G3P).

**5.** **Step 5:** _Triose phosphate isomerase_ then converts
dihydroxyacetone-phosphate into
glyceraldehyde-3-phosphate, so we obtain **2**

**molecules of G3P** . This isomerization will allow the

G3P molecules to undergo subsequent reactions of
glycolysis.

**-**
**Payoff phase:**

**6.** **Step 6: Oxidation of G3P.** An
**inorganic phosphate** is added to
G3P by an enzyme,
_glyceraldehyde-3-phosphate_
_dehydrogenase_ . Electrons are
released and captured by **NAD+**,
which converts to **NADH** . A **high**
**energy molecule** is produced,
**1,3-bisphosphoglycerate.**

**7.** **Step 7: 1st production of ATP:** It
occurs by **substrate level**
**phosphorylation.** The phospho
group in C1 is transferred to ADP by
_**phosphoglycerate kinase.**_

*[PDF p. 96]*

The hydrolysis of this bond releases enough energy to phosphorylate **ADP**,
releasing **ATP** . 3-phosphoglycerate is produced as a product.

**8.** **Step 8: Migration of the phosphate**, which is transferred **from C3 to C2.**
We go from 3-phosphoglycerate to **2-phosphoglycerate,** a reaction which is
catalyzed by _**phosphoglycerate mutase.**_

**9.** **Step 9:** There is a **dehydration** carried out by an _enolase_, turning it to
**phosphoenolpyruvate** (with a double bond). This is the second **high-energy**
**intermediate** of glycolysis, which will allow to obtain ATP through substrate
level phosphorylation in the next stage. It has a **high phosphate transfer**
**potential.**

**10. Step 10: 2nd production of ATP** . Phosphoenolpyruvate loses its phospho
group. It’s transferred by a _**pyruvate kinase**_ to an ADP molecule, which
results in the synthesis of **ATP** . Finally, we obtain **Pyruvate** .

**Glycolysis balance:** 2ATPs are consumed in the preparatory phase, but out of the two G3P
molecules, each yield 2 ATPs. Therefore, the payoff phase gives us 4 ATP. The final net
balance is **2 ATPs, 2 NADH + H+ and 2 pyruvate molecules.**

**Regulation of glycolysis**

Glycolysis is regulated through 3 enzymes: hexokinase, phosphofructokinase-1 and
pyruvate kinase.

   - **Hexokinase** is allosterically **inhibited** by its product, **glucose-6-phosphate.**
   - **Phosphofructokinase** is allosterically **activated** by low energy markers ( **AMP** and
**ADP** ) and **fructose 2,6-bisphosphate,** and **inhibited** by **ATP and citrate,** when in
high amounts, means there is already enough energy in the mitochondria, indicating
there is no need to oxidize high amounts of glucose.

   - **Pyruvate-kinase** has **two isozymes: M** (skeletal muscle,...) **and L** (liver). They are
**allosterically inhibited** by **ATP, Acetyl-CoA** (indicates enough energy is going to be
produced) **and alanin** (because it’s a source of pyruvate. It can be converted to it, so
high amounts of alanin indicate the cell there is no need to produce more pyruvate).
In addition to this, the **L isozyme can undergo a covalent modification** by

phosphorylation and dephosphorylation. The **phosphorylated form** is
**inactive** and the **non-phosphorylated form, active.**
When blood glucose concentration is low, glucagon is released and binds to
receptors in the liver. This will increase intracellular cAMP production and
promote the activation of PKA (protein kinase A) which will phosphorylate and
inactivate pyruvate kinase L. (As it becomes phosphorylated, it goes into
inactive form). This PKA will increase in hypoglycemia.
In turn, if blood glucose concentrations are high, the pancreas releases insulin
which will trigger a series of reactions that activate a phosphatase which will
dephosphorylate pyruvate kinase. That way, it will become activated and will allow
the conversion of phosphoenolpyruvate to pyruvate and the continuation of the
degradation of glucose.

*[PDF p. 97]*

**Importance of the presence of phosphorylated compounds during glycolysis**

   The phosphate groups confer the compounds a **negative charge**, which prevents
them from escaping the cell during glycolysis.
   - The **energy released** from the breakage of anhydride bonds in ATP during the
preparatory phase is **conserved in the form of phosphoesther bonds,** which in the
pay off phase will allow the synthesis of ATP.

   The interaction between phosphate groups and the active site of the enzymes will
**decrease the activation energy** and increase the specificity of the reaction.

**Fates of pyruvate**

   - The **most part** is transported to the **mitochondria**

and oxidized into CO2.

   - There are also some **tissues without**

**mitochondria (erythrocytes)** or some that work in
**hypoxia**, like the **skeletal muscle** during sport.
Therefore, the pyruvate follows a different pathway.
**Fermentation** will be carried out, throughout lactic

fermentation.

**Lactic acid fermentation** reduces

pyruvate to lactate: NADH needs
to be restored, so electrons will be

used to reduce pyruvate into lactate. By this reduction, NADH
oxidation yields NAD+ and it’s restored. This way our cells don’t
completely waste pyruvate. In skeletal muscle, lactate accumulates
in the muscle and is then transported to the liver to be reconverted
into glucose.
It **yields less energy than aerobic respiration**, because NADH (each NADH will
produce 2.5 ATPs) and 2 pyruvate molecules (which at the end of the cycle would
have also produced ATP) are lost, **but we can restore NAD+.**

**Other sugars that can undergo glycolysis**

   - **Mannose:** it’s phosphorylated and then

there is an isomerization. It’s converted

into fructose-6-phosphate which enters
the glycolytic pathway.

   - **Fructose:** it’s phosphorylated to
fructose-6-phosphate by an _hexokinase_,
and then enters the glycolytic pathway.

In the liver it’s metabolized in a different

way: the liver doesn't have _hexokinase_,
but _fructokinase_, which phosphorylates
C1. When, further on, the _aldolase_ breaks
the molecule into 2, it results in dihydroxyacetone-phosphate and glyceraldehyde,
which is then phosphorylated by a _triose kinase_ and converted to G3P.

*[PDF p. 98]*

- **Galactose:** mainly obtained from the breakdown of lactose. **Galactosemias** are
complicated human diseases that are based on galactose accumulation due to
galactose metabolism impairment. They come from the lack of enzymes such as
_galactokinase, UDP-glucose epimerase_,
_UDP-glucose-galactose-1-phosphate-uridyltransferase,...._
It’s mainly carried out in the liver. Galactose is phosphorylated into
**galactose-1-phosphate,** which then reacts with a molecule called **UDP-glucose**
(catalyzed by _UDP-glucose-galactose-1-phosphate-uridyltransferase_, which

catalyzes the attack of the phosphate to the
anhydride bonds) and binds to UDP (uracil
nucleoside diphosphate). The end products
are glucose-1-phosphate and UDP-galactose:
there is a sugar exchange/transfer. The **G1P**
can be **further oxidized**, but the

**UDP-galactose** is **epimerized** into glucose,
**into UDP-glucose.** Now this UDP-glucose
can further participate in the reaction that
converts galactose into glucose-1-phosphate.
If galactose accumulates in the liver, it affects
its function and also has repercussions on the
brain. If this happens, then the patient suffers
**galactosemia** . Clinical symptoms: mental
retardation, liver failure,... Metabolic tests are
usually performed on babies to detect
metabolic illnesses such as galactosemia.

**Glycolysis and tumors**

**Glycolysis occurs at elevated rates in tumors.** Initially, tumors are **not vascularized**
(haven’t undergone angiogenesis), so they have to grow in **anaerobic conditions.** They are
characterized by an extreme and big growth, so they need a lot of energy, and one of the
quickest ways to do this is **fermentation** . It gives out less energy but by increasing the rate
of fermentation it can be compensated. Knowing that they are highly dependent on
glycolysis, we can synthesize drugs that interfere with glycolysis in tumors to treat them.
The more glycolytic enzymes, the more opportunities to obtain energy from these pathways:
hypoxia triggers the expression of **HIF-1** (inducible hypoxia factor) which increases the
transcription of **glucose transporters** (GLUT1 and GLUT3) that allow it to take up more
glucose, and of many enzymes such as _**hexokinases**_ (tumor has more ability to carry out
glycolysis).
**Cancer patients usually present hypoglycemia** due to all of this, even before being
diagnosed with cancer.
**CT scans and PET scans** allow to differentiate a normal glucose intake from an abnormal
one. Glucose is marked and given to the patient, and then its uptake is observed on the

scan.

*[PDF p. 99]*

From the Molecule to the Cell I Alberto Pérez-Mediavilla

### **The Citric Acid Cycle (Krebs Cycle)**

- The oxidation of glucose to pyruvate is a necessary step on the way to energy
production, but not a sufficient one

- If you remember that process (glycolysis) you realize that the amount of ATP
generated in it is 2 molecules.

- However both, the pyruvate produced in the process and the NADH that is
generated, have a high potential to generate ATP through aerobic oxidative

processes.

- Now we will see how pyruvate, instead of being reduced to lactate
(fermentation), undergoes a total oxidation to CO 2

- This process, as a whole, leading to the complete oxidation of the organic
fuels (glucose, fatty acids, amino acids) is known as **cellular respiration**

- **Cellular respiration takes place in three stages** :

- **First stage** : carbon skeletons of organic fuels are
oxidized to a common compound **called acetyl-**
**coenzyme A.** Depending on the starting molecule,
the route used to convert the initial carbons into
acetate units is different. As we have already seen,
**in the case of glucose, we will reach pyruvate**
**through glycolysis**, and as we will see later,
**pyruvate will be converted into acetate through**
**an additional oxidative decarboxylation** reaction
(pyruvate dehydrogenase reaction)

- **Second stage** : in this stage, the acetate (acetylCoA) is incorporated into **the citric acid cycle** (or
Krebs cycle) where the two carbons are oxidized to
CO 2 . At this stage of cellular respiration, **most of**
**the energy released during oxidations is**
**temporarily conserved as NADH and FADH** **2** **.**

- **Third stage** : the reduced cofactors (NADH and
FADH 2 ) generated both in the first and in the
second phase, are oxidized and the released
electrons will end up reducing O 2, previously
passing through a chain of electron-transport
molecules. . In this process, **known as oxidative**
**phosphorylation,** a large amount of energy is
released that allows ATP to be synthesized

- As seen in the diagram, the Krebs Cycle is the central axis of cellular
metabolism

- It is the gateway to the aerobic metabolism for those molecules that will allow
to produce energy

- It is also a pathway that provides precursors required for biosynthesis of other
molecules, Aa, nucleotides, porphyrins, and even glucose (from oxaloacetate)

- Itself, the Krebs cycle does not generate ATP; its function is to generate
electrons in the form of NADH and FADH 2

1

*[PDF p. 100]*

From the Molecule to the Cell I Alberto Pérez-Mediavilla

- These reduced cofactors are those that in stage three of cellular respiration
drive the synthesis of ATP

**Production of Acetyl-coenzyme A**

- As we have said, before entering the citric acid cycle, sugars and fatty acids
must be converted to activated acetate units (acetyl-CoA.)

- **Fatty acids**, as we will see in another moment, have their own oxidation
pathway to acetyl-CoA which is called **β-oxidation**

- Glucose, on the other hand, is initially oxidized to pyruvate (glycolysis) so that
it is later oxidized and decarboxylated to acetate.

- The reaction is catalyzed by a multi-enzyme complex called pyruvate
dehydrogenase (PDH) that is located in the mitochondria.

- The global reaction is an oxidative decarboxylation in which pyruvate loses its
carboxyl group as CO2 and the two remaining carbons are transformed into an
acetyl group attached to Coenzyme A (acetyl-CoA)

- In the total computation of the process, a pyruvate molecule is decarboxylated
and oxidized to acetate bound to CoA and a NAD + molecule is reduced to

NADH

- Therefore, the energy that is released during the decarboxylation and
oxidation of pyruvate is transformed into a thioester bond with CoA and into a
reduced electronic transporter.

- Later we will see how the subsequent oxidation of NADH is able to boost the
synthesis of 2.5 ATP

**The Pyruvate dehydrogenase complex (PDH)**

- Under the name pyruvate dehydrogenase,
three different enzymes and five
coenzymes are agglutinated **: thiamine**
**pyrophosphate (TPP), FAD, coenzyme**
**A, lipoic acid and NAD** **[+]**

- It is made up of many copies of three

enzymes:
**E1: pyruvate dehydrogenase**
**E2: dihydrolipoyl transacetylase**
**E3: dihydrolipoyl dehydrogenase**

- **TPP** : is the coenzymatic form of vit B1. It
participates in decarboxylation reactions. Its
**deficit causes** a disease known as **Beri-**

**Beri** that manifests itself with nervous and

cardiac disorders. The absence of TPP
prevents glucose from being oxidized, which has serious consequences on the
nervous system
**Exacerbated thiamine deficiency** causes **Wernicke-Korsakoff syndrome**,
characterized by memory loss, confusion, and paralysis. It is common among
alcoholics due to poor intestinal absorption of thiamine

2

*[PDF p. 101]*

From the Molecule to the Cell I Alberto Pérez-Mediavilla

- **FAD** (flavin and adenine dinucleotide) is an electronic
transporter formed by the union of two nucleotides, one with
adenine and the other with riboflavin. Riboflavin is vitamin B2

- **NAD** **[+]** (nicotinamide and adenine dinucleotide) is an electronic
carrier formed by the union of two nucleotides, one with nicotinamide
(Vitamin B3) and the other with adenine. **Vitamin B3 deficiency**
**causes Pellagra** characterized by rough, reddish skin (dermatitis),
fatigue and encephalopathies

- **Lipoic acid**, derived from octadecanoic acid. It is characterized by
two sulfhydryl groups that serve to bind the acetate derived
from the decarboxylation of pyruvate and to transport electrons.

- The toxicity of As (arsenic) is due to the fact that it binds to the
SH groups of lipoic acid, blocking PDH and other enzymes that
contain this vitamin such as α-ketoglutarate dehydrogenase

- **Coenzyme A** is a cofactor acting as a transporter and activator of the acetate
derived from the decarboxylation of pyruvate. It is made up of three elements:
**β-mercaptoethylamine, pantothenic acid and 3'-phosphoadenosine 5'-**
**diphosphate**

3

*[PDF p. 102]*

From the Molecule to the Cell I Alberto Pérez-Mediavilla

- The reaction occurs in five stages such that the intermediate substrates never
leave contact with the PDH complex, this process is **called substrate**
**channeling**

   - **PDH regulation** : there are two regulation mechanisms

         - **allosteric inhibition** by NADH and acetyl-CoA. They both
compete for binding sites on their enzymes and keep E2 in
acetylated form, unable to accept the hydroxyethyl group of TPP.

**- covalent modification** by phosphorylation and
dephosphorylation: dihydrolipoyl transacetylase has two enzymes
linked:

               - **PDH kinase** : inactivates PDH by phosphorylation in Ser;
this kinase activity is stimulated by NADH and acetyl-CoA

             **PDH phosphatase** : reverses inhibition by
dephosphorylation

*[PDF p. 103]*

From the Molecule to the Cell I Alberto Pérez-Mediavilla

**Reactions of the Citric Acid Cycle**

  - The first difference that
can be observed, after
studying glycolysis, is that
the latter does not
regenerate glucose, is a
linear sequence of
reactions; on the other
hand, Krebs cycle is a
cyclic sequence of
reactions: the first
compound of the cycle,
oxaloacetate, is
regenerated at the end of
the cycle

  - At each lap of the cycle
there is an **entry of one**
**Ac-CoA and the release**
**of 2 CO** **2**

   - **Each turn uses an OA to condense with the Ac-CoA** to form citrate.

  - In theory there is no loss of OA, however, some Krebs intermediates serve
as biosynthetic precursors, so the OA could be depleted preventing the entry
of new Ac-CoA molecules.

  - To avoid the loss of OA, cells have some reactions, called **anaplerotic**, to
allow them to synthesize OA and prevent the cycle from stopping

  - We can say that Krebs cycle is not only a catabolic pathway but it is also
an anabolic pathway. These types of pathways are called **amphibolic** .

  - All reactions take place in the mitochondria; In addition, the enzymes
necessary for the final transfer of electrons to O2 coupled with the synthesis
of ATP (oxidative phosphorylation) are also in the mitochondria.

5

*[PDF p. 104]*

From the Molecule to the Cell I Alberto Pérez-Mediavilla

**The eight steps of Krebs cycle**

**1. Formation of citrate**

   - The first reaction is the condensation of

Ac-CoA with OA to form citrate

   - It is catalyzed by citrate synthase. The
methyl carbon of acetyl binds to the
carbonyl of OA

   - During the reaction, an intermediate
compound, citroyl-CoA, bound to the
active site of the enzyme, is generated.
Hydrolysis of the thioester makes the
reaction highly exergonic.

   - In this reaction the CoA is recycled

**2. Formation of isocitrate**

   - Citrate is transformed to its isocitrate

isomer

   - Is a reversible isomerization reaction
catalyzed by aconitase (aconitate
hydratase)

   - The reaction consists in the addition

and elimination of a molecule of H2O

   - Aconitase has an Fe-S center that

serves to fix the substrate and to add

or eliminate water.

   - Although citrate is a symmetric molecule, aconitase acts only on the two
carbons that come from OA.

   - It is inhibited by ATP and NADH so that after a copious meal, there is an
accumulation of citrate that will leave the mitochondria and will be used
to synthesize fatty acids and therefore fats in the form of TAG

   - Fluoroacetate is an aconitase inhibitor; it becomes fluoroacetyl-CoA
which inhibits it.

**3. Oxidation to isocitrate to α-ketoglutarate and CO** **2** **.**

   - Isocitrate dehydrogenase catalyzes the oxidative decarboxylation of
isocitrate giving rise to α-ketoglutarate and CO 2

   

   - There are **two isozymes of isocitrate dehydrogenase** : one of them is
mitochondrial and uses NAD [+] as an oxidation-reduction cofactor, the
other is both mitochondrial and cytosolic and uses NADP [+] as a cofactor.

6

*[PDF p. 105]*

From the Molecule to the Cell I Alberto Pérez-Mediavilla

   - The NADP [+] -dependent isozymes main mission is to generate NADPH
necessary for biosynthetic reactions

**4. Oxidation of α-ketoglutarate to succinyl-CoA and CO** **2** **.**

   - Once again there is an
oxidative decarboxylation
catalyzed by the
multienzymatic complex αketoglutarate dehydrogenase

   - The final electron acceptor is
NAD [+]

   - **In this reaction, as occurred in PDH, the released energy is**
**conserved as NADH and as a thioester bond in succinyl-CoA**

   - Equally to the pyruvate dehydrogenase, **α-ketoglutarate**
**dehydrogenase complex is made up of 3 enzymes and 5 coenzymes**

   - The enzymes are:

`o` E1: **α-ketoglutarate dehydrogenase**
`o` E2: **dihydrolipoyl transuccinylase**
`o` E3: **dihydrolipoyl dehydrogenase**

   - The coenzymes are: **TPP, lipoic acid, CoA, FAD and NAD** **[+]**

   - The reaction is identical to that of PDH

**5. Conversión del succinil-CoA en succinato**

   - In the next reaction, the hydrolysis of
succinyl-CoA is coupled to the
phosphorylation of GDP.

   -   - **The energy released at the**
**hydrolysis of the thioester is used**
**to promote the synthesis of a**
**phosphoanhydride bond (GDP+Pi)**
**and the formation of succinate and GTP**

   - The enzyme that catalyzes this reaction is **succinyl-CoA synthetase**

   - The reaction takes place in 3 stages:

1) Succinyl-CoA reacts with the Pi and forms succinyl-P and
releasing CoA
2) the P group of succinyl P is transferred to an His residue of the
enzyme, releasing succinate
3) the P group of His is transferred to GDP, forming GTP

   - This type of mechanism in which ATP (or GTP) is synthesized is known
as **substrate-level phosphorylations**

   - The GTP produced in this reaction is easily converted to ATP through the
action of nucleoside diphosphate kinase

**GTP + ADP ↔ GDP + ATP**

7

*[PDF p. 106]*

From the Molecule to the Cell I Alberto Pérez-Mediavilla

**6. Oxiation of succinate to fumarate**

   - The succinate formed from succinylCoA is oxidized fo fumarate by the
**succinate dehydrogenase**

   - This enzyme is a **flavoprotein**
**(because contains FAD as**
**cofactor); it is strongly attached to**
**the inner mitochondrial membrane** .

   - **The electrons** released during the oxidation of succinate **reduce FAD to**
**FADH** **2** and subsequently **pass to the mitochondrial electron**
**transport chain where they will drive the synthesis**
**of 1.5 ATP** molecules

   - This reaction **is inhibited by malonate**, a structural
analog of succinate. The addition of malonate to the
mitochondria **blocks the Krebs cycle.**

**7. Hydration of fumarate a malate**

   - The **reversible hydaration of fumarate to**
**L-malate is catalyzed by fumarase.**

   - **Fumarase is an stereospecific enzyme**
and only can use the trans isomer of malate

**8. Oxidation of malate to oxaloacetate**

   - The last reaction of Krebs cycle consists on
the **oxidation of malate** to oxaloacetate,
catalyzed by **malate dehydrogenase** .

   - Although the equilibrium of this reaction
lies very far to the left, under physiological
conditions, the continuous consumption of
OA by citrate synthase makes the OA
concentration low, so the reaction is
shifted to the right..

8

*[PDF p. 107]*

From the Molecule to the Cell I Alberto Pérez-Mediavilla

**Energy production**

   - Krebs cycle reactions lead to:

1) **oxidation of an acetate**
**group** (acetyl-coA) to **2CO** **2**
2) **3 NAD** **[+]** are reduced to
**3NADH**
3) **1 FAD** is reduced to
**1FADH** **2**
4) **1 ADP** is phosphorylated
to **1ATP** (although in the
cycle it is GTP, but we can
assimilate it to ATP)

   - Each molecule of NADH will lead the production of 2.5 ATP (oxidative
phosphorylation)

   - The molecule of FADH 2 will lead 1.5 ATP (oxidative phosphorylation)

   - Therefore, each turn of the Krebs cycle generates 10 ATP

   - If we consider the energy generated in the complete oxidation of a
glucose molecule as a whole the balance will be:

Glucose →2 pyruvate + 2 ATP +2NADH (glycolisis)
2 pyruvate → 2 Ac-CoA + 2 CO 2 +2NADH (Piruvate dehydrogenase
complex)
2 Ac-CoA → 4 CO 2 + 20 ATP (Krebs cycle)

   - Making the corresponding conversions of NADH and FADH 2 to ATP
(oxidative phosphorylation), we conclude that up to **32 ATP are obtained**

**Regulation of the Krebs cycle**

  - The flow of carbon atoms from pyruvate to the Krebs cycle is subject to
regulation at **two levels** :

**- the conversion of pyruvate to acetyl-**
**CoA**

**- the entry of acetyl-CoA into the cycle**

  - In other words, the enzymes that are subject
to regulation are **fundamentally pyruvate**
**dehydrogenase and citrate synthase.**

  - It is also regulated at the level of isocitrate
dehydrogenase and α-ketoglutarate
dehydrogenase.

  - We have previously seen the regulation of
pyruvate dehydrogenase

  - Regarding the reactions of Krebs cycle, there
are three factors that govern the flow rate
through the cycle:

     - **the availability of substrates**

     - **the inhibition by accumulated products**

9

*[PDF p. 108]*

From the Molecule to the Cell I Alberto Pérez-Mediavilla

     - **the allosteric back-inhibition** of the enzymes that catalyze early
stages of the cycle.

    - In general, the overall activity of the cycle depends on the availability of
NAD [+] for the oxidative reactions.

    - Actually the availability of NAD [+] depends on the consumption of NADH
by the respiratory chain, which in turn, depends on the rate of consumption
of ATP by cells

     - **The accumulation of NADH**, which occurs when ATP consumption is
low, **inhibits citrate synthase, α-ketoglutarate dehydrogenase** . Under
these circumstances, the malate dehydrogenase reaction is also reversed,
which causes a decrease in the
availability of oxaloacetate,
thereby also slowing down the
citrate synthase reaction.

     - **The accumulation of**
**products** (citrate, succinyl CoA
and ATP) inhibits the three
limiting stages of the pathway:
**citrate synthase, isocitrate**
**dehydrogenase and α-**
**ketoglutarate**
**dehydrogenase)**

     - **In muscle, Ca** **[++]** **,** which acts as a signal for contraction, **activates**
**isocitrate dehydrogenase and α-ketoglutarate dehydrogenase, and, as**
**we have seen previously, pyruvate dehydrogenase.**

    - Citrate acts as an accommodator of the flow of metabolites between
glycolysis and CAC since it inhibits phosphofructokinase I

**Anaplerotic reaction to replenish Krebs cycle intermediates**

   - As we have already said, CAC provides precursors for many biosynthetic
pathways.

   - α-ketoglutarate and OA can serve as precursors of Asp and Glu, and
through these compounds as nucleotide precursors.

   - OA is a glucose precursor

   - Succinyl-CoA is an intermediate in the synthesis of porphyrins

   
   - To avoid a decrease in the concentration of OA as a consequence of the
exit of intermediates from the cycle, there are some reactions that allow
synthesizing OA or other intermediates; these reactions are called
anaplerotic

   - The most important anaplerotic reactions, in liver and kidney, is the
reversible carboxylation of pyruvate to form OA, catalyzed by pyruvate
carboxylase (liver and kidney)

**Pyruvate + HCO** **3**

**-** **+ ATP ↔ OA + ADP + Pi**

- This reaction consumes energy and requires the presence of biotin as a
cofactor.

- Biotin is covalently linked to pyruvate carboxylase.

10

*[PDF p. 109]*

From the Molecule to the Cell I Alberto Pérez-Mediavilla

   - Another element necessary in the reaction **is acetyl-CoA, which acts as**
**an allosteric activator.**

   - The accumulation of acetyl-CoA is fundamentally the consequence of a
decrease in the concentration of OA.

   - **In heart and skeletal muscle** there is another reaction in which
Phosphoenolpyruvate (PEP) is converted into OA by phosphoenol
pyruvate carboxykinase

**PEP + CO** **2** **+ GDP ↔ OA + GTP**

- There is a third reaction consisting of the carboxylation of pyruvate to malate,
catalyzed by the malic enzyme

**Pyruvate + HCO** **3**

**-** **+ NAD(P)H ↔ Malate + NAD(P)** **+**

11

*[PDF p. 110]*

**Lecture 5.6. Gluconeogenesis and glycogen metabolism**

**GLYCOGEN**

**Glycogen** is the **storage** form of glucose in **animals** . Glycogen is
composed of glucose units linked by **α1→4 glycosidic bonds, with**
**α1→6 branches** . It’s present in the cytosol in the form of granules. The
process of glycogen synthesis is especially important in the liver and
skeletal muscle. Glycogen is used **in the liver** as g **lucose stores**
**convertible to blood glucose,** whereas **in the muscle** it is used as a
**source** of glucose for **energy** production.
As with most of the biosynthesis and degradation pathways, glycogen
synthesis and glycogen degradation follow distinct metabolic pathways,
which allows independent control of each route.

**Glycogen breakdown / Glycogenolysis**

In the first step, **glucose** residues are **removed at the**
**non-reducing ends** of the glycogen molecule in a process
known as **phosphorolysis** . Glucose molecules are released
as glucose 1-phosphate. This reaction is catalyzed by
_**glycogen phosphorylase.**_
_(This enzyme needs pyridoxal phosphate “PLP” (which comes_
_from pyridoxine or vitamin B6) as a cofactor. Glycogen_
_debranching enzyme catalyzes the hydrolysis of α1→6_
_bonds.)_
The fact that this reaction is a phosphorolysis is beneficial,
since it is not necessary to phosphorylate the released glucose molecule (if it were a
hydrolysis, glucose would then need to be phosphorylated, spending one ATP molecule).

Afterwards, **glucose 1-phosphate** is **isomerized** to **glucose 6-phosphate** in a reaction
catalyzed by _phosphoglucomutase_ .

**- In muscle cells, glucose 6-phosphate is degraded to obtain energy.**

**- In liver cells, glucose 6-phosphate enters the endoplasmic reticulum** where _glucose_

_6-phosphatase_ catalyzes its **hydrolysis to**
**glucose.** Glucose is then transferred to the cytosol
through a specific membrane transporter, and
**leaves the hepatocyte** via the **GLUT2**
**transporter.** Free glucose can go through the
blood to other organs, such as the brain or muscle.
Therefore, the **liver** is **responsible for**
**maintaining a constant level of glucose in the**

**blood.**

*[PDF p. 111]*

**Glycogen synthesis**

The synthesis of glycogen is catalyzed by _**glycogen synthase,**_ which **requires** the use of
the nucleotide sugar **UDP-glucose** : the enzyme catalyzes the transfer of the glucose
residue from UDP-glucose to the non-reducing end of the glycogen molecule through the
**formation of** a new **α1→4 glycosidic bond.** The enzyme **requires as a primer** a **chain of**

**at least eight glucose residues** which are generated
by the catalytic activity of another enzyme
known as _**glycogenin**_, which **acts as an**
**initiator in the synthesis of glycogen.**
_Glycogen branching enzyme_ catalyzes the
introduction of branches in glycogen.
The **energy** for the formation of the α1→4
glycosidic bond is provided by the
**hydrolysis** of the **O-glycosidic bond** in the
nucleotide sugar.

**Coordinated regulation of glycogen synthesis and degradation**

Glycogen **breakdown and synthesis** are **reciprocally**
**regulated.** This prevents glycogen from being synthesized at
the same time that it is being broken down.
It’s coordinated through the regulation of the enzyme
activities of _glycogen phosphorylase_ and _glycogen synthase._
Both enzymes are dimers that can be phosphorylated at
specific tyrosine residues. _**Glycogen phosphorylase**_ is
**activated by phosphorylation,** whereas _**glycogen**_
_**synthase**_ is **inhibited by phosphorylation.** In that way, if
the cell regulates the phosphorylation of both enzymes using the same mechanisms, when
glycogen breakdown is activated, glycogen synthesis is inhibited, and viceversa.

**Hormonal regulation of glycogen metabolism**

The main hormones involved in the regulation of glycogen metabolism are **insulin,**
**epinephrine and glucagon.** These hormones are released mostly in response to circulating
glucose levels and **regulate** the **activity of** _**glycogen phosphorylase**_ **and** _**glycogen**_
_**synthase**_ . The body maintains fasting blood glucose levels at approximately 80 mg/dL.

                        - **Insulin** is a peptide hormone **produced by β cells in**
**the Langerhans islets** of the **pancreas** . Insulin is released in
response to **high levels of glucose in the blood.** The role of
this hormone is to reduce glucose levels. Insulin **induces** the
**dephosphorylation** of _glycogen phosphorylase_ and _glycogen_
_synthase_ . Therefore, insulin **inhibits glycogen breakdown**
and **stimulates glycogen synthesis.**

*[PDF p. 112]*

- **Epinephrine (adrenaline)** is released in response to **stress** or **low levels**
**of glucose in the blood.** The role of the hormone is to prepare the body
for a **fight-or-flight response** to a perceived harmful event. A part of this
preparation is to **increase blood glucose** levels to **make it available for**
**muscle cells.** Epinephrine induces the phosphorylation of _glycogen_
_phosphorylase_ and _glycogen synthase._ Therefore, epinephrine
**stimulates glycogen breakdown** and **inhibits glycogen synthesis.**

   **Glucagon** is a peptide hormone released by **α cells in the Langerhans**
**islets of the pancreas.** Glucagon has the opposite effects of insulin. It is secreted in
response to low levels of glucose in the blood. Glucagon **induces** the
**phosphorylation of** _**glycogen phosphorylase**_ **and** _**glycogen synthase.**_ Therefore,
glucagon **stimulates glycogen breakdown** and **inhibits glycogen synthesis.**

While both epinephrine and glucagon have similar effects, **epinephrine acts on skeletal**
**muscle and the liver,** while **glucagon acts only on the liver. Insulin acts on both**

**tissues.**

**Glycogen storage diseases**

A number of genetic diseases are known in which genes encoding enzymes involved in
glycogen metabolism are mutated. These diseases are known as **glycogen storage**
**diseases** or **glycogenosis** . Clinical symptoms range from relatively mild to fatal. Most of
these diseases **affect the storage of glycogen** in organs where glycogen accumulates:
**liver, muscle** and, to a lesser extent, **kidney** .

   One of the first glycogen storage diseases to be described
was **von Gierke’s disease (type Ia).** This disease is
associated with mutations in _glucose 6-phosphatase_ and it
is one of the most serious, since the **liver cannot release**

**glucose** into the blood.
   - **Glycogen storage disease type 0** is associated with
mutations in _glycogen synthase,_ which **impairs liver**
**glycogen synthesis.**

In these two diseases, patients are prone to low blood glucose (hypoglycemia).

   - In **Pompe disease (glycogen storage disease type II)**
there is an **accumulation of glycogen in the lysosomes**
due to defects in _lysosomal α1→4 glucosidase,_ affecting
multiple tissues, but mainly skeletal and cardiac muscle.
   - In **Ardle’s disease (glycogen storage disease type V)**
there is a deficiency of _muscle phosphorylase._ **Cramps**
and **muscle weakness** occur mostly after exercise, but

the liver is not affected.

   - Finally, **Lafora disease,** a **progressive epilepsy,** is caused by
mutations in genes that regulate the production of glycogen. The
disease is characterized by the **formation of Lafora bodies in**
**neurons and other tissues** (Lafora bodies contain large
amounts of poorly branched glycogen).

*[PDF p. 113]*

**GLUCONEOGENESIS**

Gluconeogenesis is the **synthesis of glucose from noncarbohydrate precursors**
**(lactate, glycerol, amino acids,..)**

**Gluconeogenesis** is an essential process needed to maintain the necessary amounts of
glucose in our body. Many tissues (such as the brain, adrenal medulla, erythrocytes, testes,
or embryonic tissues) require glucose as the sole source of fuel, and most tissues also
require glucose for other functions, such as synthesis of nucleotides or the carbohydrate
portion of glycoproteins and glycolipids.
After a **meal containing carbohydrates, blood glucose levels rise.** Some of the glucose
from the diet is stored in the liver as glycogen. After 2 or 3 hours of fasting, glycogen begins
to be degraded by the process of glycogenolysis, and glucose is released into the blood. As
glycogen stores decrease, adipose triacylglycerols are also degraded, providing fatty acids
as an alternative fuel and glycerol for the synthesis of glucose by gluconeogenesis. Amino
acids are also released from the muscle to serve as gluconeogenic precursors.
After approximately 30 hours of fasting, liver glycogen stores are mostly depleted and
gluconeogenesis becomes the only source of blood glucose.

In mammals, the **main organ where gluconeogenesis takes place is the liver** (90% of its
total production). It **also** occurs in the **renal cortex,** in the **cells of the renal tubule**, and to
a lesser extent in the **intestinal epithelium.** Gluconeogenesis in the liver is responsible for
maintaining blood glucose levels, so the brain and muscle are able to obtain enough glucose

to meet their metabolic demands.

Gluconeogenesis **shares with glycolysis** the **initial and final components: glucose and**
**pyruvate.** However, **gluconeogenesis** is **not the reverse pathway to glycolysis.** The
reason is that the formation of pyruvate from glucose is a highly exergonic process.
Therefore, the reverse process is not possible and a different **thermodynamically**
**favorable route is required.** Seven of the ten reactions are the same in glycolysis and
gluconeogenesis, whereas **three steps are different.**

The **differential steps between glycolysis and gluconeogenesis** are:

   Synthesis of phosphoenolpyruvate from pyruvate

   Formation of fructose 6-phosphate from fructose 1,6-bisphosphate

   Conversion of glucose 6-phosphate into glucose

*[PDF p. 114]*

**Gluconeogenesis pathway**

**-**
**Synthesis of phosphoenolpyruvate from pyruvate:**

It takes place through reactions in the mitochondria
and the cytosol and requires two energy-consuming
steps.

**Pyruvate** can come from the **deamination of**

**alanine or lactate.**

                     - Alanine transamination takes place in the

mitochondria.

                     - The conversion of lactate to pyruvate occurs in the
cytosol. In this case, the pyruvate must be
transported into the mitochondria.

**In the mitochondria, pyruvate is transformed**

**into oxaloacetate:**

_**Pyruvate carboxylase**_, the enzyme that catalyzes
the process, **contains biotin** (vitamin H or B7), a
prosthetic group covalently attached to the enzyme.
This coenzyme is key in many carboxylation
reactions. _Pyruvate carboxylase_ is an **allosteric**
**enzyme** that **acts as the first regulator** of the
gluconeogenic pathway.

**Acetyl-CoA** is an **allosteric activator of** _**pyruvate carboxylase,**_ since high levels of
acetyl-CoA indicates the need for oxaloacetate. If there is enough ATP in the cell,
oxaloacetate will be consumed in gluconeogenesis. If there is not enough ATP, oxaloacetate
will be used for the entry of an acetyl-CoA into the Krebs cycle ( **anaplerotic role of**
_**pyruvate carboxylase**_ **).**

If gluconeogenesis continues, **oxaloacetate**
**must be transported to the cytosol,** where
the rest of the reactions will take place.
Oxaloacetate cannot pass the inner

mitochondrial membrane because it does

not have a specific transporter for it.
Therefore, oxaloacetate is transformed into
malate through the action of _mitochondrial_
_malate dehydrogenase._
**Malate leaves the mitochondria** using the malate/α-ketoglutarate transporter.
In the cytosol, **malate** is **reoxidized to oxaloacetate** in a reaction catalyzed by _cytosolic_
_malate dehydrogenase._
_Cytosolic malate dehydrogenase_ and _mitochondrial malate dehydrogenases_ are examples of
isozymes. They are different enzymes that catalyze the same chemical reaction.
Finally, **oxaloacetate** is **decarboxylated and phosphorylated** to produce
**phosphoenolpyruvate** in a reaction catalyzed by _**phosphoenolpyruvate carboxykinase.**_
The CO2 incorporated into pyruvate by _pyruvate carboxylase_ is the same that is now lost.

*[PDF p. 115]*

This carboxylation-decarboxylation sequence represents a way to “activate” pyruvate so that
decarboxylation of oxaloacetate allows the synthesis of phosphoenolpyruvate.

In summary, the steps by which pyruvate is converted into phosphoenolpyruvate are:

1. Transport of pyruvate from the cytosol to the mitochondria

2. Conversion of pyruvate to oxaloacetate

3. Reduction of oxaloacetate to malate

4. Transport of malate from the mitochondria to the cytosol

5. Oxidation of malate to oxaloacetate

6. Conversion of oxaloacetate to phosphoenolpyruvate

The overall **balance** of the conversion of **pyruvate to phosphoenolpyruvate** is:

Pyruvate + ATP + GTP + H2O → phosphoenolpyruvate + ADP + GDP + Pi + 2H+
The **consumption of two high-energy phosphate groups** allows the process to be
thermodynamically favorable. Another goal that is achieved in this process is the net
**transport of NADH from** the **mitochondria to** the **cytosol** . This is very important, as NADH
is required for gluconeogenesis in the step catalyzed by _glyceraldehyde 3-phosphate_
_dehydrogenase._

**-**
**Synthesis of fructose 1,6-bisphosphate from phosphoenolpyruvate:**

The next six reactions in which phosphoenolpyruvate is transformed into fructose
1,6-bisphosphate are the same reversible reactions that are used in glycolysis:

1. Phosphoenolpyruvate + H2O ⇌ 2-phosphoglycerate (catalyzed by _enolase_ )

2. 2-Phosphoglycerate ⇌ 3-phosphoglycerate (catalyzed by _phosphoglycerate mutase_ )

3. 3-Phosphoglycerate + ATP ⇌ 1,3-bisphosphoglycerate + ADP (catalyzed by
_phosphoglycerate kinase_ )

4. 1,3-Bisphosphoglycerate + NADH + H+ ⇌ glyceraldehyde 3-phosphate + NAD+ + Pi
(catalyzed by _GAPDH_ )

5. Glyceraldehyde 3-phosphate ⇌ dihydroxyacetone phosphate (catalyzed by _triose_
_phosphate isomerase)_

6. Glyceraldehyde 3-phosphate + dihydroxyacetone ⇌ fructose 1,6-bisphosphate
(catalyzed by _aldolase_ )

_(Note that all the reactions in gluconeogenesis are to be performed twice up to the reaction_
_catalyzed by GAPDH because two three-carbon precursors are required to make a molecule_
_of glucose)_

*[PDF p. 116]*

**-**
**Synthesis of fructose 6-phosphate from fructose 1,6-bisphosphate**

This reaction is the **second by-pass** of the **irreversible reaction** catalyzed in glycolysis. In
gluconeogenesis, _fructose 1,6-bisphosphatase-1 (FBPase1)_ catalyzes the hydrolysis of
fructose 1,6-bisphosphate, where inorganic phosphate is released.

**-**
**Synthesis of glucose 6-phosphate from fructose 6-phosphate**

Fructose 6-phosphate ⇌ glucose 6-phosphate

This isomerization reaction, catalyzed by _phosphohexose isomerase,_ is **reversible**, and,
therefore, it is **shared with glycolysis.**

**-**
**Synthesis of free glucose from glucose 6-phosphate**

In the **third by-pass step**, Pi is hydrolyzed from glucose 6-phosphate in a reaction catalyzed
by _glucose 6-phosphatase._ This enzyme is found on the inside of the endoplasmic reticulum
membrane of hepatocytes, but it is not found in the brain or muscle. As a consequence,
**glucose can be dephosphorylated and released to the bloodstream only by**
**hepatocytes.**
**Glucose 6-phosphate** enters the **endoplasmic reticulum** through a specific translocase
and its hydrolysis gives rise to free glucose, which is then transported to the cytosol through
another specific membrane transporter, and **leaves the hepatocyte via the GLUT2**
**transporter** . Free glucose can go through the blood to other organs, such as the brain or

muscle.

**Overall balance of gluconeogenesis**

2 Pyruvate + 4 ATP + 2 GTP + 2 NADH + 2 H+ + 4 H2O → glucose + 4 ADP + 2 GDP + 6 Pi

+ 2 NAD+

**Six high-energy phosphate bonds** are used in the synthesis of glucose from pyruvate.
This allows gluconeogenesis to be a thermodynamically favorable pathway.

**Gluconeogenic precursors**

The three major **carbon sources** for hepatic gluconeogenesis are **lactate, glycerol and**
**amino acids.** Kidneys predominantly use amino acids. Animals cannot produce glucose
from fatty acids.

**- Lactate** is produced from pyruvate **by erythrocytes and anaerobic exercising muscles.**
- **Glycerol** is released from **adipose stores** of triacylglycerols.
- **Amino acids** come mainly from **muscle**, where they are obtained by protein degradation.
_All amino acids (except for leucine and lysine) are glucogenic because they can be_
_converted to pyruvate or citric acid cycle intermediates. Alanine is the most abundant amino_
_acid that arrives to the liver, since it is produced in the muscle from other amino acids._

*[PDF p. 117]*

**Regulation of gluconeogenesis**

**Two steps** are the main regulatory points in gluconeogenesis:

**-**
**Pyruvate to phosphoenolpyruvate (oxaloacetate)**

**-**
**Fructose 1,6-bisphosphate to fructose 6-phosphate**

**1.** **Pyruvate to oxaloacetate/phosphoenolpyruvate**
Pyruvate is converted to phosphoenolpyruvate when _**pyruvate carboxylase**_ is
**allosterically activated by high levels of acetyl-CoA** (produced, for example, by oxidation
of fatty acids). At the same time, **ADP**, a surrogate of low cell energy, acts as a **negative**
**regulator of** _**phosphoenolpyruvate carboxykinase.**_ Therefore, in a situation in which there
is enough energy (high levels of acetyl-CoA and low levels of ADP) the conversion of
pyruvate to oxaloacetate is induced.

At the same time, _**pyruvate kinase**_, the corresponding glycolytic enzyme, is **inhibited by**
**acetyl-CoA** and by its phosphorylation induced by glucagon. The activity of _**pyruvate**_
_**dehydrogenase**_, the enzyme responsible for the conversion of pyruvate into acetyl-CoA is
also strongly **inhibited by acetyl-CoA.**

In summary, **high levels of acetyl-CoA inhibit glycolysis** **and promote gluconeogenesis.**

**2.** **Fructose 1,6-bisphosphate to fructose 6-phosphate**
A futile cycle in which both _**phosphofructokinase-1 (PFK1)**_ and _**fructose**_
_**1,6-bisphosphatase (FBPase1)**_ act simultaneously is prevented by **allosteric regulators**
with opposite effects in these two enzymes. The key regulator of both processes is **fructose**
**2,6-bisphosphate.** This regulator strongly **induces PFK-1** and **inhibits FBPase1.** The
hormone glucagon induces the degradation of fructose 2,6-bisphosphate. Therefore,
glucagon induces gluconeogenesis and inhibits glycolysis. Another allosteric regulator at this
level is **AMP**, which **activates PFK-1** and **inhibits FBPase1**, blocking gluconeogenesis
when energy levels are low.

*[PDF p. 118]*

**Lecture 5.7. Oxidative phosphorylation and ATP synthesis**

**Oxidative phosphorylation** is the process that
allows the cells to **obtain ATP** by phosphorylating
ADP through the use of the energy released by the
**transport of electrons.** During the traffic of
electrons through the respiratory chain, the electron
gradient potential will be turned into a **proton**
**gradient** . The energy coming from those
oxido-reductions will be used to pump protons from
the matrix into the intermembrane space. Then the
**ATP synthase** will use this gradient to produce

ATP.

The **inner mitochondrial membrane** is **highly impermeable for H+** . The protons can’t go
into the intermembrane space if not through specific channels.

**Structure of the mitochondria**

   Outer membrane: permeable to small molecules and ions

   Mitochondrial matrix: contains all the necessary enzymes for Krebs cycle, PDH
complex, beta-oxidation and the oxidation of amino acids.

   Intermembrane space

   Inner mitochondrial membrane: contains the complex that forms electron-transport
chain and ATPsynthase. It’s highly impermeable, even to H+, which allows the
establishment of a proton gradient.

**Electron funnelling to the respiratory chain**

Carried out by electron carriers such as **NADH** and **FADH2**, which accept electrons from
_dehydrogenases_ and funnel them to the electron transport chain.

**Respiratory electron carriers**

The mitochondrial respiratory chain is made up of **electronic transporters that act**
**sequentially.** Most of them contain a series of **electron carrier prosthetic groups** that
allow the transport of e- (accept and give up electrons).
In addition to NADH and Flavoproteins there arre 3 types of electron transport molecules:

   - **Coenzyme Q or Ubiquinone:** This lipidic molecule can transport **2 electrons**
(partially reduced when 1 e- is added). It’s liposoluble, found inside the membrane
and hydrophobic: it can receive electrons (2) from one complex and transfer to

another one. It’s an **intermediate/connector.**

   - **Iron-sulfur clusters:** They are **one electron** carriers.

   **Cytochromes:** They are proteins with **heme prosthetic groups** . In mitochondria,
there are 3 types: **A, B and C**, and are strongly bound to proteins. **Cytochrome C** is

*[PDF p. 119]*

an exception, since it isn't located in a specific complex, but bound to a soluble
protein which allows it to transfer e- from one complex to another. It’s an
**intermediate/connector.** (2 connectors: ubiquinone and cytochrome C).

**How are these transporters arranged?**

Because we can isolate them, we know that there are 5 complexes at the inner membrane
that belong to the respiratory chain (complex I, II, III, IV and lastly the ATPsynthase).

**1.** **COMPLEX I**

It's the _**NADH ubiquinone oxidoreductase.**_ As cofactors for electronic transport, it contains
FMN and Fe-S centres. It **receives electrons from NADH** and transports a pair of e- to
**coenzyme Q,** in this way reducing it. During this transport and due to the energy release,
there is a **proton pump** : **4 H+** are **transferred** to the intermembrane space. It’s at the same
time an electron carrier and a proton pump. These two events go hand by hand.
In other words: it’s a **coupling centre.**
Subsequently, the **reduced ubiquinone transfers the electrons to complex III.**

**2.** **COMPLEX II**

It’s the _**succinate dehydrogenase system.**_ **Electrons from succinate** enter through
complex II. It’s the only membrane-bound enzyme of the Krebs cycle. **FADH2** transfers the
electrons through complex II and at the end **reduces coenzyme Q** . Complex II **couples the**
**oxidation of succinate with the reduction of ubiquinone/coenzyme Q**, and finally,
Coenzyme Q transfers the electrons to complex III. There is **no proton pump** . It’s not a
coupling centre.

Therefore, electrons stripped from organic molecules enter the respiratory chain through
Complex I and Complex II.

There are two **additional gates**, alternative electron
incorporations:

                           **Glycerol-3-phosphate dehydrogenase**
**(G3PDH)** shuttle, which oxidizes glycerol and
incorporates electrons by **reducing coenzyme Q.**

                           - The **electron transport flavoprotein (ETFP)**,

which receives the electrons from the FADH2 released

at the first oxidation of fatty acids ( **beta oxidation** )
which go and **reduce coenzyme Q.**

*[PDF p. 120]*

**3.** **COMPLEX III**

Also known as _**ubiquinone-cytochrome C oxidoreductase.**_ It’s a coupling gate. During the
pass of electrons from ubiquinone to cytochrome C, there is a **proton pump** . It generates
part of the gradient. These electrons **reduce cytochrome C,** and will then be tansfered to
complex IV.

**4.** **COMPLEX IV**

Complex IV or _**Cytochrome oxidase**_ transports electrons **from Cytochrome C to O2.**
Cytochrome C is oxidized, e- pass through complex IV and there is a **proton pump** (2 H+).

Oxidative phosphorylation is **mainly regulated by the availability of ADP** that can be
phosphorylated to ATP.

**Oxidative stress**

During electron transport, some electrons can **leak** from
the process and partially reduce oxygen in membrane
lipids, proteins,... generating **reactive oxygen species**
**(ROS).**
There are systems that protect against this.
The most important system is the one catalyzed by
_**glutathione reductase.**_ Glutathione contains SH groups
that **can reduce H2O2** (an oxidizing agent) **to water**, and
so glutathione molecules become oxidized by forming
**disulfide bridges** . In this way, **they avoid** the **oxidation**

**of other structures.**

This defense mechanism is **highly dependent on the**
**availability of NADPH** to reduce glutathione dimers
(oxidized in its dimeric form, reduced in the monomeric
form) so they can be reused in reducing oxidizing agents

such as H2O2.

During aging, there is more of this stress, because as we
get older we express less of these protective enzymes.
Antioxidants also help to avoid this oxidative stress.

**-**
**Defense mechanisms against ROS during hypoxia**

During **hypoxia** there is an imbalance between the entry of electrons to the respiratory chain
and the transfer of electrons to O2 (absent in these conditions). This leads to an **increase in**
**the formation of ROS.** To avoid this, in addition to the _**glutathione peroxidase**_ **system**,
cells have additional defense mechanisms, which act through an increased transcription of
the **hypoxia-inducible factor (HIF1)** : it activates the transcription of proteins such as
glucose transporters (increasing glucose entry), glycolytic enzymes (glycolysis ATP
production is increased) and lactate dehydrogenase (more pyruvate is converted to lactate
instead of Acetyl CoA), and activates _PDH kinase_ which inhibits the activity of the PDH
complex and subsequently, decreases the flow of electrons through the respiratory chain
and the possibility of ROS production.

*[PDF p. 121]*

**How is the proton gradient used to synthesize ATP?**

We need an **electron donor, ADP and phosphate**, and an **electron acceptor**, in this case
**Oxygen** .
_If we take isolated mitochondria and up ADP and phosphate only, nothing happens. Very_
_little ATP is produced. If we add succinate as a source of electrons, the mitochondria can_
_oxidize it to fumarase. In this case, we have oxygen consumption and ATP synthesis. If we_
_add an inhibitor like CN-, the process is stopped. There is a direct relationship between_
_electronic transport and ATP synthesis.If the proton gradient is stopped for example by_
_adding molecules that uptake H+ (DNP), we break the gradient. The oxygen is consumed_
_(accepts the electrons) but ATP synthesis is stopped._
There are some mitochondria, mainly in **brown fat**, that
contain **uncoupled mitochondria** . These mitochondria
are specialized in the use of energy to mainly **produce**
**heat**, not ATP. The difference is that they contain **pores** in
the inner membrane (holes promoted by **thermogenin** )
that act as a **proton channel, breaking the H+ gradient** .
They are mitochondria that are less efficient, and produce

more heat than ATP.

**Mechanism:**

**ATPsynthase** is a complex of enzymes that contains **2**
**functional units: F1** ( **soluble part** ) and **F0** ( **integral**
**membrane part** ). They are both connected. **F1** contains the
**catalytic centers.**
Subsequently, F1 is made up of **alpha and beta subunits** (3
and 3). Each **beta subunit** contains the **ATP active site** . F0 and
F1 are **connected** through an arm, the **gamma subunit.**
The interesting part is that gamma is directly in contact with one
of the three beta subunits, and it can turn. If F0 turns, gamma
turns. When it turns, it contacts another beta subunit. **Each of the three beta subunits has**
**a different conformation** . Each can be in one of the three: **beta open** (nothing bound),
**beta-ADP conformation** (active site binds adp and p); beta-tie/ **beta ATP.**
When proton gradient is released through F0, it promotes the **turn of the gamma subunit:** it
is in contact with beta open, and when 3 protons go through F0, gamma turns 120 degrees
and becomes in contact with betaATP. The previous one changes its conformation into betaADP. **Every three protons released, one ATP is synthesized.**

*[PDF p. 122]*

**Shuttle systems to convey cytosolic NADH into mitochondria for oxidation**

Aerobic glycolysis is **highly dependent on the availability of NAD+.**
The **regeneration of NAD+** requires that **NADH** be **reoxidized** in the mitochondrial matrix
by **complex I** of the electronic transport chain.
Since the inner mitochondrial membrane is **impermeable to NADH,** special **shuttle**
**systems** are required to transport cytosolic NADH to the mitochondria.

   - **Malate-aspartate shuttle:** it functions in **liver, kidney and heart mitochondria** . This
metabolic cycle introduces the electrons of NADH into the mitochondria in the form of
**malate** . Subsequently, through the oxidation of **malate to oxaloacetate**, electrons

reduce mitochondrial **NAD+** to **NADH** .

   - **Glycerol 3-phosphate shuttle:** present in **skeletal**
**muscle and brain** . This shuttle differs from the previous
one in that it yields the reduction equivalents from NADH
to ubiquinone, so it does not use the entry gate of
complex I and avoids one coupling center, which leads

into **1 ATP less.**

*[PDF p. 123]*

**Lecture 5.8. Pentose-phosphate pathway**

**PENTOSE-PHOSPHATE PATHWAY**

**GSH = glutathione.** It’s the main regulator of oxidative stress in our
body. Made up of Glutamyl-cysteinyl-glycine. Two glutathione
molecules bind to produce a **dimer**, and the released electrons can

be used to **reduce oxidative stress.** The formation of a disulfide

bridge is an oxidation (release of electrons).This way, they prevent
the oxidation of proteins, lipids,... **they maintain normal levels of**
**oxidative stress by dimerization.**
Glutathione needs to be replenished. For it, we use an enzyme, _**glutathione reductase,**_
which catalyzes the disulfide bonds. To catalyze the reaction, an electron carrier is needed to

donate electrons and maintain normal levels of oxidative stress. This donor is **NADPH** .

Without it, the stress can’t be regulated. NADPH is also involved in the synthesis of DNA, of
lipids,... It’s **obtained through the pentose-phosphate pathway.**

There are 3 main goals of the pentose-phosphate pathway: production of NADPH, formation
of pentoses or degradation of pentoses.
The **pentose phosphate pathway** is an **alternative route to glycolysis for glucose**
**oxidation.** This pathway has the following purposes:

**1.** **Generation of NADPH**

The degradation of glucose to pyruvate and its subsequent complete oxidation to CO2 and
H2O are aimed at generating ATP. Alternatively, some electrons obtained from the oxidation
