of nutrients, instead of being transferred to O2, can be conserved for biosynthetic purposes.
The molecule in charge of **collecting, transporting and donating this reducing power** is
**NADPH**, which is **mainly generated in the pentose phosphate pathway.** NADPH serves
as a **donor of electrons** in biosynthetic pathways.

The pentose phosphate pathway is
important in tissues where **fatty acid**
**and steroid biosynthesis** is actively
taking place, such as the mammary
gland, adipose tissue, adrenal cortex,
or liver. **Lipid biosynthesis requires**

**NADPH** to reduce the more oxidized

precursors.

NADPH is also a source of electrons

for the **reduction of ribonucleotides**

**to deoxyribonucleotides** for **DNA**
synthesis. Therefore, **rapidly**
**proliferating cells,** such as those of
bone marrow, skin and intestinal
mucosa, have **high activity of enzymes of the pentose phosphate pathway.**

*[PDF p. 124]*

Finally, NADPH is important in **protecting cells against reactive**
**oxygen species (ROS)** such as **hydrogen peroxide** and superoxide
**free radicals.** For this reason, the pentose phosphate pathway is
especially active in **erythrocytes**, cells highly exposed to O2 and very
sensitive to oxidative damage.

**2.** **Synthesis of pentoses**

The pentose phosphate pathway allows the **synthesis of pentoses from**
**glycolytic/gluconeogenic precursors.** In turn, pentoses are important precursors for the
synthesis of other molecules. For example, **ribose** is used for the synthesis of nucleic acids,
ATP and coenzymes (such as CoA, NAD+ or FAD).

**3.** **Recycling of pentoses**

Through the pentose phosphate pathway, **pentoses** are **converted into hexoses and**
**trioses,** so that they can be incorporated into the glycolytic or the gluconeogenic pathways.

The pentose phosphate pathway takes place in the **cytosol. It can be anabolic or**
**catabolic** and is divided into two phases:

   - **Oxidative phase:** an **irreversible** process that produces NADPH and ribose
5-phosphate.

   - **Non-oxidative phase:** a **reversible** process to connect pentoses with the
glycolytic/gluconeogenic pathways (for the degradation of pentose).

**A)** **Oxidative phase**

The first reaction of the pentose phosphate pathway
is the **oxidation** of **glucose 6-phosphate** in a
reaction catalyzed by _**glucose 6-phosphate**_
_**dehydrogenase.**_ In this reaction, a molecule of
**NADPH is formed.** The enzyme that regulates the
pathway is the one that catalyzes this step, and a
high amount of people have a mutation in this

enzyme.

Next, the **lactone** ring is hydrolyzed to **6-phosphogluconate** in a reaction catalyzed by

_lactonase_ .

In the third reaction, _6-phosphogluconate dehydrogenase_ catalyzes the **decarboxylation**
(CO2 is released, going from glucose to a pentose of 5 carbons) **and oxidation** of
6-phosphogluconate to **ribulose 5-phosphate** . In that way, a pentose is produced from an
hexose, and a **second NADPH molecule is obtained** .

*[PDF p. 125]*

Ribulose 5-phosphate can be **isomerized** into ribose 5-phosphate, catalyzed by
_phosphopentose isomerase._
In some tissues the process stops here (e.g., highly proliferating tissues where NADPH and
ribose are required for nucleotide synthesis).

The net **balance** of these reactions is:

Glucose 6-phosphate + 2 NADP+ + H2O → ribose 5-phosphate + CO2 + 2 NADPH + 2 H+

**B)** **Non-oxidative phase (reversible)**

The second phase of the pentose
phosphate pathway is the **non-oxidative**
**synthesis of glycolytic intermediates**
**from pentoses.** In particular, **two**

**hexoses and one triose are formed**

**from three pentoses** (2·6C + 3C =
3·5C). The reactions in this phase are
easily **reversible**, so pentoses can also
be synthesized from
glycolytic/gluconeogenic intermediates.

The three pentoses that feed the pathway are **one** molecule of **ribose 5-phosphate** and
**two** molecules of **xylulose 5-phosphate** . Through **three carbon-exchange reactions** two
molecules of **fructose 6-phosphate** and one molecule of **glyceraldehyde 3-phosphate** are
produced (which are intermediates of glycolysis).

Two enzymes participate in this phase, _**transketolase**_ (first and third reaction) which uses
**thiamine pyrophosphate/TPP** (from thiamine or **vitamin B1** ) as a coenzyme, and
_**transaldolase**_ (second reaction). Transketolase catalyzes the first and third reaction of the
process, and transaldolase the second one.

The **balance** of the non-oxidative phase of the pentose phosphate pathway is:

2 Xylulose 5-phosphate + ribose 5-phosphate → 2 fructose 6-phosphate + glyceraldehyde
3-phosphate

**Global balance of the pentose phosphate pathway**

3 Glucose 6-phosphate + 6 NADP+ + 3
H2O → 2 fructose 6-phosphate +

glyceraldehyde 3-phosphate + 6

NADPH + 6 H+ + 3 CO2

*[PDF p. 126]*

**Regulation of the pentose phosphate pathway**

**-**
**Oxidative phase:**
The oxidative phase of the pentose phosphate pathway is controlled by the allosteric
regulation of the activity of _**glucose 6-phosphate dehydrogenase.**_ **NADP+** is the **main**
**positive allosteric regulator** of _glucose 6-phosphate dehydrogenase._ Therefore, an
**increase in NADP+ accelerates the pathway,** whereas an **increase in NADPH slows it**

**down.**

Another regulator of the enzyme is **oxidized glutathione** (in the dimer form), an indicator of
a **high level of oxidative stress.** Oxidized glutathione is an **allosteric activator** of _glucose_
_6-phosphate dehydrogenase._

**-**
**Non-oxidative phase:**
**None of the enzymes** involved in the non-oxidative phase **are regulated.** The three
reactions in this phase are reversible. Therefore, the process proceeds in one direction or
another depending on the concentration of the different metabolites.

**Deficiencies associated with the pentose phosphate pathway**

**-**
**Thiamine deficiency:**

**Thiamine (vitamin B1)** is the precursor of thiamine pyrophosphate ( **TPP** ), the cofactor of
_**transketolase**_ _._ TPP is also a cofactor of other important enzymes of our metabolism,
pyruvate dehydrogenase and α-ketoglutarate dehydrogenase.

Signs of **thiamine deficiency** include weakness, weight loss, swelling in the legs, irritability
and confusion. If the deficiency is severe, it can be lethal. Disorders caused by thiamine
deficiency include **beriberi** and **Wernicke–Korsakoff syndrome.**
**Wernicke–Korsakoff syndrome** is an **encephalopathy** more common among people with
**alcoholism** (heavy alcohol consumption interferes with the intestinal absorption of thiamine).
Symptoms include memory loss, confusion, disorientation, inability to speak, ataxia or visual
difficulties, and may progress to partial paralysis, psychosis, coma and death.

**Genetic deficiencies affecting the pentose phosphate pathway**

The most frequent genetic alteration of the
pentose phosphate pathway is the **deficiency in**
**the enzyme** _**glucose 6-phosphate**_
_**dehydrogenase (G6PD).**_ This defect results in a
**decrease** in the **production of NADPH.** It is one
of the most frequent enzyme deficiencies known
(400 million people worldwide are affected, with
frequencies of up to 25% in parts of Africa and
the East). The deficiency **reduces the enzyme**
**activity.**

*[PDF p. 127]*

Individuals who carry a G6PD deficiency are **asymptomatic under normal conditions.**
However, in **situations in which the cellular oxidative stress increases** (e.g., by
infections or ingestion of certain drugs or foods) G6PD-deficient cells are not able to produce
enough NADPH to maintain the redox homeostasis. One of the **most affected cells** are
**erythrocytes**, a cell type highly exposed to oxidation by the presence of O2. Consequently,
oxidative stress can cause **severe hemolytic anemia** in G6PD-deficient individuals.

**Why are there so many people with this deficiency?**
Because people with it are more **resistant to malaria**
infection. Plasmodium falciparum is very susceptible to
oxidative stress, so people with the disease create an
unwelcoming medium for the parasite to grow and
reproduce. Therefore, it can be seen not as a disease but
as an **adaptation** to the environment. It’s a disease highly
prevalent in areas affected by malaria parasites.
Quinine was initially used to treat malaria because it’s
believed to cause oxidative stress. Quinine treatment in

individuals with this deficiency can trigger hemolytic
anemia, so before treating with quinine we have to assure
that the person does not have this deficiency.
Quinine has been replaced with better anti-malaria drugs, but gin and tonic has prevailed (it
was used during wars to treat malaria. Quinine was mixed with water, sugar and alcohol to
make its ingestion smoother).

*[PDF p. 128]*

**Lecture 5.9. Amino acid catabolism: the urea cycle**

**Metabolism of nitrogen-containing biomolecules,** such as proteins and nucleic acids,
differs significantly from that of carbohydrates and lipids. The degradation of proteins and
nucleic acids also **yields nitrogen products** that are **toxic** and have to be eliminated.
Consequently, **nitrogen-containing compounds** are **not stored in our body.** For that
reason, we must continually supply nitrogen through the diet. It is said that our organism
needs to be in nitrogen balance, where daily intake of nitrogen through the diet equals to that
lost through excretion.

In animals, degradation of amino acids takes place in the following circumstances:

   During intracellular **protein turnover**, some amino acids are reused, while others
undergo degradation.

   - After a **protein-rich meal,** if ingested amino acids exceed the body’s needs, the

excess amino acids cannot be stored and are catabolized.

   In pathological conditions, e.g. diabetes mellitus, or when dietary protein is
insufficient, e.g. during starvation, **proteins** (such as muscle proteins) are **broken**
**down** for the production of energy and precursors (such as glucose).

**General strategy for amino acid catabolism**

For most amino acids, the first step in their
degradation is the **separation of the amino**
**group from the α-keto acid** (the carbon
skeleton). This happens through
**transamination** . An amino group is
transformed into a keto group.
The **α-keto acids** undergo oxidation to CO2
and H2O, or provide intermediates for the
synthesis of carbohydrates, fatty acids or

ketone bodies.

The **amino group** can be used for the
biosynthesis of nitrogenous compounds, or
catabolized to urea, which will be excreted in

the urine.

**Transamination and transport of amino groups to the liver**

**Transamination** is the process by which **nitrogen is removed from amino**
**acids.** In this process, nitrogen is transferred as an amino group from the
original amino acid to α-ketoglutarate. In that way, the **amino acid** is
converted **to** its corresponding **α-keto acid** and **α-ketoglutarate to**
**glutamate** .

*[PDF p. 129]*

Transaminations are **reversible**, so they are involved both in amino acid **degradation and**
**synthesis.**
The enzymes that catalyze these reactions are known as _**transaminases**_ and are located in
the cytoplasm. **Pyridoxal phosphate (PLP)**, which derives from pyridoxine or vitamin B6, is
the required cofactor for these reactions.

**Amino acid degradation** occurs **in all tissues.** However, the **urea cycle**, in which the
nitrogen from the amino group is metabolized for its elimination, takes place primarily in the
**liver** . Thus, there are **mechanisms to transport the nitrogen to the liver. Glutamine and**
**alanine** are the major carriers of nitrogen in the blood.
Glutamine is used for most extrahepatic tissues, whereas alanine is primarily exported from

muscle cells.

**1.** **Transport of glutamine to the liver**

In **extrahepatic tissues,** the transport of ammonia groups to the liver is carried out by
**glutamine** . In fact, glutamine is the **most abundant amino acid in the blood.**

By the action of the enzyme _aspartate_
_transaminase (AST)_, glutamate collects the
amino groups from amino acids. **Two**
**molecules of glutamate** are used to **form a**
**molecule of glutamine** . Glutamine is a
non-toxic form of ammonia transport, and also
functions as a source of amino groups for
many biosynthesis reactions.

Glutamine **travels to the liver for processing.** Once in the liver, _glutaminase_ catalyzes the
conversion of **glutamine to glutamate and NH4+** in the mitochondria. **Glutamate** is further
processed by _glutamate dehydrogenase_, **releasing another ammonia molecule** . The
**ammonia groups** enter the **urea cycle** and the **α-ketoglutarate** can be used in the **citric**
**acid cycle** to obtain energy, or enter **gluconeogenesis** to produce glucose.

**2.** **Transport of alanine to the liver**

**Alanine** can also be used for **transport of**
**amino groups to the liver in a non-toxic**
**form,** mainly by **muscle** . Once glutamate
is formed by transamination, it can transfer
its α-amino group to **pyruvate** by the
action of _alanine aminotransferase (ALT)._
Because the muscle is metabolizing
glucose through glycolysis, pyruvate is
readily available in the muscle.
**Alanine** passes into the blood and **travels to the liver** . Alanine is the **second most**
**abundant amino acid in the blood.** In the cytosol of hepatocytes, ALT catalyzes the
**transfer of the amino group** from **alanine to α-ketoglutarate,** forming **pyruvate** and
**glutamate** .

*[PDF p. 130]*

**Glutamate** can then enter mitochondria, where NH4+ is released.
**Pyruvate** is either **oxidized or** converted to glucose via **gluconeogenesis** . Newly formed
glucose can enter the blood for delivery back to the muscle. This cycle of moving carbons
and nitrogen between the muscle and the liver is known as the **glucose-alanine cycle.**

**THE UREA CYCLE**

Once **ammonia** from amino acid

degradation is deposited in the
mitochondria of hepatocytes, it

**has to be eliminated** to avoid its

**toxicity** .
**Humans** are **ureotelic animals**,

that is, **we eliminate ammonia**

**as urea. In each urea molecule,**
**two nitrogen atoms are**

**eliminated.**

The synthesis of urea takes
place in a pathway known as the
**urea cycle.**

The urea cycle takes place almost exclusively in the **liver** . Once formed, urea passes into
the **bloodstream** and travels to the **kidneys**, where it is excreted into the **urine** .

The urea cycle takes place in two cellular compartments, **mitochondria and cytosol.** The
process is composed of **five steps,** and starts with the ammonia deposited in the

mitochondria.

**Reactions of the urea cycle**

**1.** **Synthesis of carbamoyl phosphate:** in the mitochondrial matrix, ammonia binds to
CO2 to produce **carbamoyl phosphate.** This process requires the **hydrolysis of**
**two ATP** molecules. The reaction is catalyzed by _**carbamoyl phosphate synthetase**_
_**I**_ (!!!)

**2.** **Condensation between carbamoyl phosphate and ornithine:**
carbamoyl phosphate enters the urea cycle by **donating** its
**carbamoyl group** to the amino acid ornithine to form the amino
acid **citrulline**, with the release of inorganic phosphate. The
reaction is catalyzed by _**ornithine transcarbamoylase**_ _(OTC)._
Citrulline **leaves the mitochondria to the cytosol** using a
specific **carrier** .

*[PDF p. 131]*

**3.** **Condensation between citrulline and aspartate:** the
carbonyl group of citrulline reacts with aspartate forming
**argininosuccinate** . This cytosolic reaction is catalyzed by
_argininosuccinate synthetase_ and requires the **hydrolysis of**
**two high-energy phosphate bonds.** In that way, the **second**
**amino group** is introduced in the cycle.

**4.** **Cleavage of argininosuccinate:** argininosuccinate is cleaved in a reaction
catalyzed by _argininosuccinate lyase_ to form **arginine** and **fumarate** . **Fumarate** can
enter the mitochondria to join the **citric acid cycle**, allowing the **regeneration of**
**oxaloacetate,** the source of aspartate for the urea cycle.

**5.** **Cleavage of arginine:** the cytosolic enzyme _arginase_
catalyzes the cleavage of arginine to yield **urea** and
**ornithine** . _**Arginase**_ is **only present in the liver of ureotelic**
**animals (only found in the liver).** Ornithine is transported

**back into** the **mitochondria** to initiate another round of the

urea cycle.

**Overall balance of the urea cycle**

CO2 + NH4+ + 3 ATP + aspartate + 2 H2O → urea + 2 ADP + AMP + 4 Pi + fumarate

**Urea synthesis is energetically expensive for the organism.** This explains why dietary
proteins are more important as a source of precursors than as a source of energy.

**Regulation of the urea cycle**

The activity of the urea cycle is **regulated at two levels:**

   - **Long-term regulation:** when the **diet** is rich in protein, the rate of synthesis of liver
enzymes involved in the urea cycle is increased. This also occurs during prolonged
**starvation**, when muscle protein begins to be used.

   - **Short-term regulation:** _carbamoyl phosphate synthetase I_ is **allosterically**
**activated** by **N-acetylglutamate** . N-acetylglutamate comes from the
reaction of glutamate and acetyl-CoA. It’s a signal that there is enough
energy (AcCOA) and nitrogen to be eliminated (glutamate).

*[PDF p. 132]*

**Defects in the urea cycle**

   **Hyperammonemia** is a pathological condition in which
**high levels of ammonia** are **present in the blood.**
Ammonia is **neurotoxic**, causing lethargy, tremors,
slurred speech, blurred vision, vomiting, coma, and death.
Hyperammonemia can be caused by:

**Hepatic damage:** from alcohol, toxic chemicals, hepatitis, etc.
- **Genetic deficiencies in enzymes of the urea cycle:** the most
frequent deficiency affects _**ornithine transcarbamoylase (OTC),**_
but deficiencies can also be found in _carbamoyl_
_phosphate synthetase I, ornithine translocase_, _argininosuccinate_
_synthetase, argininosuccinate lyase, arginase,_ and even
_N-acetylglutamate synthase,_ the enzyme responsible for the synthesis of the allosteric
activator of the pathway.
Individuals with deficiencies in enzymes of the urea cycle cannot tolerate a diet rich in
protein. They need to follow a strict diet supplemented with essential amino acids. There are
also specific therapies for particular enzyme deficiencies.

**CATABOLISM OF α-KETO ACIDS**

The carbon skeletons **derived from the transamination** of amino acids are **degraded** or
can be **derived for the synthesis of carbohydrates or fatty acids.** In this respect, amino
acids fall into two categories: **glucogenic and ketogenic.**

**Glucogenic amino acids** are those that
**give rise to pyruvate or citric acid**
**cycle intermediates,** which are
precursors of **glucose** . All amino acids,
except lysine and leucine, are
glucogenic, at least partially **.**
**Ketogenic amino acids** give rise to
**acetyl-CoA** or **acetoacetyl-CoA**, neither
of which can be used for glucose
production. Lysine and leucine are the
only amino acids that are purely
ketogenic.

**Cofactors involved in the catabolism of α-keto acids**

Several enzyme **cofactors** participate in the **degradation of the carbon skeleton** of amino

acids.

Some of the most relevant cofactors are:

*[PDF p. 133]*

- **Pyridoxal phosphate (PLP):** derived from **vitamin B6**, is used in **transamination**
reactions, but also in isomerizations, modifications of amino acid side chains,

decarboxylations, etc.

   - **Biotin: vitamin B7**, it’s a cofactor that participates in **carboxylation** reactions.
Besides reactions in amino acid metabolism, biotin is also a cofactor for acetyl-CoA
carboxylase.

   - **Tetrahydrofolates (THF):** derived from **folic acid** or **vitamin B12,** participate in

**one-carbon transfer** when the carbon is in intermediate oxidation states.

   - **S-adenosylmethionine (SAM or AdoMet)** : is the characteristic **cofactor for**
**methylation** (transfer of a methyl group). Apart from its role in amino acid
metabolism, it is also the cofactor of the _methyltransferases_ responsible for the
**methylation of cytosine in DNA.**

   - **Tetrahydrobiopterin (BH4):** participates in **oxidation-reduction** reactions.
_**Phenylalanine hydroxylase**_, the first enzyme in the catabolic pathway of
phenylalanine, requires the **cofactor tetrahydrobiopterin** . This cofactor carries
**electrons from NADPH to O2**, becoming oxidized to dihydrobiopterin in the process.

**Genetic defects in the catabolism of α-keto acids**

Phenylketonuria, tyrosinemia, homocystinuria, nonketotic hyperglycinemia or maple syrup
urine disease. They can be diagnosed by analyzing amino acid concentrations in body fluids.

One of the most frequent amino acid metabolism disorders is

**PHENYLKETONURIA** . This disorder is associated with a

genetic deficiency of _**phenylalanine hydroxylase.**_ The
deficiency results in an over **accumulation of phenylalanine**,
which is converted into **toxic compounds.** Individuals affected
by this disease usually develop seizures, brain damage, and

mental retardation.

*[PDF p. 134]*

**Lecture 5.10. Fatty acids oxidation**

**Mobilization of stored fats**

In hypoglycemic situations, hormones such as glucagon and
epinephrine are released. This triggers the activation of adenylate
cyclase in adipose tissue, causing an increase of cAMP. This will
activate PKA which will subsequently phosphorylate a protein present
in the lipidic droplet, called **perilipin** . **Phosphorylated perilipin**
**attracts cytosolic lipase to TAGs**, which are hydrolyzed into
**glycerol** and **fatty acids.** They are released into the bloodstream to
be further oxidized in the tissues where the energy or precursors are

needed.

**Activation and mobilization of fatty acids**

Fatty acids with 12 or less carbons can enter the mitochondria, but those with **14 or more**
**Carbons** must be transported by a system known as the **carnitine shuttle.**

First, the **fatty acid is activated** by
**binding** to **Coenzyme A**, a reaction
catalyzed by _fatty acyl-CoA_
_synthetases_ (catalyze the formation
of a thioester bond). In the reaction,
ATP is completely hydrolyzed to
AMP, which implies the **expenditure**
**of 2 ATPs.** Thus, a high-energy
molecule is formed, **fatty-acyl CoA.**
This molecule is transferred to the mitochondria through the carnitine shuttle: first, **CAT I**
**(** _**carnitine acyl transferase I**_ **) transfers** the fatty acid **from the CoA to carnitine**, forming
**acyl-carnitine.** This molecule enters the mitochondria through a transporter, and in the
matrix, the fatty acid is **transferred from carnitine to a new CoA** molecule by **CAT II**
**(** _**carnitine acyltransferase II**_ **)** located on the inner face of the inner mitochondrial

membrane.

This transport is the **stage that limits the rate of fatty acid oxidation** . The principal
allosteric regulator of CAT I is **malonyl CoA** (molecule formed in the first stage of lipid
synthesis. This way, fatty acids aren’t being formed and degraded at the same time).

**FATTY ACID OXIDATION**

It occurs in three stages:

   Beta-oxidation, where Acetyl-CoA and electron carriers are

released from the f. a. chain.

   In the second stage, Acetyl-CoA is incorporated into the Krebs
cycle to obtain energy.

*[PDF p. 135]*

In the third stage, NADH and FADH2 molecules channel the electrons to the
respiratory chain, allowing ATP synthesis.

**Beta-oxidation of saturated fatty acids**

1. C2 and C3 of the fatty acid are oxidized by _**acyl-CoA**_
_**dehydrogenase**_ . There are **3 isozymes** of this enzyme,
depending on the length of the fatty acid that is being oxidized.
They all have **FAD** as an oxidation-reduction **cofactor**, which
accepts the released electrons ( **FADH2** ) in the reaction and
passes them to the electron transport chain through a protein
called **electron transport flavoprotein (ETF).**

        - **LCAD** (12-18 carbons)
        - **MCAD** (4-14 carbons)
        - **SCAD** (4-8 carbons)

2. A molecule of **H2O** is added to the double bond, to form
**beta-hydroxyacyl-CoA,** a reaction catalyzed by _Enoyl-CoA_
_hydratase._

3. The **dehydrogenation of C3** (beta carbon) occurs, forming
**beta-ketoacyl-CoA.** This reaction is catalyzed by
_hydroxyacyl-CoA-dehydrogenase_, where the electron acceptor
is **NAD+,** which will form **NADH** .

The total yield of this process would be 108 ATPs, but because 2 ATPs are spent in
oxidation, the **net yield** is **106 ATPs.**

**Regulation of fatty acid oxidation**

**Pathologies caused by Fatty-acyl-coA dehydrogenases defects**

   - One of the most frequent defects is due to a **mutation in MCAD** . It causes **fat**
**accumulation** in the liver, low blood glucose, vomiting and coma.

*[PDF p. 136]*

There are other cases of mutations which produce a **deficit in the ability to obtain**
**energy from fat oxidation**, which causes serious heart disease and skeletal muscle

disorders.

**KETONE BODIES**

During periods of high activity, **Acetyl-CoA** produced by beta-oxidation is mainly oxidized in
the Krebs cycle. But in the liver there is an alternative fate, where it can also become **ketone**
**bodies: acetone, acetoacetate and beta-hydroxybutyrate.** The final destination of these
ketone bodies is to be exported to **other tissues** like skeletal and cardiac muscle, in some
cases the brain,...

**Synthesis of ketone bodies**

1. The first step consists of the **condensation of two acetyl-CoA**
molecules through the action of _thiolase_, giving rise to
**acetoacetyl-CoA.**

2. **Acetoacetyl-CoA is condensed with a third acetyl-CoA**
molecule to form **beta-hydroxy-beta-methylglutaryl-CoA**, by
_**HMG-CoA synthase**_ .

3. **HMG-CoA breaks down,** forming **Acetoacetate and**
**Acetyl-CoA.** Acetoacetate can be reduced to
**beta-hydroxybutyrate** or decarboxylated to **acetone**, which is
**highly volatile** and exhaled in the lungs.

In healthy people, the amount of
acetone is low. In **diabetics**, a **large amount of**
**acetoacetate** and therefore, **acetate**, is produced,
because fatty acid degradation increases due to the
unavailability of glucose. This explains why their breath
smells of this compound. The excessive increase of

ketone bodies in untreated diabetics causes a decrease

in blood pH ( **ketoacidosis** ) which

can lead to coma. Diets **low in**

**carbohydrates** can also lead to

ketoacidosis.

4. **Acetoacetate and hydroxybutyrate** are soluble, so via the
blood they reach **peripheral tissues** . Acetoacetate is activated
by forming an ester bond with CoA, generating
**acetoacetyl-CoA.**

5. Finally _thiolase_ cleaves acetoacetyl-CoA producing **two**
**Acetyl-CoA molecules** that are **oxidized in Krebs to obtain**

**energy.**

*[PDF p. 137]*

**Lecture 5.11 and 5.12 . Nucleotide metabolism I and II**

   They are precursors of DNA and RNA

   They are essential carriers of energy (ATP, GTP)

   They act as cofactors (FAD, NAD+,..)

   They are activators of biosynthetic intermediates (ex: UDP-glucose)

   Act as second messengers (ex: cAMP)

   For some pathogens, these pathways are essential (either they can’t carry it out
themselves or they need a large amount of nucleotides). If we are able to block
specific nucleotide pathways, we can fight against the pathogens

   Tumors need a large amount of nucleotides in order to divide, so interfering with
nucleotide metabolism allows them to fight against them.

   Nucleotides can’t be stored, with the exception of ATP. If the cell needs to divide, it
needs to synthesize nucleotides.

There are two strategies for nucleotide synthesis:

1. **De-novo synthesis.** Starting the process from the beginning, by using small
precursors. This process consumes a large amount of ATP, so cells synthesize
nucleotides when they have a high energy level.
Nitrogen would come from two sources: **glutamine and aspartate** (principal N
donors during nucleotide synthesis).
Deoxyribonucleotides are synthesized after ribonucleotides are made de-novo,
through the exchange of an OH with a H.

2. **Salvage pathway:** allows cells to **recycle nitrogenous bases.** During nucleotide
catabolism, those nitrogenous bases (mainly purines) can be rejoined to a pentose.
This process consumes much less energy.

**DE NOVO PURINE NUCLEOTIDE SYNTHESIS**

_(no need to study the whole pathway, only know which are the precursors for each atom in_
_the purine ring)_

- C5 comes from aspartate

- N3 comes from glutamine

- C6 comes from CO2

- N1 from aspartate

- C2 and C8 from a **formyl tetrahydrofolate** group (folate becomes activated into
tetrahydrofolate, resulting in formyl-tetrahydrofolate). Folate is necessary for nucleotide
synthesis. Without it, the neural tube in embryos closes, resulting in impairments in the
formation of the spinal cord. One of the diseases this may cause is **spina-bifida.**

**Purine de novo pathway**

First, **ribose-5-phosphate** (comes mainly from the pentose-phosphate pathway) is activated
by phosphorylation, turning into **5-Phosphoribosyl-1-pyrophosphate (PRPP)** by the
enzyme _PRPP synthase._ This step is common to purines and pyrimidines.

*[PDF p. 138]*

Then comes the **commitment step:** an **amino** group from **glutamine** is incorporated to C1
of ribose, by _**glutamine-PRPP-amidotransferase**_ .
After various reactions and the closure of the rings, a molecule of **Inositate (IMP)** is formed.
At this point, the synthesis pathway forks to generate either AMP or GMP.

   - The conversion of **IMP to AMP** occurs through the incorporation of an amino group
from **Aspartate (Asp)** . Asp joins C6, forming adenylosuccinate by the use of **GTP** as
energy donor. Then adenylosuccinate is broken by the enzyme
_adenylosuccinate-lyase_, releasing fumarate and **Adenylate (AMP)** .
   - **GMP** is formed by oxidation of IMP at C2, and the subsequent incorporation of an
amino group from **Glutamine** . In this case, the energy donor is **ATP** .

**Regulation of purine synthesis**

3 levels of regulation ( **feedback inhibition** ) :

   - 1st level, in _**PRPP synthetase**_ : This process requires ATP, so it’s **inhibited by** the
presence of **ADP** (low energy levels = no-go for the process). ATP activates the

process.
   - 2nd level, in _**glutamine-PRPP-amidotransferase**_ **/ commitment step:** it’s **inhibited**
by the final products of the process, **AMP** and **GMP** . But they are both weak
inhibitors. If they are both at high level, they execute a **synergic inhibition** (strong
inhibition). **IMP** also acts as a strong inhibitor.

   3rd level, at the **branching level: AMP inhibits** the pathway for it’s synthesis at this
level by blocking _**adenylosuccinate-synthase**_, favouring IMP to be used for GMP
synthesis; **GMP inhibits** _**IMP dehydrogenase**_, stimulating AMP synthesis.

**PURINE SALVAGE PATHWAY**

   NUCLEOTIDE CATABOLISM: They can be ribo or deoxyribonucleotides. By
dephosphorylation, we obtain a nucleoside. The next step is the separation between
the base and the ribose. Ribose can be reused, but the **purine ring** has to be
**degraded** (either Adenine or Guanine). The final end product of this degradation is
**uric acid.** It has a **low solubility and can be toxic**, so the less uric acid we produce,
the better. If uric acid levels increase, a disease that is caused can be gout (uric acid
accumulates in the joints). In order to **decrease the amount of uric acid produced**
through catabolism, we find this **salvage pathway.** It’s also a way to synthesize
nucleotides without the energy consumption that de-novo synthesis requires.

There are 2 enzymes for the salvage pathway:

   - _**Adenine phosphoribosyltransferase**_ **(APRT):** Adenine + PRPP = AMP

   _**Hypoxanthine-guanine phosphoribosyltransferase**_ **(HGPRT):**

    - Hypoxanthine + PRPP = IMP (precursor of AMP and GMP)

   - Guanine + PRPP = GMP

*[PDF p. 139]*

A lack of activity in these enzymes results in **gout** . A mutation in **HGPRT** is worse, because
it creates GMP and precursors for AMP (ex: IMP)

There is a syndrome, **Lesch-Nyhan syndrome**, which is caused by almost no activity in
**HGPRT** . Lots of **uric acid** is **accumulated**, which damages the **kidneys** but also the **brain**,
causing neural disease.

**PYRIMIDINE DE NOVO SYNTHESIS**

- N3 comes from Glutamine

- C2 comes from CO2

- Aspartate also acts as a Nitrogen precursor

**Synthesis pathway pyrimidines**

First, the amino group from Glutamine condensates with CO2 (reaction catalyzed by
_**carbamoyl-phosphate synthetase II**_, present in the cytoplasm) by consuming 2 ATPs.
Carbamoyl phosphate is created, and then condenses with aspartate to form carbamoyl
aspartate through _**aspartate-transcarbamoylase**_ (ATC), which is one of the main control
points of the route. The ring is closed by dehydration, and oxidized (NADH release), giving
way to Orotate. Ribose-5-phosphate binds to the ring, generating a nucleotide called
Orotidylate. It’s then decarboxylated (CO2 release) and uridine is generated, with which the
final product is Uridylate (UMP).
UMP can be phosphorylated and aminated to form CTP (cytidine-5-triphosphate).

**Regulation**

CTP levels regulate the pathway through allosteric inhibition of the CAD complex.
During apoptosis, the CAD complex is degraded, so the synthesis of pyrimidines is stopped.

**SYNTHESIS OF DEOXYRIBONUCLEOTIDES**

All **deoxyribonucleotides** are **synthesized by their corresponding ribonucleotide**, by
exchanging an OH group by an H group through a small electron transport system.
There is only one enzyme for this process: _**ribonucleotide reductase**_ . It uses
ribonucleoside-diphosphates as substrates. During the process, two H groups will be
required and provided by NADPH coming from the pentose-phosphate pathway. Without
enough NADPH we can't synthesize enough ribonucleotides.
_**Ribonucleotide reductase**_ is a heterodimer which contains two alpha-beta subunits. The
alpha subunits contain **two regulatory sites** : one is for the **specificity** (drives what would
be the substrate that is used) and the other, the **activity** site.
Depending on which is the allosteric molecule bound, the enzyme will use one specific
substrate or another. **ATP** in general allosterically **activates** the enzyme, and **dATP** is an

**inhibitor.**

ADP turns to dADP, GTP to dGDP, UDP to dUDP,...

*[PDF p. 140]*

**Synthesis of dTMP**

**Thymine** nucleotide is only used in its deoxy form ( **dTMP**, only found in DNA). The
deoxythymidine nucleotides come from deoxyuracil nucleotides (dUMP), and involve a
**methylation reaction.**
We can interfere with cells with high growth rate just by interfering with the synthesis of
deoxythymidine nucleotides. If this can be done specifically on tumor cells, we can fight very
effectively against them. This has become a therapeutic project, but the only problem is that
it also affects healthy fast-growing cells, so it has many side effects.

**Mechanism:**

To transform **dUMP**, we have to add a **methyl group** on the uracil base. This reaction is
catalyzed by _**thymidylate synthase**_ ( **methylene-tetrahydrofolate** is the donor of the methyl
group), and catalyzes the **transfer of the methyl group.** Once methylene-tetrahydrofolate
donates the methyl group, it becomes deactivated to dihydrofolate, and we obtain **dTMP.**
Continuous vitamin ingest is important to keep this process going. To reactivate
m-tetrahydrofolate: **dihydrofolate** is reduced, catalyzed by _**dihydrofolate reductase**_, by
using **NADPH**, reducing it to **tetrahydrofolate**, and then a methyl group is transferred from
serine (which becomes glycine). Tetrahydrofolate becomes **methyl-tetrahydropholate** one
again.

**Fluorouracil** acts **against** _**thymidine synthase**_, so it’s used to fight tumors. It acts as an
inhibitor of _thymidylate synthase._
There are also _dihydrofolate reductase_ inhibitors: methotrexate and aminopterin, which are
used for antitumoral purposes as it blocks synthesis of dTTP.

**CATABOLISM OF NUCLEOTIDES AND GOUT**

During **purine catabolism**, the final product is **uric acid.** An excess of uric acid can result
for example in **gout** (pain in the joints due to the accumulation of uric acid), but more serious
diseases can occur if the amount of uric acid is higher. This low-soluble molecule, if very
concentrated, can **precipitate** inside **tubules in the kidney**, or the **nephrons**, and give way
to **kidney failure.**
**From GMP and AMP**, we obtain, after a series of steps, **xanthine** (they both converge in
this step), which is then transformed in a reaction catalyzed by _**xanthine oxidase**_ into **uric**
**acid.** That’s why **salvage pathways are important**, they prevent the overly high production

of uric acid.

There are drugs that inhibit xanthine oxidase (last 2 steps from AMP), which prevents the

formation of uric acid.

Another disease caused by the impairment in the adenine nucleotide catabolism is the
**severe combined immunodeficiency syndrome (SCID).** A lack in _**adenosine deaminase**_
produces **increased** amounts of **AMP and dAMP** (which **inhibits** _**ribonucleotide**_
_**reductase**_ ), so the synthesis of ribonucleotides si impaired. Those cells with high growth
rate are affected, for example, lymphocytes. Their mitosis is impaired and this causes a **lack**
**of immunological response.** It’s caused by a mutation in the ADA gene. One of the first
diseases treated by gene therapy.

*[PDF p. 141]*

**Lecture 5.13. Synthesis of lipids**

We will mainly focus on the **synthesis of fatty acids** . The most part of glucose carbons that
can´t be stored as glycogen, will be stored as triacylglycerols. But these fatty acids can be
used for other functions: for example, to synthesize membrane lipids (specially during
childhood).
Lipids have many functions, such as pigments, cofactors, membranes,...

**SYNTHESIS OF FATTY ACIDS**

Synthesis and fatty acid catabolism are highly independent. Beta oxidation occurs in the
mitochondria, and synthesis in the cytosol. This allows us to **coordinate** the processes.
When the cell needs energy, it oxidizes fatty acids, but it won’t be synthesized. Specific
markers make that coordination possible.

The excess of carbohydrates (glucose) will be used to synthesize fatty acids: it’s oxidized to
pyruvate, and this is oxidized and decarboxylated to acetyl-CoA.
When there is balance between energy need and ATP, the most part of Acetyl CoA will be
oxidized through the Krebs cycle; But if the **ATP/ADP and NADH/NAD+ ratios are high**, the
Krebs cycle is slowed, and Acetyl-CoA accumulates in the mitochondria. Also, there is a
citrate accumulation. This situation will cause the release of citrate to the cytoplasm, where it
is broken to **Acetyl-CoA** and **Oxaloacetate** . These precursors will be the **donor of carbons**
**for the synthesis of fatty acids** and other molecules.
To do this, **Acetyl-CoA** first **needs to be activated** . It’s activated through a **carboxylation**
catalyzed by _**Acetyl-CoA-carboxylase**_ . **Biotin** (a cofactor used for carboxylation) acts as a
**cofactor** in this reaction. A carbon enters and there is a **consumption of ATP** which goes to
ADP, and the final product is **Malonyl-CoA** . This molecule is the **main regulator of**
**beta-oxidation**, because it’s an **allosteric inhibitor of CAT I** (transporter for transporting
fatty acids into the mitochondria): malonyl-CoA concentration is high at the cytoplasm when
a large amount of citrate is being released out of the mitochondria due to Acetyl-CoA
accumulation in the Krebs Cycle, which symbolizes energy.

**Acetyl-CoA carboxylase regulation**

This reaction is **allosterically inhibited by Palmitate (Palmitoyl-CoA),** a fatty acid, the
**main final product of the fatty acid synthesis.**
It’s **allosterically activated by citrate**, when there is an excess citrate in the mitochondria.
It can **also** be **regulated by covalent modification**, changes in the conformation of the
enzyme. It can either be
**phosphorylated** ( **inactive** ) or
**dephosphorylated** ( **active** ). This
phosphorylation is mediated, in
general, by **PKA**, which is
deactivated by phosphorylation.
But in the muscles it’s additionally
regulated by AMPK. It’s activated
by a _**phosphatase**_ (PPase).

*[PDF p. 142]*

**Low glucose concentrations** will activate **glucagon and epinephrine,** and therefore,
**PKA**, inhibiting the ACC enzyme as it becomes phosphorylated.
With **high glucose levels**, **insulin** is activated, which activates _**phosphatase,**_ thus
promoting fatty acid synthesis. The end product (malonyl-CoA) inhibits beta-oxidation.

**Fatty acid synthesis mechanism**

In the **inner mitochondrial membrane,** there is a **citrate transporter** which allows its
release. Once in the cytoplasm, _**citrate lyase**_, which **releases two acyl carbons** . The 4

other carbons remain as oxaloacetate.

The **oxaloacetate** is transported back to the mitochondria by two methods:

   - Through the **reduction of oxaloacetate to malate**, by _**malate dehydrogenase**_ **,**
which enters inside the mitochondrial matrix because there is a **malate transporter.**
Once inside the mitochondria, it can be reoxidized to oxaloacetate.

   - Another option is that this **malate** can be **decarboxylated and oxidized** by the use
of **NADP** . An **oxidative carboxylation** is carried out. The product is **pyruvate**, which
enters the mitochondria through a **pyruvate transporter.**

Fatty acid synthesis is regulated by the enzyme _**Fatty Acid Synthase**_ . It’s an enzyme with 7
different domains (MAT domain, KR domain, KS domain, TE domain, ER domain,...). In the
**middle** there is an additional domain, the **ACP** ( **acyl-carrier-protein**, with an “arm” that is
part of the coenzyme A). It’s function is to maintain the fatty acid bound during the whole

process.
Fatty acid biosynthesis would be something like a reverse

beta-oxidation:

**1.** **Condensation**

**2.** **Reduction**

**3.** **Dehydration**

**4.** **Reduction**

This enzymatic complex enlarges the fatty acid until the fatty acid bound to the prosthetic
group has **16 carbons** (in other words, **palmitic acid** ). Then the **thioesterase activity** is
activated, and **palmitic acid is released from the complex** . That’s why palmitate is an
allosteric inhibitor of the process (reverse feedback). From this palmitate, we can **introduce**
**unsaturations** and **generate other fatty acids.**
Linoleic and linolenic acid are essential fatty acids; they have to be introduced in our system
through the diet.

**Overall balance**

To generate palmitate, we need to activate **7 Acetyl-CoA** **+ 7 ATP + 7 CO2**, which gives us
**7 malonyl-COA 7 ADP + 7 P.**
Then, **7 malonyl-CoA molecules, 1 Acetyl- CoA and 14** **NADPH + H+** give us **Palmitate,**
**8 CoA, 7 CO2 and 14 NADP+**

*[PDF p. 143]*

Therefore, **8 AcCOA+7ATP+14NADPH+14H+** gives us **Palmitate+14 NADP+8CoA+6H2O.**
It’s a high energy process, so it’s only carried out when possible.

Keep in mind the relation between carbohydrates and fatty acids, and the relation between
beta-oxidation and f. a. biosynthesis.

*[PDF p. 144]*

**Lecture 5.14. Cholesterol**

**Structure of cholesterol**

It is mostly **hydrophobic**, with a very small **hydrophilic**
area. It’s a hydrophobic molecule.
The drugs used to treat high cholesterol levels are called
**statins**, as it is a competitive molecule that binds to the
same binding site as cholesterol.

**Cholesterol functions and sources**

- It’s an important **component of the membrane** that regulates **fluidity** and **stability** . All our

membranes have cholesterol.

- It’s also an important **precursor** for different molecules, such as bile acids (participate in
the absorption of lipids by emulsionating them) or steroid hormones.

There are two main sources:

   - **Diet** : it’s absorbed by our enterocytes and enters the bloodstream.
   - **De novo synthesis** : the **liver** is the organ in which cholesterol is mostly synthesized.
If we reduce the cholesterol amount we ingest, we will synthesize more de novo
cholesterol, and the other way round.

**Cholesterol synthesis**

   - **Source of carbon atoms:** they come from **acetate**, in the form of Acetyl-CoA. All

carbon atoms from cholesterol come from acetate. It’s a **condensation of acetates.**

**-**
**Steps:** we typically divide it into **4 steps**

1. **Acetyl-CoA to Mevalonate** (6C): there is a

**condensation** of **three acetate** molecules.

2. **Mevalonate to Phosphorylated isoprene:** it
has 5 carbon atoms, so a **decarboxylation**
occurs. CO2 is released and we produce an
isoprene.
3. **Phosphorylated isoprenes to squalene:** by
**condensation**, hepatocytes create a 30
Carbon molecule, **squalene** (condensation of 6
isoprenes)
4. **Squalene to cholesterol:** throughout many
reactions (cyclation, elimination,..) squalene

becomes **cholesterol** .

   - **Formation of mevalonate:** it’s the last reaction in the

first step. The **precursor** is a molecule called
**HMG-CoA (hydroxy-methyl-glutaryl-CoA).** The
process is catalyzed by _**HMG-CoA reductase**_, and
there is a **reduction** of the **carboxylic acid** group,
which is reduced to **alcohol** (2 reductions).

*[PDF p. 145]*

Therefore, **2 NADPH** molecules are needed for this reduction (carboxylic acid to
aldehyde to alcohol), they are oxidized into **2 NADP+.**

    - This is the reaction at which cholesterol synthesis is **regulated** .

    - Also, it’s the point where **statins** work: they are inhibitors of this enzyme, _HMG-CoA_
_reductase:_ statin acts as a **competitive inhibitor**, binding to the same site as
mevalonate to the enzyme (reversible inhibition).

**Cholesterol synthesis regulation**

We need **18 Acetyl-CoA, 36 ATPs and 16 NADPH for the** **synthesis of 1 cholesterol**
**molecule.** It’s a very **expensive process** for our cells. That’s why it has to be highly
regulated. In addition to this, it’s not easily eliminated and it’s dangerous to eliminate it.

It’s regulated through the regulation of _**HMG-CoA reductase**_ . There are different levels of
regulation:

**-**
**Long-term levels**
   - **Short-term levels:** by **covalent modification (phosphorylation)** . A **kinase**
**phosphorylates (inhibition)** and a **phosphatase dephosphorylates (activates).**

**Factors that determine cholesterol synthesis**

   - **Glucagon** : released when **low glucose levels** in the blood. All the energy will be
derived to the production of glucose. Fatty acid and cholesterol synthesis will be
stopped.
   - **AMP** : high levels of AMP symbolize low energy. Therefore, cholesterol synthesis will

be inhibited

   - **Insulin** : it’s released when there are **high glucose levels**, and symbolizes abundant
carbon and energy sources. Therefore it activates cholesterol synthesis and other
anabolic processes.

**Fates of cholesterol**

   - **Formation of bile acids** for the emulsion of intestinal lipids. Bile
acids are stored in the **gallbladder**, and secreted into the small
intestine after a fatty meal. They assist the **emulsification of**
**fats** . In the liver we produce cholesterol and bile acids, which go
to the gallbladder for storage, and are then released into the

intestine. The bile acids are then excreted in the feces or

reabsorbed, back into the gallbladder. There are drugs that
inhibit this reabsorption to reduce cholesterol levels.

   - **Steroid hormones** (Such as **progesterone**, which can then give place to cortisol,
testosterone,estradiol,...). Male and female sex hormones differe in the first ciclic
ring: the female one is generally an aromatic ring.

                            - **Vitamin D3:** it comes from

7-dehydrocholesterol. It’s a vitamin we can produce
**endogenously** . By using **UV light** as the **catalyzer**, a
bond in 7-dehydrocholesterol is broken, generating
**cholecalciferol (Vit D3).**

*[PDF p. 146]*

**Cholesteryl esters** (esterification of cholesterol, bound to fatty acids). This occurs
because in that way, the hydrophobicity of cholesterol is higher, which is good for it’s
storage and transport in….

**Transport of lipids**

**Lipoproteins** are the molecules that we use to **transport cholesterol and fatty acids** in the

form of TAG.

Inside the lipoprotein we find all the hydrophobic elements. The density of each lipoprotein is
related to the amount of protein and lipids.
In our body, there are **4 major lipoproteins:**

   - **Chylomicrons** : lipoprotein produced in the **enterocytes** of the intestine when the
lipids obtained from the diet enter our system. From here, chylomicrons enter the
circulation and arrive at the **liver** or **other tissues** . When the body doesn’t want
these fatty acids anymore, they arrive at the liver where they are excreted.
   - **VLDL** : particles produced in the **liver** . They go in the bloodstream, **releasing fatty**
**acids** for other tissues. When the lipids are released, the VLDL becomes another
molecule, richer in cholesterol, called LDL.

   - **LDL** : particle **rich in cholesterol** . Through this molecule, the liver releases
cholesterol to other cells. B100 is the protein that recognizes the receptor (review
LDL internalization through clathrin vesicles). There are people with **deficiencies in**
**the receptor** that doesn’t allow them to internalize cholesterol. It’s known as **familial**
**hypercholesterolemia.**
   - **HDL** : particle produced in the **intestine**, whose function is to **remove cholesterol**

and send it to the liver.

*[PDF p. 147]*

**Lecture 5.15. Amino acid synthesis**

**Fixation of Nitrogen**

**Nitrogen** can be easily
obtained through the air.
However, there is a

limitation: it has to be

incorporated into
biomolecules. For that, it

has to be converted to

**ammonia (FIXATION)**, but
we can’t carry out this
process, not because it’s not a thermodynamically favourable process, but because it’s **very**
**slow.** The **activation energy** is so **high**, we don’t see any change.
This is solved with an enzyme ( _nitrogenase complex_ ), but it’s only found in some bacteria
and algae. The rest of living beings depend on these organisms to obtain Nitrogen.
**Ammonia**, once obtained, needs to be incorporated into biomolecules, specifically to form
Glutamate. We don’t carry out this process because ammonia is toxic for humans, but plants
can carry out this process. From glutamate, there can be transaminases to produce other
aminoacids. Glutamate can also be converted to glutamine by glutamine synthetase. This
glutamine can be used for the biosynthesis of N-containing compounds.
This **glutamate coming from plants** is our **source of Nitrogen** . From glutamate we can
form Glutamine or other nitrogen containing compounds.

_**Glutamine synthetase**_

Very **complex enzyme**, it has **12 subunits**, each with **8 allosteric sites** (very complex
regulation).
It’s not only allosterically regulated, but also regulated by covalent regulation. But in this
case, not by phosphorylation (like in most cases), but by adenylation.

**-**
**Allosteric regulation**

Glutamate is converted to glutamine by the incorporation of
NH3 and ATP expenditure. _(synthetase = ATP_
_expenditure)_ . Glutamine can then be converted to AMP,
CTP, Tryptophan, Histidine,... **Glutamine is the precursor**
**of many Nitrogen-containing compounds** . These
products and other **N containing molecules** carry out the
**allosteric regulation**, by **inhibiting** the activity of the

enzyme.

*[PDF p. 148]*

**-** **Covalent modification**

Carried out by **Adenylation** : Tyrosine binds to an
AMP group and forms **adenylate** . The enzyme will be
**inhibited by adenylylation, and activated when**
**deadenylylation occurs.**

**Regulation of** _**glutamine-synthetase**_

- When the **cell needs nitrogen-containing compounds,** _**glutamine-synthetase**_ **is active**
**and not Adenylated.**

- When there is **no more need for N compounds**, there is an **adenylylation** : AMP binds to
_glutamine-synthetase_ by ATP expenditure, through an enzyme called
_**adenylate-transferase (AT)**_ . In order for this enzyme to work (be active) and transfer the P
in ATP to _glutamine-synthetase_, **P II has to be bound to AT.**
This covalent modification (the binding of P II) is reversible.

- The **deadenylation** occurs when the same enzyme that catalyzes the adenylylation
_(adenylyl-transferase)_ **binds to UMP.**
What determines that the regulatory protein (AT) is free or bound to UMP?
This is **determined by another enzyme**, which catalyzes th **e transfer of UMP to the**
**regulatory protein** . This enzyme is known as _**uridyltransferase (UT)**_ .

The _**uridyltransferase**_ is regulated
through allosteric regulation.

   - It has two main **activators** :

**alpha-ketoglutarate** (precursor
of Glutamate by transamination,
so high levels of alpha
ketoglutarate means there is

lots of Glutamate available for

the synthesis of N compounds.
We don’t know why glutamate
isn’t the regulator, and alpha
ketoglutarate is) **and ATP**
(symbolizes available energy for
synthesis).
   - UT is **inhibited** by **Glutamine**
(high glutamine levels inhibit the
synthesis, as it is an end
product. We don’t need to
produce more glutamine).

The more steps you have, the more possibilities you have to regulate the processes. The
difference between more complex organisms and simpler ones is the level of complexity in
regulation.

*[PDF p. 149]*

**Carbon skeleton sources**

**-**
**Glycolysis**

**-**
**Citric acid cycle**

**-**
**Pentose phosphate pathway**

Mammals aren’t very good at synthesizing amino acids. Most of them have to be obtained

from the diet.

**Non-essential amino acids**

   Alanine can be synthetized in the liver from pyruvate

   Aspartate can be synthesized from oxaloacetate

   Serine, synthesized from glycolytic precursors

   - Glutamate

   Asparagine

**Conditionally essential amino acids**

   Arginine, cysteine, glutamine,...

We produce them, but we need an additional source for correct functioning of the body.

**Essential amino acids**

   - Histidine

   - Leucine

   - Isoleucine

   Lysine

   - Methionine

Most amino acids are essential.

**Molecules derived from amino acids**

**Amino acids are also very important for the production of other molecules.** For
example, purine and pyrimidine nucleotides; Nitric oxide, that comes from arginine;
Histamine (inflammatory molecule) that comes from histidine; Epinephrine, that comes from
tyrosine (first converted into dopamine); Serotonin, that comes from tryptophan; many
neurotransmitters are formed by amino acid precursors,....

**Porphyrias**

Illnesses derived from **defects** in the production of **haem groups.**

*[PDF p. 150]*

**TBL 5.1. Ethanol metabolism**

Ethanol is an organic compound with the chemical formula CH3-CH2-OH. Ethanol is
naturally produced by the fermentation of sugars. It has medical applications as an antiseptic
and disinfectant. It is used as a chemical solvent, in the synthesis of organic compounds, as
an alternative fuel source, and as an ingredient in alcoholic drinks. It is used as a
recreational drug due to its psychoactive properties. According to the World Health
Organization (WHO), alcohol is responsible for more than 5 percent of deaths worldwide.

**Ethanol fermentation**

Yeast ( _Saccharomyces cerevisiae_ ) and other microorganisms ferment **glucose to ethanol**
**and CO2** . In this process, glucose is first converted to pyruvate by glycolysis. Afterwards,
pyruvate is converted to ethanol in two steps. In the first step, catalyzed by _pyruvate_
_decarboxylase_, pyruvate is decarboxylated to produce **acetaldehyde** . In a second step,
acetaldehyde is **reduced to ethanol** in a reaction catalyzed by _alcohol dehydrogenase_ . In
this reaction, NADH is oxidized to NAD+. Because yeasts perform this conversion in the
absence of oxygen, alcoholic fermentation is considered an anaerobic process. It yields 2

ATP molecules.

Ethanol fermentation has many uses,
including alcoholic beverage production
and bread dough rising.

**Ethanol metabolism in humans**

Ethanol is both lipid- and water-soluble. It is therefore readily absorbed from the intestine by
passive diffusion and enters the blood. More than **90%** of the absorbed ethanol is
**metabolized in the liver**, and only 2% to 10% is excreted through the lungs or kidneys.

The **main enzyme** responsible for ethanol metabolism in the liver is _**alcohol**_
_**dehydrogenase**_ ( **ADH** ), a **cytosolic** enzyme that catalyzes the **oxidation** of **ethanol to**
**acetaldehyde** with the concomitant reduction of **NAD+ to NADH** . Approximately 90% of the
acetaldehyde that is generated in the cytosol of hepatocytes is further metabolized to
**acetate** in the liver. The major enzyme involved in this process is mitochondrial
_**acetaldehyde dehydrogenase**_ ( **ALDH** ), which catalyzes the **oxidation of acetaldehyde to**
**acetate** with an additional generation of **NADH** .

When ethanol intake is **high**, _alcohol dehydrogenase_ activity is insufficient to process all the
acetaldehyde produced from ethanol, which can lead to its **accumulation in the liver.**

*[PDF p. 151]*

**Acetaldehyde** is a **toxic** compound believed to exert toxic actions in the liver. Acetaldehyde
can also enter the blood and mediate toxic effects in other tissues. It’s thought to be
responsible for **hangover symptoms** such as **headache or nausea.**

In contrast, **acetate** has **no toxic effects**, and can be activated to acetyl coenzyme A
(acetyl-CoA) in the liver, but mostly in other tissues:

Acetate + CoA + ATP → acetyl-CoA + AMP + PPi (catalyzed by _acetyl-CoA synthetase_ )

Acetyl-CoA can enter, for example, the citric acid cycle or the pathway for fatty acid
synthesis.

There is an **alternative route** of ethanol

oxidation in the liver. Approximately **10% to**
**20%** of ingested ethanol is **oxidized**
**through a microsomal ethanol-oxidizing**
**system** (MEOS), comprising **cytochrome**
**P450** mixed-function _**oxidases**_ (e.g.
**CYP2E1** ) in the **endoplasmic reticulum of**

**liver cells.**

**Genetic variations in the capacity to metabolize ethanol**

Each of the enzymes involved in ethanol metabolism **(ADH, ALDH and CYP2E1)** are
actually a family of several isoenzymes. Individual **genetic variations** in these isoenzymes
influence the rate of ethanol clearance from the blood, and the degree of alcohol
consumption. For example, more than **80% of acetaldehyde oxidation** in the human liver is
catalyzed by **ALDH2**, which has a **high affinity for acetaldehyde** and is highly specific.
However, individuals with a common **allelic variant** of ALDH2 (designated as **ALDH2*2** )
have a markedly **decreased capacity for acetaldehyde metabolism.**

**Alcoholism treatment**

Alcoholics are frequently treated with **ALDH inhibitors** (e.g., **disulfiram** ) to help them
**abstain from alcohol intake** due to **discomfort** caused by the **accumulation of**
**acetaldehyde** after alcohol consumption. Unfortunately, alcoholics who continue to drink
while taking this drug are exposed to the toxic effects of elevated acetaldehyde levels.

**Drug-alcohol interactions**

The **overlapping specificity in the catalytic activity of P450 enzymes** is responsible for
several cases of **drug interactions** . For example, **phenobarbital**, a barbiturate long used
as a sleeping pill or for treatment of epilepsy, is converted to an inactive metabolite by
**cytochrome P450** monooxygenases **CYP2B1 and CYP2B2** . The **risk of addiction** or
overdose is **greatly increased** when **phenobarbital** is **combined with alcohol.**

*[PDF p. 152]*

**Toxic effects of ethanol metabolism**

The **liver** is particularly susceptible to **alcohol-related injury**
because it is the primary site of alcohol metabolism. As alcohol is
broken down in the liver, a number of potentially **dangerous**
**by-products are generated**, including **acetaldehyde** and **reactive**
**oxygen species (ROS).** ROS are highly reactive free radicals
derived from the activity of MEOS and the formation of
acetaldehyde adducts. As a consequence of the accumulation of
these toxic metabolites, a large proportion of **heavy drinkers**
develop **chronic liver diseases**, such as **fatty liver, hepatitis or**

**cirrhosis.**

Ethanol ingestion also has acute **effects on liver metabolism**, including **inhibition of fatty**
**acid oxidation, stimulation** of liver **triacylglycerol synthesis**, **ketoacidosis**, **lactic**
**acidosis and hypoglycemia.**

**Prolonged alcohol abuse** also leads to a **vitamin deficiency**, because alcoholics tend to
eat less, and excessive alcohol consumption negatively impacts the **lining of the**
**gastrointestinal tract**, which can **compromise the absorption of nutrients** . One of the
most common and serious types of alcohol-related vitamin deficiency is **lack of thiamine**
**(vitamin B1). Wernicke–Korsakoff’s encephalopathy** is the most frequently encountered
manifestation of **thiamine deficiency** in alcoholics.

**CASE 1**

1. Ketogenic diet = high fats, low carb diet
2. 13 ATPs per ethanol molecule
3. 7.1 kcal/g
4. Empty calories=it has little to none nutritional value. They are calories that don’t fill
you up, although do provide energy.
5. 70-130 mg/dl or 3.6 to 5.5. mmol/L
6. A ketone body. 0.4-0.5 mmol/Lis the normal beta-hydroxybutyrate concentration
7. 0.5-1 mmol/L is the normal range of serum lactate
8. The normal range of blood pH is 7.35-7.45
9. Jane’s symptoms: she is not consuming enough carbohydrates, so she is in a state
of hypoglycemia. All the blood glucose comes from gluconeogenesis produced by the

liver.

She is in ketoacidosis due to her diet (extremely high levels of ketone bodies) and
her blood pH is too low (acidosis). All this is due to hypoglycemia. The liver is
overwhelmed by producing glucose and trying to degrade ethanol, which causes an

accumulation of acetate.

Hypoglycemia symptoms: dizziness, fast heart rate, anxiety,...

We conclude that Jane has: Hypoglycemia, metabolic acidosis (ketoacidosis and
lactic acidosis),...

*[PDF p. 153]*

Gluconeogenesis: the pyruvate to synthesize glucose comes from aminoacids,
glycerol, lactate,... Amino Acids and lactate are being used to create glucose. From
lactate to pyruvate, NADH is released. If we add ethanol to this equation, we see that
even more NADH is released in the liver. The levels of NADH are very high.
Therefore, the equilibrium will shift to reduce the levels. Instead of shifting towards
glucose, it shifts towards pyruvate, and therefore aminoacids and lactate.
Gluconeogenesis is stopped. Lactate accumulates (as shown in the test).
Also, the reduction of glucose produces the mobilization of fatty acids. They are
degraded to produce Acetyl-CoA and energy. For the Krebs cycle, we also need
oxaloacetate, but it’s depleted due to NADH accumulation. Therefore, acetyl-coA
can't go into the Krebs cycle. It is destined to the synthesis of ketone bodies (the
other fate of Acetyl-CoA). Acetyl-CoA can’t be used to produce glucose, because by

decarboxylation it’s used to
produce energy.

In conclusion: our

metabolism is affected

because of an increase in

the NADH/NAD+ ratio.

(hypoglycemia,
hyperlipidemia because of
the inhibition of fatty acid
oxidation, ketoacidosis,
lactic acidosis,...)

**CASE 2**

Eto may present a mutation in the _acetaldehyde dehydrogenase_ enzyme, which inhibits the
transformation of acetaldehyde to acetate for its degradation. Acetaldehyde begins to
accumulate in the liver and causes hangover-like symptoms, due to its toxic effects.
ALDH2*2, allelic variant of acetaldehyde dehydrogenase. Very low activity. This is known as

alcohol-flush reaction.

*[PDF p. 154]*

**TBL 5.3. Integration of metabolism**

**Division of labour of tissues**

Metabolism isn’t the same in all tissues. It’s a coordinated process in different systems.

**The Cori cycle**

Cycle established between muscle cells and liver cells. Muscles at high activity produce
lactate due to lack of oxygen, which goes into circulation as not to decrease ph in the cell.
It’s transported to the liver as it’s a precursor for glucose, and gives place to
gluconeogenesis. (Glucose to Pyruvate to Lactate to Pyruvate to Glucose again).

*[PDF p. 155]*

**Prolonged fasting situation**

Fatty acids are released from adipose
tissue and travel through the blood **bound**
**to serum albumin,** to be degraded in the
liver. Fatty acids oxidation takes place here
in the liver, and the resulting Acetyl CoA is

transformed into Ketone bodies

(hydroxybutyrate). Hydroxybutyrate is then
transported to the brain or other tissues
where it will be converted into Acetyl CoA.

**Two main fates of acetyl-CoA:**
**degradation or synthesis**
Our body makes ketone bodies because, if
we want to degrade acetyl CoA and we’re in
a hypoglycemic situation, there is a lack of
oxaloacetate as it is being destined to
glucose synthesis. Acetyl CoA can’t enter
the Krebs cycle, so it’s “stored” as Ketone

bodies and travels to the tissues where

energy is needed.
This process occurs also in diabetics, as
their body doesn’t detect the presence of glucose in the blood.
Prolonged fasting or untreated diabetes therefore lead to ketoacidosis.

*[PDF p. 156]*

**Lecture 6.1. The clinical laboratory**

**1st step:** doctor meets patient. Take **clinical history and general examination.**

**Use of biochemical tests**

Biochemical tests are used in diagnosis, monitoring, screening and prognosis.

**Diagnosis**

**- Homeostasis assessment:** to check that there is a balance and everything is in normal
range values. Athletes, occupational medicine, primary care,... undergo many of these tests.

**- Screening:** Screening is run to detect a disease with no symptoms yet (lack of symptoms
doesn’t allow diagnosing the disease). This can be achieved by screening.
For example, Prostate Specific Antigen (PSA) is present in the blood when a prostate cancer
arises. Males over a certain age are recommended to take this test and check values, so
there is an early detection and proper treatment.
Also in colon cancer: feces are analyzed, and if there’s blood, a patient undergoes a
colonoscopy to confirm colon cancer or other pathologies.
Babies also undergo screening tests to check for different types of hormones and correct
amounts (ex: congenital hypothyroidism).

**- Prognosis:** some tests can be used to establish a prognosis. For some tests, higher levels
indicate presence of a disease, and in other cases, lower levels indicate it.

**- Treatment:** we can perform a test to choose a treatment.
For example, in the case of osteoporosis. There has to be a balance between bone
synthesis and bone degradation. There can be a decrease in bone synthesis or an increase
in bone degradation, and there are medicines to treat each situation.

**- Monitor progression of a disease**

**Clinical laboratories**

Laboratory facilities: there is a specimen collection area. The core facility goes on for 24h (in
case of emergency). There are also specialised areas inside the lab.
Operational models: organized by tests or by specialties; public, private or partnerships; ….

*[PDF p. 157]*

**Purpose of the clinical laboratory**

It’s a facility in a medical centre where human samples are analysed to obtain **reliable and**
**reproducible** analytical **results** that provide the clinician an **aid** in screening, diagnosing or
monitoring the patient.
In order to get reliable, reproducible results, we need to make sure all processes are highly
monitored and controlled: preanalytical conditions, analytical and post-analytical phase.
**Errors in the analytical process**

Errors generally occur in the **post-analytical**
**phase (15-25%)** and the **pre-analytical**
**phase (70%),** because many people take
part in the preanalytical phase and it’s difficult

to control.

Once the sample is correctly ordered and
taken, the sample needs to be labelled and
identified. To avoid errors, ask active

questions and make sure the sample has at
least two unique identifiers.

*[PDF p. 158]*

**Lecture 6.2. Preanalytical phase**

To prevent errors in the different stages of analysis, **patient identification** is carried out by
asking **active questions** and obtaining **at least two unique identifiers.**

**Factors which can cause variation**

   - **Interferences:** an interference is a substance present in the sample, **different** to the
analyte intended to be measured, which alters the measured result.

   **Biological variations** are abnormal changes in the concentration of substances
which **ought to be present in the sample,** although in another range of values
_(example: glucose, lipids,... if in excess, due to hyperglycemia or hyperlipidemia, alter_
_the analysis)_

**Sources of preanalytical variation**

**1. Non modifiable biological variation**

**Sex, age, race, pregnancy,...** This info is necessary in order to determine normal range
values (ex: levels of alkaline-phosphatase. Differs between ages). Black race usually has
higher levels of PSA; that doesn’t mean they are suffering from cancer.

**2. Modifiable biological variation**

**a)** **Food intake:** how does this affect test results?

**Enhanced analytes:** for example, glucose: if we ate glucose rich foods, this increases
glucose levels in the blood. Same thing happens with lipids. This does not mean the patient
has hyperglycemia or hyperlipidemia.

**Metabolic changes** : after food intakes, the **hormone percentage** also changes, such as
glucagon and insulin. A diet high in proteins causes increased urea and uric acid.

**- Lipemia interferences** : they alter blood samples:

   - There are problems with **turbidimetric** and **spectrophotometric methods.** The
serum appears more dense, losing its cloudy-transparent consistency.
   - Also, lipemic samples interfere with **aqueous components,** and there is a
discrepancy in the results.
   - Lastly, lipemic samples **decrease the access to antibodies** during **immunoassays**
and there is less binding to the antibodies **,** giving false negative results.

**b)** **Stress** may also interfere with the test results _(ex: huge peak of cortisol and_

_adrenaline)._ Panic attacks during the tests. The alternative is a saliva test performed

at home.

**c)** **Physical exercise** also interferes with normal values. CK values are acute after

physical exercise.

**d)** **Drug history:** antibiotics, chronic disease drugs,...

*[PDF p. 159]*

**3. Blood withdrawal**

**Patient’s position:** this affects the obtained values. It’s generally
recommended for the patient to sit for a few minutes before taking it.

**Application of the tourniquet** : if it’s left for too long, there start to
be **metabolic changes** after **longer than 5 minutes.** There’s a flux
of water, low diffusible ions and low molecular weight substances out
of the blood, producing an **increased concentration of high**
**molecular mass compounds.**

The physical characteristics of the sample also affect it:

**- Hemolysis of erythrocytes:** if they break because the sample isn’t properly drawn, the
sample becomes reddish and there’s a release of everything that was inside the
erythrocytes, most importantly **potassium and** _**lactate dehydrogenase**_ **.** We get abnormal

values of these two substances.

**- Lipemia:** we get false results for **sodium (Na+).**

**- Presence of fibrin:** it interferes with the aspirated volume, which means there will be less
reaction and therefore a **false negative result.**
There is a machine that measures all these variants, to give an idea of the quality of the
sample. If it’s not viable, another sample has to be performed.

**Patient preparation**

**-** **Fasting state:** 12h after the last meal

**-**
**Morning collection** (between 7 and 9, due to circadian rhythm and the hormone
levels throughout the day)

**-**
**No confounding factors** (Avoid performing heavy exercise, shift work, jet lag and
changes in time zone… Patients need to be in a state that represents their
physiology).

There may also be special preparations and specific conditions.
All samples have to be considered hazardous/potentially infectious. They may contain viral
infections. There are special containers to dispose of needles and such.

**Traceability**

Lab samples have a **bar code** which contains all the info for tracing he sample and knowing
who the patient is, age, sex, who ordered the test, who performed the test and who validated
it, if there were any incidents, in which analyzor it was processed,.. Everything can be traced
back and the sample can also be easily found in the lab.

*[PDF p. 160]*

**Lectures 6.3 and 6.4. Measurement techniques and results**

**Methods**

There are different types of methods which differ in complexity, sample volume, equipment,
cost, analytical performance,... specialists need to choose the more adequate method for the
hospital.
In the lab, there are manual techniques (HPLC) and automated analyzers. Maintenance is
important for the analyzers. Everyday it needs to be **calibrated** (establish a reference) and a
**quality control** has to be run, to ensure the results are **reliable** .

**POINT OF CARE TESTING**

POC tests are tests designed to be used at or **near the site** where the patient is located.
They do not require a permanent dedicated test and can be performed out of the physical

facilities of the clinical laboratories.

**POC vs Central laboratory**

There are advantages and disadvantages:

**-**
**Advantages:**

- POC gives out **faster results**, and there is a shorter preanalytical phase. It allows us to
make decisions about the patient’s health earlier. Ex: on helicopters, there is POC to
measure for example troponin. High levels of troponin released by cardiomyocytes = heart

attack.

- POC are **simpler to perform.**

- POC are **easily portable.** Lots of evidence that POC improves patient care and outcomes,
as it shortens hospitalization time and allows us to make decisions quickly.

- POC has been proven to have a positive effect on patient outcomes

   **Disadvantages:**

- Increased **cost**

- Depending on the technique, it needs more or less **calibration** and there can be a lack of
experience from personnel in these standardized tests.

- Standard **protocols aren’t rigurously established** (no daily quality control), so there may

be tests with **false results.**

**Urinalysis**

- Can be performed at home or in the lab

- Dip the strip in urine and compare colors to the reference ones on the bottle.

- Measures: pH, glucose, total amount of proteins, ketones, bilirubin,
urobilinogen, blood (presence of erythrocytes), leukocytes and nitrates.

- Gives a good general idea of the hydrolytic balance and infections

- Reflects not only the state of the kidneys but also of the blood, etc

*[PDF p. 161]*

**Urine drug test**

- Urine is put in the sample well and reacts with the established antigens.

- There is a **control band** (which appears when the sample has migrated all the way to the
top and has reacted) **detection bands** (measure cocaine, morphine,...). The result is
considered positive when there is no detection

band.

- The sample has to be taken in person, and in a
place with **no water access** so the patient cannot
dilute the urine. Also, an adulteration test is

carried out simultaneously to make sure that the
composition of the fluid is compatible with that of
urine. It measures pH, density and creatinine (a
molecule that is filtered through the kidneys and
appears in urine in very small amounts).

**Blood gas analysis**

- Measures PO2, PCO2, pH,..

- Also measures hemoglobin (cooximetry)

- Metabolites, glucose, lactates,..

- Ions such as sodium, potassium, Cl-,...

- Bilirubin

- Creatinine

-Generally used only less than 200 microliters.
-Usually located in intensive care units, operation rooms,...

**Fast tests (such as Sars-Cov2 antibodies test)**

- Sample taken and deposited in the indicated sample well

- A buffer solution is added, to allow the reaction to take place

- For **positive results,** we observe both the control and detection band; in **negative results,**
there is no detection band; the result won’t be valid if the absence of the control band
indicates the sample hasn’t fully migrated to the top as it was insufficient.

**Pregnancy tests**

Pregnancy tests target a specific component in the urine. Always search for the control

band.

The method behind POC testing is **immunochromatography:**

**antibodies** are used for the detection. There is a control

antibody (control band, which symbolizes that
the sample has migrated to the top), detection antibody and
labelled antibody.

*[PDF p. 162]*

**Whole blood vs plasma**

**TURNAROUND TIME**

Whole blood contains both red blood cells and the plasma
liquid portion. **Whole blood is 83% water.**
Therefore, when measuring the amount of glucose, it isn’t
exactly the same value as the one we get from measuring
glucose from plasma. In plasma, it’s **10% more**

**concentrated** than in whole blood.

Usual amount of time between the time a **specimen** is **obtained** and the test **results** are
**reported** . It’s a parameter used for **quality control** . There is a turnaround time established
for every parameter. Ex: troponin = less than 30 minutes; cholesterol = 3 hours; depending
on the urgency.

**Calibration of techniques**

**Calibration** is the relationship between the instrument signal and the concentration of an
analyte. Manufacturers provide calibrators. Calibration curves in automated systems are
usually very stable. It’s important to calibrate with certified **reference materials.**

**Precision and accuracy**

In the lab we work to obtain the true value of the sample. But we don’t always get the same
result in the same sample, there are usually **imprecisions** .

   - When measuring **precision**, we get **spread values** called **random errors.**
   - There are also **systematic errors** when measuring **veracity:** sometimes we get
similar results, but they are **far from the target values.** The veracity is the distance
between the target value and the results.

   Lastly, there are true results, which are both precise and accurate.

For each parameter we measure the **precision** and
**veracity**

**Quality assurance**

   - To detect **random errors**, we perform an **internal quality control:** a solution with a
**known** concentration is used and run everyday, twice a day.
   - To detect **systematic errors**, we use **external quality control:** a bottle with an
**unknown** solution is measured. You report the result and then compare with the
reported results from other laboratories.

*[PDF p. 163]*

**Analytical sensitivity and analytical specificity**

   **Sensitivity: how little of the analyte the method can detect.** For example,
pregnancy tests in a pharmacy have a higher limit than in labs. There are different
sensitivities. In hospitals, they cannot risk a false negative, hence the higher
sensitivity. This sensitivity depends on the methods.

   - **Specificity** : **how close the measured value is to the actual, true value** .

**Analytical interference:** a substance present in the system that causes a **deviation** in the
measured value compared with the true value.
This interference can be **endogen** (Hemolysis, lipemia and icteria, proteins…) **or exogen**
