(like drugs, which interfere with the system, or carry-over, if the pipet is used many times and
samples are mixed from one patient to the next).
We may suspect analytical interference when:

- the values don’t seem to correspond with the signs and symptoms the patients show.

- Also, when there is a disagreement between two variables which are physiologically
dependent.

- When there are unlikely/unbelievable results

- When there are important sudden changes in the pattern of a patient’s health

**Interferences:**

- Classics: hemoglobin, bilirubin, lipemia,...

- Hydroxyurea and urea

- Immunotherapy with the immunofixation

- Anti-insulin antibodies: if the patient has them, the reagent cannot reach the reactant

**Types of results**

- Qualitative (+/-)

- Semiquantitative

- Quantitative (numbers and units)

*[PDF p. 164]*

**Lecture 6.5 and 6.6. Interpretation of clinical results I and II**

There are reference values/intervals for each test. These **reference intervals** are a range of
the results generated in a **reference population.** Usually the population consists of
**apparently healthy individuals.** To prove that the population is apparently healthy, we have
to do further tests (TAC,...). A reference interval/range is not a “normal” range, but a
reference to help in the interpretation of the result.
This reference range depends on the population (ex: vitamin D levels aren’t the same in
Sweden than in Spain, because we have more sun exposure), race, sex, age,... but also on
the method (technique employed) and the sample (not the same to employ serum or
plasma)

There are 2 ways to **generate reference intervals** : take our own reference values (each lab
generates their own) or take one already generated. For generating them, we have to
employ the 3 phases.

   **Preanalytical phase:** select a correct reference population. Next, choose a number
of individuals that are representative of that population (100 or more participants).
You then have to standardize the preanalytical phase: perform all the tests in the
same conditions, and give the patients the same guidelines, to minimize preanalytical

errors.

   **Analytical phase:** choose the method and the type of sample.

   **Postanalytical phase:** it consists in the processing of the data. By using the
Gaussian curve, we get the interval, a range which includes 95% of the values
obtained. The lower limits (2.5%) and higher limits (2.5%) are excluded.
It is normal that 5% of people have levels above or below the normal reference

range.

Generating reference values is quite expensive, so usually what is done is a **transfer of**
**reference values,** by using reference values from an external source.
For it, you have to validate that reference you’re taking in 20 individuals, to ensure that the
population, preanalytical phase, sample type,.. are similar.

Not only reference ranges aid in interpreting results, but also **clinical decision values.**
In some parameters we don’t employ the reference interval. For example, for **cholesterol**
we employ the limits that have been clinically validated to prevent **cardiovascular disease** .
In some parameters, this is more useful. In **glycated hemoglobin**, this parameter is very
high in **diabetic** patients. It’s more useful in some cases to validate if these values are
conferring a health risk. Or when a cardiologist orders **troponin** analysis, he is looking for
the probability of **heart attack** . A limit is used above which heart attack has a high
probability.
For clinical decision values, they are **more general** values, reached by a **consensus** .

Lastly, we find **critical values.** It reflects a physiopathological state which can **threaten** a
patient’s life unless something is done promptly. They are **extreme values.**

*[PDF p. 165]*

To deal with critical values:

   - 1st, **definition** : we have to choose the parameter from which we establish that there
is a critical situation. This is done together with the clinicians.

   2nd, a **detection system** for critical values (computer)

   3rd, **notify** the ordering physician that there is an alarming result. It has to be done
following a protocol. The doctor has to repeat name of the patient, number, units and
name of the test (as safety protocol of communication). If the ordering physician is
not available, we call a nurse.

   4th, **register** that the protocol has been followed.
   - 5th: at the end of the year, the limits and values are **revalidated**, so as to not cause
too many unnecessary critical value situations.

For the **interpretation of serial results,** we have the reference value for the change.
The **reference change value:** It’s the difference in the concentration required to have a
clinical significance, taking into account the variation that we always have when we perform
a test. We compare results between two digits obtained from the same patient. The total
variation is the composive (raíz cuadrada) of the variation of prea. phase + v. in analytical
phase + biological variation.
The RCV is established by the lab. For a change in a test to be clinically significant, it has to
be higher than RCV.

**Interpretation of test results. Specificity and sensitivity**

In an ideal situation, test results

completely separate the diseased
population from the healthy ones.

But most of the time in clinical

practice, the diseased population
overlaps with the healthy one.
Therefore we can get **false**
**positives** and **false negatives.**

In order to determine if a test is

positive or negative, we have to determine the **specificity and sensitivity** of the procedure.

   **Sensitivity:** percentage of sick people which are corrected classified by the test.
Desired sensitivity: more than 80%

   **Specificity:** percentage of healthy people which are corrected classified by the test.

   **Diagnostic efficiency:** percentage of correct results

*[PDF p. 166]*

**Cut-off**

We **need to establish a cut-off** to determine the limit from which we consider a test result

as positive or negative. The cut-off can be moved to increase either specificity or sensitivity,
attending to different criteria:

   - **High sensitivity** is used to determine the **maximum number of sick people,** while
risking having false positives (FP). This sort of cut-off is used in **neonatal screening**
(we don’t want to risk a false negative, while a false positive wouldn’t be such a
problem) or in **serious treatable disease.**
There will be nearly no false negatives, but there will be a larger number of false
positives that will undergo more tests to confirm if they have the disease.
   - **High specificity** is used to detect **the maximum number of healthy people**,
therefore risking false negatives. It’s used in the case of **serious untreatable**
**diseases** (diagnosing it won’t change the course of the illness, if there is one, so it’s
less of a risk) and **treatments with high morbidity and no high efficacy** (we don’t
want to misdiagnose a disease for which the course of treatment could be lethal).

**The development of ROC**

**Receiver operating characteristics** are curves designed to **select the best cut-off**, that is,
the one that gives the highest sensitivity with the highest specificity.
It serves to **compare** types of tests (the one with the higher area under the curve is better),
and gives an idea about the **discriminating power** of the test.

**Predictive values**

**Predictive values** are used to interpret the patient’s results. They help calculate the
probability of someone having or not having a certain disease. There are positive PV ( **PPV** )
and negative PV ( **NPV** ).

*[PDF p. 167]*

**Lecture 6.7 and 6.8. Clinical enzymology I and II**

For screening purposes, we usually measure **circulating enzymes** in the plasma. Some
enzymes are present in the blood as a result of the turnover/remodeling of a tissue. We also
have enzymes as a result of tissue damage. It’s useful to identify the **damaged organ.**

**Plasma enzymes**

Normally, after catalyzing, the enzymes are deactivated and degraded.

   - **Necrosis** : during necrosis, the enzymes inside
the cytoplasm appear in circulation.
   - **Induction** : certain drugs (alcohol) increase GGT,
an increase in cell production of this enzyme, so
there is more segregation.
   - **Obstruction** : the enzyme cannot be released to
be secreted, so it goes to the blood.

   **Cell-proliferation** : if there are more proliferating
cells, there are more enzymes being secreted.

**-**
**Decreased degradation**

When there is an increase in an enzyme, we have to identify from which tissue it’s being
released and why it's being secreted at higher levels.

**Factors that determine the amount of enzyme in circulation**

**1.** **The rate of release of enzyme into bloodstream**

**After cell damage:**

   - The enzymes are found in **different locations** . Cytoplasmic
enzymes are more easily released than mitochondrial
enzymes. Therefore after cell damage the first enzymes to
appear are those from the cytoplasm. Also, membrane
enzymes appear in the blood before cytoplasmic enzymes.
Lastly, those from the mitochondria.
   - The **molecular weight** of an enzyme also determines the rate of release. High
molecular weight enzymes take longer to appear.
   - The **extent of the damage**, if low, results in a small enzyme release. If there is huge
tissue damage, the enzyme concentration in blood is much more elevated.
Finding high molecular weight enzymes implies that there has been prolonged cell damage.

**No cell damage:**

   Degree of induction
   - The **rate of cell proliferation** will determine enzyme levels.

*[PDF p. 168]*

**2.** **Plasma clearance rate**

The **type of dysfunction** in the clearance organ will determine which enzymes will appear in

increased levels.

**Isoenzymes**

Isoenzymes are different enzymes that present the same activity.

_-_
_Amylase_

_-_ _Creatine kinase_

_-_
_Alkaline phosphatase_

_-_
_Lactate dehydrogenase_

Each **isoenzyme** is **organ specific.** When a specific enzyme concentration is increased, we
can measure the individual isoenzymes to determine the place of damage.

**Determination of enzyme activity**

**A)** **Preanalytical factors**

Enzyme concentration highly depends on age, sex, physical exercise, time of sample
collection,...

**-**
**Age**
_Alkaline phosphatase_ is present in the liver, placenta,.. so children have a natural increase of
this enzyme during growth. It’s expression depends on age, so we need to report **specific**
**reference ranges by age.**

**-** **Gender**

Values vary between men and women. So **specific reference ranges** are set **for each sex**
independently **.**

**-**
**Physical exercise**
_Creatine kinase_ is an enzyme located in the muscle, and can be released after physical
education. That’s why it's not advisable to do physical exercise before a blood test.

**-**
**Time of sampling**
It’s a good idea to ask when the pain started or when the accident occured, because some
enzymes may take longer to appear on the screening due to the rate of release.

**B)** **Analytical phase**

When we measure the activity of an enzyme in blood, it depends on enzyme concentration,
substrate concentration, incubation temperature, pH, amount of cofactors or inhibitors,....

*[PDF p. 169]*

Analysis is a very standardized process, that way, when we measure the activity, this only
depends on the amount of enzyme present in the plasma. It’s all done at a specific
temperature which has to be reported on the file.

**Measurement units:**

   **Catalytic activity**
There are 2 units: the katal (moles of substrate transformed per second) and International
Units ( **IU** ), (micromoles transformed per unit).

**-**
**Enzyme concentration**
2 units: katal per Litre (katal/L) or International Unit per Litre (I **U/L** )

**C)** **Post-Analytical phase and interpretation**

There are useful tools to aid in this:

**1.** **Performing serial enzymes measurements**
If values decrease towards homeostasis, it gives an idea about the patient’s normal levels
and if these are increasing or decreasing.

**2.** **Performing isoenzymes analysis**
It helps to identify the **site** where the enzyme is being released in higher levels, and if these
levels are to be expected from that tissue in that specific patient.

**3.** **Measuring more than one enzyme**
Enzymes are not always tissue specific and are released at different rates during pathology.
So it's common to order **panels** (ex: lipid panel) where you **analyze several enzymes.** If
they are all increased, you can suspect that the origin of the damage comes from that organ.

**ENZYMES AND TISSUES**

**LIVER**

For liver analysis we have **five measurable enzymes** . None of them is specific
from the liver, they are located in **different parts of hepatocytes** and they have
**different half lifes** . We need these 5 enzymes to explore liver function. The most
commonly used are _aminotransferases / transaminases_, which transfer amino
groups from alpha-ketoglutarate, with TPP cofactor.

_**-**_ _**Aminotransferases**_

**- AST** (aspartate + ketoglutarate = glutamate + oxaloacetate)
     - **ALT** (pyruvate + glutamate = alanine + ketoglutarate)

**AST (** _**aspartate transaminase**_ **)**, in addition to the liver, is also **found in**
**erythrocytes,** so when a sample is **hemolyzed**, we cannot consider AST

to assess liver function.

*[PDF p. 170]*

**AST** is located in cytoplasm and mitochondria. **ALT (** _**alanine transaminase**_ **)** only in
cytoplasm. So mitochondrial AST will take longer to be released in case of damage.
Therefore, AST (17-87 h) has a higher half-life than ALT.

_**-**_
_**Gamma-glutamyltransferase**_
In the liver, we also find _**gamma-glutamyltransferase.**_ It’s located in the **membrane that**
**faces the bile conducts** . **There will be different enzyme increases regarding cell lysis**
**or bile flow.** That’s why we measure the different enzymes that are located in different areas

of the liver.

A decrease in bile flow causes an increase in _gamma-GT._ A rise in
_gamma-GT_ in serum is indicative of cholestasis, alcohol intake
and drugs.
If we have **viral hepatitis**, the virus enters the cell, and we will
find increased _**transaminases**_ . If there is a stone **blocking bile**
**flow**, there is a huge increase in _**gamma-GT**_ and little increase in

transaminases.

_**-**_
_**Alkaline-phosphatase**_
It’s a phosphatase that requires a basic pH for its activity. It contains membrane
**metallometals** as **cofactors** for its function (therefore, it can’t be measured in
EDTA tubes).
It is located in **sinusoids and bile canaliculi, bone, intestine and placenta.**
There are several isoenzymes of this enzyme. It increases during an increase in
**osteoblastic activity** (which may happen due to **fractures**, **Vit D deficiency**
which causes impairment of bone synthesis and degradation,...).
It’s also related to **liver damage:** it appears quite quickly in circulation, during
**cholestasis** or **cell damage.**

_**-**_
_**Lactate-dehydrogenase (LDH)**_
It’s a big enzyme made up of **4 units (high molecular weight**, takes **longer to appear in**
**circulation)** . Its function is to catalyze the reaction from **lactate to pyruvate.**
An **increase of LDH** in blood means that there is **cell lysis** somewhere in the body, but it’s
not tissue specific, so we need to measure the different isoenzymes to identify the damaged

tissue.

The screening of these enzymes gives us either **bile tree information** (in relation to bile
flow) or **integrity information** (cell damage).

*[PDF p. 171]*

**-**
**Liver-related pathologies**

**a)** **HEPATITIS**
Mainly _**transaminases**_ are released during hepatitis, an inflammation caused by viral
infection or ingestion of toxic substances (drugs, mushrooms,...)
In these cases, the ALT and AST are ten times higher than the reference values due to liver
damage (should be lower than 30, is found in levels of 300-3000 or higher)

**b)** **LIVER CIRRHOSIS**
It is a chronic condition, in which there is irreversible

liver damage.

**c)** **HEPATOCARCINOMA**

**d)** **CHOLESTASIS**

Occurs when the **flow of the bile** is **slow** or even

**stops.** There is an obstruction and the liver enzymes
are released and appear in the circulation.

**SKELETAL MUSCLE**

We measure **creatinine kinase** . It’s higher in men than women, and in people with
increased physical activity. It’s a dimer with **2 different subunits (M and B).** The
combination of these dimers gives:

   - CK1 or **CK-BB**

   - CK2 or **CK-MB**

   - CK-3 or **CK-MM**

In the brain, 100% is CK-BB.

In the muscle, 97% CK-MM and 3% CK-MB.

In the heart, 78% CK-MM and 22% CK-MB.

Total CK doesn't tell us much. We have to analyze the **specific isoenzymes** to locate the
**altered organ /site.**

**-** **Severe skeletal muscle lesions**

We find increases in CK when there are severe skeletal muscle lesions, **increase in**
**isoform MM / CK-3** (trauma, car accident, dystrophy,...). **Not** present in **neurological**

**muscular diseases.**

   **Cardiac damage**
Cardiomyocytes break and lead to heart attack, releasing CK (specifically CK-2).

**-**
**After intense physical exercise**
We will mainly find CK-3/CK-MM present in the bloodstream.

*[PDF p. 172]*

**PANCREAS**

We employ 2 enzymes: _amylase_ and _lipase_ .

_**-**_
_**Amylase**_
It hydrolyzes alpha-1,4-glycosidic bonds. It’s a **metalloenzyme** (requires metals for its
activity, can’t be measured in EDTA). It has **low molecular weight** so it appears quite
quickly into the system. This also allows it to **filter** through the kidney and into the **urine** . But
also, it has a very **short half life**, so it doesn’t remain in the blood for long.
It has 2 isoenzymes: **pancreatic and salivary**
**isoenzymes,** so total amylase can come from the
saliva or pancreas.
We see an increase in _amylase_ during **acute**
**pancreatitis** (6-8 h after the initiation of the
damage). Levels increase 3 times over reference

values. BUT the amount doesn’t correlate with the

severity of the damage. It remains in circulation for
a maximum of 3-5 days.

   _**Lipase**_
The main source of this enzyme is the **pancreas** .
It’s a more tissue specific enzyme than _amylase_ . But it **takes around 24h to appear** in
circulation. We have to simultaneously measure amylase and lipase. Lipase has a **longer**
**half life** (7-14 days). It’s a **late marker** but **more specific of pancreatic dysfunction.**

**ACUTE MYOCARDIAL INFARCTION**

Risk factors lead to atherogenesis and **thrombus** formation. If it’s located in
the heart ( **coronary artery** ), it blocks blood flow to the heart and this tissue

won’t receive nutrients and O2. The **tissue dies** and all its **contents** are

**released into the circulation** .

When a cardiomyocyte doesn’t receive glucose and oxygen, the membrane breaks and the
content is released. Cardiomyocytes contain **sarcomeres** . When there is damage, **troponin**
**I, troponin C and troponin T are released.** These troponins are located in all muscle fibers,
but **T and I** have **specific cardiac isoenzymes. “cTnT” and “cTnI”** are measured to detect
the death of cardiomyocytes.
The ability to measure these isoenzymes has allowed to change the definition of myocardial
infarction: cardiologists define **myocardial infarction** depending on **if there has been a**
**troponin release** (elevated cardiac troponin values, either I or T isoform). In order to
diagnose myocardial infarction, we need to measure specific troponins, and a rise or a fall
indicate myocardial injury. It is considered if the results differ from the 99 percentile.

*[PDF p. 173]*

**TBL 6.1. Specimen collection and processing**

There are different types of blood collection tubes

   - The **red, orange and yellow** capped tubes are used to obtain serum and perform
immunological studies, etc.
   - **Green** capped tubes contain **Heparin**, an anticoagulant which prevents clotting by
binding Thrombin (factor required for the coagulation cascade)
   - **Light blue** caps correspond to **Sodium Citrate** tubes, which are used to assess
coagulation (PT, APTT studies,...).

   **Gray** capped tubes usually contain **Sodium Fluoride (NaF)**, which inhibits glycolysis
and allows us to perform **glucose tests** and assess blood sugar.
   - **Purple** capped tubes contain **EDTA**, which prevents clotting by binding Ca2+
(another factor in the coagulation cascade).

*[PDF p. 174]*

**TBL 6.3. Lipid analysis**

**Why should a lipid analysis be done?**

- It is good for general health examination

- Allows to identify dislipidemias, lipid disorders

- Helps in cardiovascular risk assessment. Dislipidemias tend to elevate this risk of

cardiovascular diseases.

**Preanalytical factors**

We have to consider a series of factors before assessing plasma lipids

   - **Diet** : it can lead to an increased concentration of triglycerides in the sample. But it
does not influence cholesterol. Anyway, we need to know tryglyceride concentration
to calculate LDL concentration. The analysis has to be done during fasting (12h) or
we can get false positives.
   - **Exercise** : exercise increases HDL cholesterol levels, simply with moderate exercise.
It also decreases LDL and triglyceride concentration. So when you go for a lipid
analysis, keep your normal habits. It has to be representative of your homeostatic

state.

   - **Alcohol** : moderate alcohol intake increases HDL, but long-term intake and in
excessive amounts leads to a big increase in triglyceride levels.

   **Pregnancy** : during pregnancy there is an increase in cholesterol levels, so if you
suspect you may have familial hypercholesterolemia, don’t measure it during
pregnancy or you’ll get a false positive.
   - **Diseases** such as diabetes, hyperthyroidism, kidney failure,... can also alter the

results.

   **Drugs** : can also modify lipid levels (immunosuppressive agents, steroids,...)

Therefore, the recommendations for the patient are:

   Always take the test during **fasting** (12 h no food intake)
   - Have a **consistent diet and exercise** routine during 2-3 weeks before sampling. The
sample has to be representative of your system.
   - After overcoming **disease** or **treatment**, wait for 2 weeks to have your lipid analysis.

**Visual inspection of the sample**

A sample rich in triglycerides is seen as cloudy
and milky. When we let it sit for a day at 4
degrees, chylomicrons flow to the top due to
differences in density and form a whitish top
layer. This is indicative of lipid presence in the
sample. It should be seen as transparent.

*[PDF p. 175]*

**Biochemical tests**

**1.** **Lipid profile**

   **Triglycerides:** increased triglycerides symbol a higher

cardiovascular risk that can lead to **atherosclerosis** .

   - **Total cholesterol:** total amount of cholesterol (HDL,
LDL,...) circulating in blood. Increased cholesterol over
the years, increases **atherogenesis** .
   - **HDL-cholesterol:** HDL lipoproteins are the ones in
charge of **collecting excess cholesterol** and
transporting it to the liver, so normal or high levels of HDL indicates
**cardioprotection** .
   - **LDL-cholesterol:** it’s the **“bad” cholesterol** carrier, as it binds to the receptors in
tissues and forms cholesterol plaques. High LDL levels increase **atherogenesis** .
LDL cholesterol levels aren’t measured, but calculated through a formula, the
**Friedewald equation:**

**LDLchol = Total chol - HDL chol - VLDL**

**or also**

**LDL chol = Total chol - HDL chol - (triglycerides / 5)**

This formula has a series of **limitations** : It’s only valid when triglyceride values are
under 400 mg/dL. For triglycerides between 200-400, it underestimates the real LDL
amount. Also, to calculate LDL, you are employing the variability of 3 other values, so
you are accumulating methodological errors because each analysis has a coefficient

of variation.

**Alternatives** to this formula:

        - We can use **enzymatic methods** to directly measure LDL-cholesterol,
although not many labs do this.

        Another option is, **if triglycerides are over 200**, directly **report non HDL**
cholesterol. This is useful for cardiovascular risk assessment, as non HDL

cholesterol correlates very well with cardiovascular disease risks.

There are lipoprotein ratios that give an idea about lipoprotein balance. They give a
better understanding of cardiovascular risk.

        - HDL / TC

        - LDL / TC

*[PDF p. 176]*

**Table of Recommended Clinical Decision Limits**

**2.** **Apolipoprotein measurement**

Clinical decision limits = limits at

which there is a level of

cardiovascular risk.

**-** **Apolipoprotein APO AI:** alternative for **HDL measurement.**
   - **Apolipoprotein B100:** gives an idea on **atherogenic lipoproteins** and is used for
cardiovascular risk assessment. **No fasting** is required and technically, it’s **superior**
**to calculating LDL or non-HDL cholesterol.** It’s more accurate. In obese or
diabetic patients, it’s more useful to measure these apoproteins than to measure LDL

or non-HDL cholesterol.

On the other hand, it’s more expensive.

**3.** **Other lipoproteins. Lipoprotein A**

It’s an **atherogenic lipoprotein** because its structure is very **similar** to **LDL structure** and
**contains B100 apoprotein.** Also, its structure is **similar** to **plasminogen** structure, a
protein involved in the formation of trombos.
High levels of lipoprotein A confere cardiovascular risk.

It’s measured in patients with family history of **early cardiovascular events** or patients with
**familial hypercholesterolemia** . Also, in patients with **impaired kidney function** : they don’t
degrade lipoprotein A, so it has a longer half life and increases its effects.

**Classification of dyslipidemias**

**-**
**Hyper or hipolipidemia**

**-**
**Elevated lipid:** triglycerides, cholesterol or both (mixed)

**-**
**Etiology: primary or secondary dyslipidemia**
**1. Primary:** come from **genetic diseases/disorders**, generally **polygenic** disorders.
**2. Secondary:** external factors that cause altered lipid levels. It is important to
identify primary or secondary causes, as it helps assess the disorder. Simply
correcting habits and secondary causes can treat dyslipidemia.

**Types of secondary lipidemia**

        Diabetes mellitus: increased triglyceride

        - Alcohol excess

        Obesity

*[PDF p. 177]*

- Chronic renal failure

        Hypothyroidism

**General strategy for diagnosis of dyslipidemia**

1. Do a fasting lipid profile. Confirm results 2-3 weeks later and evaluate clinical history

for cardiovascular risk assessment

2. Evaluate possible secondary causes of dyslipidemia, such as ……..
3. Once discarded, confirm primary dyslipidemia

**Diagnosis of the Metabolic syndrome**

If the patient has at least 3 of the 5 criteria,

we count them as cardiovascular risk

factors. The limits in other measurements

are shifted due to these factors.

**Atherosclerosis**

**ATHEROSCLEROSIS** is produced by circulating lipids that deposit and form an atheroma
plaque, which also contains inflammatory cells and vascular cells, and it grows when you
expose yourself to cardiovascular risk factors (smoking, obesity,...). In atherogenesis, at the
beginning, there are no symptoms. But if you continue to expose yourself to risk factors, the
atheroma plaque becomes more active and bigger and can result in strokes, ischemic
cardiopathy,...

*[PDF p. 178]*

**Cardiovascular risk factors**

   Non-modifiable: age, sex, genetic predetermination,...

   Modifiable: hypertension, diabetes mellitus, smoking, diet,...

For cardiovascular risk assessment we employ **markers**, a molecule we analyze to evaluate
the risk. There are classic markers (cholesterol, lipoprotein A,...) and new markers that have
to be analyzed in a specific way.

**Treatment of dyslipidemias**

Usually, the patient introduces **therapeutic life changes** (better diet and increased
exercise). If this doesn’t modify the levels to the required values, the patient starts a
treatment with **statins** and other drugs.

When assessing the change in values, we have to look at the **reference change value** that
has been established, to evaluate if there has been a **significant change.**

*[PDF p. 179]*

**TBL 6.4. Laboratory analysis of glucose metabolism. Diabetes mellitus**

**Insulin synthesis**

**Insulin** is a hypoglycemic hormone: it
reduces glucose levels in the blood.
**Preproinsulin** is synthesized in the ER,
containing the signalling peptide.
Once the sp is lost, we are left with
**proinsulin**, formed by insulin and the C
peptide.
Lastly, the formation of **insulin** occurs
through peptidases, which separate insulin
from the C peptide in the Golgi apparatus.

When we want to explore a patient’s insulin synthesis, we can either **measure insulin or C**
**peptide.** One molecule of proinsulin gives one molecule of insulin and another of C peptide,
so they are found in the same amounts. But C peptide has a longer half life, so it can be
easier to measure. Also, when a patient receives insulin as a diabetes treatment, we
differentiate endogenous and exogenous insulin, so insulin can’t be directly measured.
Insulin synthesis rate has to be measured through monitoring C peptide.
Proinsulin isn’t measured directly because it’s very unstable in the tube.

**DIABETES MELLITUS (DM)**

**Diabetes** can be of 2 types: **Type 1 and Type 2** (more prevalent).

*[PDF p. 180]*

**Type 1 diabetes** is a polygenic autoimmune disease where there is a **production**
**of antibodies against the islet cells of the pancreas.** To tell if it’s a case of type
1 or 2 diabetes, these antibodies are measured. In type 1, because of the immune
attack, there is **no production of insulin or C peptide.**
However **Type 2 diabetes** is caused because of **insulin resistance:** the pancreas
is able to produce insulin but the tissues can’t uptake it to reduce glucose levels.
So, when insulin is measured in patients with type 2 diabetes, high levels of insulin
are found. There is a big influence from genetic factors but also obesity and
physical inactivity.

**Criteria for the diagnosis of diabetes**

Diabetes can be diagnosed if you have one of the following criteria:

   **Fasting** (at least 8h fasting) **plasma glucose** / FPG higher than **126 mg/dL.**

   If 2 hours after having received a glucose overload, glucose levels are higher than
**200 mg/dL** during **OGTT (oral glucose tolerance test)** . Guidelines set by WHO: we
have to employ glucose containing **75g of anhydrous glucose**, dissolved in water.
   - If **glycated hemoglobin** is higher than **6.5%** . The test has to be performed in a
**certified laboratory** that has a method that is traceable to a standardized method.

   Also, if a patient with classic symptoms of hyperglycemia, polyurea,... has a random
plasma glucose higher than 200 mg/dL.

These tests aren’t valid for differentiating between type
1 and 2 diabetes. That would be the next step.

**Criteria for diagnosis of prediabetes**

**Prediabetes** also consists in a hyperglucose tolerance, a situation between a normal patient
and a diabetic. It’s important to monitor because it increases the probability of developing
hypertension and cardiovascular problems.

   Fasting plasma glucose (FPG) is found to be between **100-125 mg/dL** . It’s a range
that makes it likely to develop diabetes in the future.
   - 2h fasting after postload (75g of anhydrous glucose): PG is found between **140-200**
**mg/dL**
   - Glycated hemoglobin is found in a range between **5.7%-6.4%**

**Blood glucose test**

Can be measured in serum or plasma. We can
obtain the sample in fasting conditions or random.
NaF and a separating gel are used to stabilize the
sample.

*[PDF p. 181]*

**Oral glucose tolerance test (OGTT)**

Prior to the test, the patient fasts for 8 hours.
During the test, the patient has to be seated
with no exercise (it could reduce glucose
levels). The glucose is measured at baseline,
and after the glucose overload (75 g of oral
glucose), it’s measured every 30 minutes for
2 hours. This way we obtain a **curve** .

The insulin is measured at the same time as

glucose absorption, generating an additional
insulin curve, which allows to differentiate
between a type 1 and type 2 diabetic.

**Measurement of glycated hemoglobin**

Hemoglobin has two alpha and beta chains. GH is the result of glycation of the beta chain by
glucose. **HbA1** is the result of the **glycation** carried out **specifically by glucose.** This
glycation reaction is known as the **Amadori reaction** (caramelization of sugars).
To measure **glycated hemoglobin** (found inside red blood cells), we can’t use serum or
plasma; we take the whole blood sample and measure it in EDTA tubes, through
immunoassay.
If your glucose levels are high, the amount of HbA1 increases. Its amount depends on blood
glucose concentration.

The advantages of measuring HbA1 is that **no fasting is required**, and it also gives an idea
of the **exposure to glucose during the past 2-3 months (** which is the half life of the final
product (because the reaction is irreversible)). But it also has limitations: in patients with
shorter erythrocyte half life, it doesn’t reflect the glycemia in the past 3 months; and also, if
you don’t have hemoglobin A, and rather hemoglobin variants, this test cannot be performed.

**Gestational diabetes mellitus**

During **pregnancy** there is an **increase in insulin resistance** . In addition,
there is an increase in placental lactogen, a hormone that has the opposite
effect of insulin: it favours glucose release into the bloodstream. There are
risk factors such as family history of diabetes, obesity, age, alterations in
previous pregnancy,... that make it more likely to develop gestational
diabetes during pregnancy. There is an increased risk for the mother of
being diabetic in the future, and the baby can also develop problems.

**-**
**Tests for screening and diagnosis:**

**One step strategy:** performing oral glucose tolerance test **(OGTT).**

*[PDF p. 182]*

**Two step strategy:** doctors perform an initial screening test, **O’Sullivan test**, which is
performed at baseline and no fasting is required. 50 g of glucose overload is administered
and screening is performed after 1h. After, 100g of glucose overload and subsequent
screening is performed after fasting, to possibly confirm diabetes.

**Tests performed for long term follow up**

   **Capillary blood glucose:** Can be performed at home and it has a close correlation
with plasma glucose, so it can be used for monitoring.
   - **Glycohemoglobin A1c:** Good glycemic control implies **levels lower than 7%** of
glycated hemoglobin.

   **Insulin and C peptide tests:** they both give us the same info, the insulin synthesis
rate. In patients with insulin treatment, we measure C peptide.
   - **Glucagon test:** it’s a **dynamic** test, to evaluate the **pancreatic insulin reserve.** We
administer glucagon to the patient, which in a normal patient stimulates insulin
release to equilibrate. If you’re a type 1 diabetic, there is no increase in C peptide or

insulin.

   - **Albumin in urine:** it’s an early marker of kidney dysfunction. If detected
early, it can be prevented and the dysfunction reversed. It can be done
using a **urinary strip, 24h urine or a spot urine sample.**

   **Lipid profile:** diabetes conferes high cardiovascular risk, so it’s important
to measure the lipid panel.

   **Inflammatory and prothrombotic markers**, to measure cardiovascular

risk

*[PDF p. 183]*

### **Introduction**

Blood proteins also termed plasma proteins are the proteins present in blood plasma. Many proteins
are found in plasma because they perform their functions in the blood, others are circulating to a tissue
and others because they are released from injured cells.

Some functions performed by plasma proteins are:

- Maintenance of oncotic pressure. Albumin accounts for 40-60% of blood proteins thus, it is a major
contributor to maintaining the oncotic pressure of plasma and prevents fluid loss to the tissues.

- Transporting of water-insoluble substances, such as fatty acids or steroid hormones.

- Maintenance of acid-base balance.

- Circulating reserve of amino acids for cells, especially albumin.

- Hemostasis, since numerous factors related to coagulation and fibrinolysis are found in plasma.

- Defense, in which immunoglobulins and C-reactive protein are involved.

The concentration of each protein depends on the rate of synthesis, its distribution between the plasma
and the extravascular space, and the rate of degradation.

Most plasma proteins are glycoproteins, such as albumin, with 1% carbohydrate in their molecule.
Most plasma proteins are synthesized in the liver. An exception are immunoglobulins, synthesized in
plasma cells, and various peptide hormones such as insulin, synthesized by endocrine glands. Protein
catabolism occurs mainly in the liver. Plasma proteins are also lost by the kidney and the vascular
endothelium.

The study of plasma proteins is performed by quantifying the total concentration of proteins in plasma
and by quantifying some individual proteins. The separation by electrophoresis and semiquantification
of the different protein fractions is also a valuable diagnostic tool.

### **Total Plasma proteins**

Most laboratories routinely measure total proteins in plasma or serum. The total plasma protein
concentration is 6.6-8.7 g/dL. The general causes of changes in the total protein concentration are the
variation in the aqueous composition of the plasma and changes in the concentration of the most
abundant proteins, mainly albumin and immunoglobulins. Hypoproteinemia is more frequent than
hyperproteinemia. (Fig 1)

Hypoproteinemia may be due to decreased protein synthesis or excessive protein losses:

- For the adequate synthesis of plasma proteins, a sufficient supply of amino acids in the diet is

necessary, so the nutritional status has a decisive influence on their concentration. Therefore, states
of malnutrition or intestinal malabsorption cause hypoproteinemia. Since the liver is the main

*[PDF p. 184]*

organ of plasma protein synthesis, severe liver disease can lead to a decrease in protein synthesis
and, consequently, to low plasma protein values. Severe immunodeficiency may be accompanied
by hypoproteinemia, due to decreased immunoglobulin synthesis.

- Hypoproteinemia can also associate with kidney dysfunction becauses of high protein losses

through the kidney. Extensive burns also produce high protein losses, which cause
hypoproteinemia.

- Increased blood volume can also cause hypoproteinemia due to hemodilution. This may be due,

for example, to fluid retention.

Hyperproteinemia can also be caused by impaired synthesis or hemoconcentration. A decrease in
plasma volume causes hemoconcentration, which is reflected in hyperproteinemia. Hyperproteinemia
due to dehydration may be due to decreased fluid intake or increased fluid loss, as in the case of severe
diarrhea or vomiting. Immunoglobulin elevation occurs, for example, in patients with multiple
myeloma where there is exaggerated clonal synthesis. This means than a specific clon of
immunoglobulins is increased. Polyclonal increases of immunoglobulins also cause hyperproteinemia.

Figure 1. Main causes of alterations in protein concentration due to physiological (pregnancy) or
pathological causes.

### **Protein separation by electrophoresis**

Serum proteins are separated by their different electrical charge by agarose gel electrophoresis or by
capillary electrophoresis. The electrical charge of the protein depends on the pH of the buffer. A pH
of 8.6 is normally used. Immunoglobulins migrate in the opposite direction due to the low charge in
relation to electroendosmosis (movement of cations towards the cathode which pulls the surrounding
water molecules).

When agarose gel electrophoresis is used, proteins are stained using Coomassie brilliant blue.
Electrophoresis separates the proteins into five main bands or fractions (Figure 2): albumin is at the
anodic (+) end, followed by the α1-globulin, α2-globulin, β-globulin and γ-globulin fractions. After
staining, the gel is visually inspected and the percentage represented by each band is measured by
densitometry and the proteinogram profile is obtained.

*[PDF p. 185]*

In capillary electrophoresis, no staining is used and semi-quantification of the bands is performed
directly during migration measuring the absorbance of the peptide bond with UV light.

In order to quantify each band, the total protein concentration in the sample is also determined. The
proteinogram should be performed with a serum sample, since, with plasma, fibrinogen appears as an
additional band in the β-region, similar in appearance to a monoclonal band, which can lead to an error
in interpretation.

Figure 2. Electrophoresis of serum proteins in alkaline buffer in agarose gel and staining with
Coomassie blue. The corresponding densitometric analysis, the proportion of bands and the main
proteins influencing each one are shown. Ig: immunoglobulin

### **Immunofixation**

This technique is used to identify a monoclonal protein. Immunofixation has two phases:
electrophoresis, in which the components of the sample are separated, and immunoprecipitation of the
proteins to be detected.

First, an agarose gel is used in which the sample is applied to each lane and electrophoresis is
performed. Subsequently, an antibody that recognizes each of the different immunoglobulin heavy and
light chains is added to each lane of the gel. These antibodies diffuse into their assigned lane in the gel
and form a precipitate with their corresponding antigen. In a reference lane the proteins are precipitated
with a fixative solution. After an incubation time for the immunoprecipitate to form, the gel is washed
to remove soluble and non-precipitated proteins, and stained to identify the proteins. (Figure 3)

*[PDF p. 186]*

Figure 3. Polyclonal (M1) or monoclonal (M2) increase of immunoglobulins in serum. Example of

elimination of light chains in urine. Immunofixation of the serum sample shows the existence of
a monoclonal IgG-λ band and free λ light chains. Immunofixation of urine shows elimination of
IgG-λ.

### **Study of the main plasma proteins**

#### Albumin

Albumin is the most abundant protein in plasma, representing between 40 and 60% of the total, making
it the main protein responsible for maintaining oncotic pressure. It has a molecular weight of 68.5 kDa
and a large amount of negative charges in its molecule. Its half-life is about 21 days.

Albumin is synthesized in the liver according to protein intake and its concentration in blood, and is
metabolized in many tissues where it is taken up and hydrolyzed by the cells, taking advantage of its
amino acids. In this way, it is a reservoir of amino acids. Catabolism increases in traumatic processes,
infections and surgical interventions. Another function of albumin is that of transport, due to its high
concentration and the large number of negative charges it possesses. Thus, it transports fatty acids,
drugs, vitamins, calcium and bilirubin. It also transports various hormones. Therefore, changes in the
plasma concentration of albumin modify the concentration of these substances that it transports.

**Hypoalbuminemia** is much more common than hyperalbuminemia. The most frequent cause of an
increase in its concentration is dehydration. Its concentration can also be raised "artifactually" by
venous stasis at the time of extraction. Since albumin is the most abundant protein in plasma, its
decrease usually causes hypoproteinemia. Hypoalbuminemia may be due to physiological causes, as
occurs during pregnancy, because the circulating volume increases and, to some extent, hemodilution
occurs. It can also be due to pathological causes by:

- Lower synthesis, as in malnutrition, where low amino acid intake leads to a decrease in albumin
synthesis. For this reason, the determination of albumin in blood is frequently used in the monitoring
of the patient's nutritional status, such as in intestinal malabsorption. Albumin concentration also
decreases in liver diseases due to a decrease in the synthetic capacity of the liver.

- Losses, such as in renal disorders, can cause very severe hypoalbuminemia. γ-globulinas

#### γ-globulin

The γ-globulin region of the serum protein electrophoresis consists mainly of immunoglobulins, which
are formed by 2 identical heavy chains and 2 identical light chains linked by disulfide bridges (Fig 4).
The heavy chains are of 5 types (γ, α, μ, δ and ε) and the light chains are of two types, κ and λ, There
are five classes of immunoglobulins, differing in heavy chains: IgG, IgA, IgM, IgD and IgE.
Immunoglobulins participate in mechanisms of defense of the organism.

*[PDF p. 187]*

Figure 4. Immunoglobulin structure.

Hypogammaglobulinemia

Immunodeficiencies can be genetic or acquired, and are associated with a risk of recurrent infections.
The decrease or absence of the γ-globulin region in the proteinogram usually indicates an IgG
deficiency, usually secondary to protein loss or insufficient synthesis.

Hypergammaglobulinemia

The polyclonal increase of immunoglobulins is the normal response to infection, in which all
immunoglobulins are elevated. The monoclonal elevation of immunoglobulins is due to the synthesis
of molecules with identical structure. If the amount produced is significant, a well-defined band is
detected in the γ region of the proteinogram. To type the monoclonal band observed in the
proteinogram it is necessary to perform another technique called immunofixation. Immunofixation
consists of the use of specific antibodies against the heavy and light chains, so that a band is formed
where the monoclonal component of the sample (antigen) reacts with the added antibody.

Multiple myeloma is a malignant neoplasm characterized by the elevation of a clone of plasma cells
in the bone marrow, which produce a single type of immunoglobulin, making it monoclonal. At the
same time the production of normal immunoglobulins is inhibited, so that these patients frequently
present recurrent infections. Typical are osteolytic lesions in bone, with elevated values of alkaline
phosphatase and calcium in blood, and other non-specific symptoms such as weight loss, anemia,
hemorrhages or renal failure.

*[PDF p. 188]*

Figure 5. Production of a monoclonal immunoglobulin by a clone of plasma cells against a polyclonal
response. Electrophoresis and densitometry of the monoclonal band are indicated.

### **Acute phase reaction**

In the face of an injury of various types, mechanical, physical, chemical, biological or tumor, a
defensive response of the organism is activated, called acute phase reaction. In this reaction, certain
proteins change their concentration in the blood in response to the inflammation. They are called acute
phase reactants:

- Some proteins such as C-reactive protein (CRP) or immunoglobulins increase their concentration.
They are positive acute phase reactants.

- Others, such as albumin, decrease in concentration. They are negative acute phase reactants.

Therefore, the ratio between albumin and globulins changes, which leads to alterations in the
physicochemical properties of the plasma. Red cells have a negative electrical charge, but the increase
in immunoglobulins, with more positive charges, favors red cell sedimentation. Thus, when there is an
acute phase reaction, the erythrocyte sedimentation rate increases. It can be measured in the laboratory
by the erythrocyte sedimentation rate test, whose elevation in a patient indicates that there is an acute
phase reaction.

These proteins have a defensive mission against aggression through different mechanisms. Acute
phase reactant proteins are nonspecific, i.e. they are associated with many diseases. Therefore, their
determination is not diagnostic, but rather is used to follow the evolution of the disease and the efficacy
of treatment.

### **C-reactive protein**

C-reactive protein is synthesized in the liver. It is so named because of its property to bind tightly to
the C polysaccharide of the S. pneumoniae cell wall. Upon binding, it activates the classical
complement pathway and can initiate processes that promote the elimination of pathogenic cells.

It is the most representative of the acute phase reactant proteins, as its concentration increases
dramatically after cell injury. Its elevation is not disease specific, but supports the diagnosis of
inflammatory processes. It is very sensitive for diagnosing inflammatory intestinal diseases, and for
detecting infections and rejection in transplanted organs. Serial determination of its concentration is of
interest, since the levels and the time it remains elevated reflect the severity of the process that

originated it and its evolution.

Figure 6. Evolution of C-reactive protein concentration during an acute process: acute pancreatitis and
septic shock. Concentrations decrease, reflecting a favorable evolution.

*[PDF p. 189]*

**TBL 6.5. Plasma proteins. Clinical Cases**

**CLINICAL CASE 1**

**-**
The results from electrophoresis are caused by the nephrotic syndrome: the loss of
proteins through the kidneys drives the liver to try to compensate and reinstate oncotic
pressure, which reflects in the high alpha-2 and lipoprotein A levels.

- The patient is suffering recurrent infection due to the loss of gamma proteins
(immunoglobulins).

- The swelling is caused by the decrease in Albumin: fluid escapes from the vessels and into
the intercellular space in an attempt to reinstate oncotic pressure. This way, edemas appear.

- The increase in cholesterol and in lipoprotein A caused by nephrotic syndrome means the
patient is suffering from secondary dyslipidemia, and at high risk of atherogenesis.

**NEPHROTIC**

**SYNDROME**

*[PDF p. 190]*

**CLINICAL CASE 2**

**-**
High total proteins (TP) levels are explained by the increased monoclonal immunoglobulin
synthesis

- In protein electrophoresis, we observe a huge gamma peak. Immunofixation is performed
to observe which type of immunoglobulin is being overly produced, and we conclude that it’s
IgG-landa (there is an increase in this immunoglobulin), due to the multiple myeloma.

- There’s an increase in _Alkaline phosphatase_ (found in bone, liver, placenta…). We can
suspect it’s due to an increase in the liberation of the bone isozyme, due to the referred bone
pain in the lower extremities.

- The patient has hyperglobulinemia, specifically monoclonal

*[PDF p. 191]*

**CLINICAL CASE 3**

- Amylase and lipase are proteins found in the liver (amylase is also found in saliva; it’s an
early but less specific marker, whereas lipase is a late marker). An increase in both indicates
there must be an inflammation of the pancreas.

- There are low albumin levels because there is a decreased synthesis due to an increase in
other plasma protein levels. In this way, oncotic pressure is preserved.

- There are elevated TAG and Total cholesterol levels (in this case we can’t calculate LDL
cholesterol because we don’t know HDL concentration and TAG levels are too high to apply
the Friedewald's equation).

- There is an acute phase reaction (hence the elevated C reactive protein), so there will be
an increased erythrocyte sedimentation.

**Lecture 7.1. Cell cycle. Mitosis**

_SUGESTION: leer pdfs del Strachan! Mucha información adicional de ahí cae en el examen_
When we analyze a tissue by microscopy, we can see cells in interphase or mitosis. But not
very frequently, because in normal tissues cells aren’t continuously in mitosis.
Within the interphase there are other subphases:

   G1. GAP I phase, between mitosis and S phase

   S phase

   G2. GAP II, from S to M

Usually, cells in culture take 24h to divide. And they divide constantly as they are surrounded
by favorable conditions. But this isn’t the same in tissues.

**Interphase** (takes around 23 hours)

**-**
**G1 phase**
11 hours on average. It’s the phase where each cell carries out its function and
biomolecule synthesis.

**-**
**S phase**
Phase where cells duplicate the DNA and synthesize histones.

**-**
**G2 phase**
It’s a preparation for mitosis. Cells have to get ready to package DNA and form

chromosomes.

**Mitosis** (takes around 1 hour to occur)

**Proliferation within a specific tissue**

By immunohistochemistry, we can detect proteins participating in cell division and
observe them under the microscope.
Proliferating cells within an area of a specific tissue are called **STEM CELLS** : they
have the property of **dividing without losing telomeres** (don’t age) and are in
charge of **repopulating** tissues through proliferation. The telomere length is kept
intact in stem cells because they possess an enzyme, _**telomerase**_, which keeps it
from shortening.

The created cells (“proliferative cells”), at some point,
mature and differentiate (in this example, into enterocytes,
which will carry out the absorption of nutrients).
Stem cells have a unique property: they divide through
**asymmetric division**, meaning that through mitosis,
instead of producing two identical daughter cells, they
produce 2 different ones: one will remain as a stem cell,
and the other will mature and loose the properties of
stemness (every time it divides, it will lose telomere length and age,
and will lose its capacity to differentiate into other cells).

*[PDF p. 193]*

The newly produced and differentiated cell which initially came from the stem cell is called
**progenitor cell**, which starts dividing and proliferating a lot to repopulate. These cells will
finally **move to an area of the organ** (in this case the intestine) **and finish maturing** into
enterocytes.
The newly synthesized enterocytes generally won’t divide anymore and remain in G0, so
they won’t initiate another cell cycle (escapes G1 and the cycle).
In the process of **G0**, cells cannot divide. For example, in the case of neurons, which will not
enter the cell cycle. In some cells, the G0 phase is permanent.

**-**
**Self-generation ability of the liver**
The **liver** is the only organ in our body that is **able to regenerate by itself.** (In the
picture = stem cells or progenitor cells proliferating). We can take advantage of this
property to perform living donor liver transplants. Ex: a patient with cirrhosis, for
example due to alcohol abuse or viral infection, presents a damaged liver with scars
where the liver is trying to repair itself. The cirrhotic liver cannot regenerate, because
it’s a chronic damage. A transplant can be carried out. We cut a section of the
donor’s liver (up to 40% can be cut) and place it in the recipient, which will have the
cirrhotic liver removed. In approximately 1 month, the donor liver in the donor and
recipient will have the same shape and size previous to being downsized.

**CONTROL AT THE CHECKPOINTS OF CELL CYCLE**

3 major checkpoints:

1. **At the end of G1** : cells decide if they are going to duplicate the
DNA or not. At this point they check if **outer and external**
**conditions** are proper for cell division. If everything's ok, they
enter the S phase.
2. **At the end of G2:** the cell checks if DNA has been correctly
replicated and the external conditions are ok.
3. **During mitosis:** the cell checks all chromosomes are attached
to spindles and aligned in the equatorial plain.

The **main regulatory point is the one prior to the S phase**, because if cells enter S phase
and cannot complete the cell cycle, they will die through apoptosis. This process takes a lot
of effort and can’t be reversed once started, so it’s an important regulatory point known as
**restriction point.**

**Proteins that control the cell cycle at checkpoints**

There are **2 families** in charge:

   **Cdk (cyclin-dependent-kinases):** they **phosphorylate** other proteins. They are only
**active** when **bound to cyclins** . Without them, they cannot function. They have a
**constitutive expression**, meaning that they are always present in the cell.

*[PDF p. 194]*

- **Cyclins:** they bind Cdks to control their activity. They are **regulators** . But they **don’t**
**have a constitutive expression,** instead they have a **cyclic expression,** following
cycles of synthesis and degradation.

**Control at G1**

   **Receptor** at the membrane for nutrients, growth factors and other hormones

**-** **Cdk4**

   - 2 proteins bound to each other: **Retinoblastoma** (Rb, very important role for tumor
formation) and **E2F** (transcription factor)

When everything is inactive, **E2F and Rb** are bound together. To enter the S phase, the cell
has to check for the presence of growth factors, nutrients,... which stimulates the
proliferation of the cell. When they bind to the receptor, the signal is transmitted
intracellularly. These intracellular messengers will stimulate the **transcription** of the **Cyclin**

**D** gene. mRNA for Cyclin D will
be exported and ribosomes will
produce Cyclin D. It then binds to
**cdk4** to form an active complex,

which translocates to the

**nucleus** . **Cdk4-Cyclin D** has the
function of **phosphorylating Rb.**

The effect of this is the

separation between Rb and E2F.

Now **E2F is active** and can act

inside the cell, which will
stimulate the **transcription of**
**different genes,** genes related
with DNA replication, to begin the
cell proliferation.

**-**
**What happens when things go wrong at the G1 checkpoint?**

        **Inactivating mutations in Rb:** This causes Rb to lose its function, and
therefore **E2F is always active** and the cells, constantly dividing. This
consequently causes **cancer** because there is no regulation of
the cell process and mutations accumulate.

        **Abnormally increased levels of cyclins:** if cyclins are constantly present
and not only in peaks during the cell cycle, the **cells** will **constantly replicate**
and **tumors** will develop. This is usually observed in breast cancer. The
prognosis of a patient will be worse if he/she has high cyclin D levels.

*[PDF p. 195]*

**Control at G2**

**-**
**Receptors**
   - **Cdk1** in the cytosol

**-**
**Cyclin B**

At this point the cell will check if there has been correct DNA
replication, if it's the right size and if there are favorable
external conditions. Providing that everything is under control,
**Cyclin B gene is transcribed and translated**, and binds
**Cdk1** forming a complex. **Cdk1-Cyclin B complex** acts by
**phosphorylating** different proteins involved in:

                - **The nuclear lamina** (meshwork of filaments that maintains the
shape of the nucleus. It’s attached to the inner membrane of the nucleus
and formed by proteins known as **lamins** ): when these **lamins are**
**phosphorylated,** they **disintegrate,** allowing chromosomes to move
freely through the cytosol and to consequently bind to histones.

**-** **Chromosome condensation**

**-**
**Spindle formation**

**Inactivation of the cell cycle**

The cell cycle is inactivated by:

1. **Inhibition of intracellular signalling.** Even in the presence
of lots of nutrients, cells are able to stop proliferation
2. **Cyclin degradation:** once cyclins have played a role in the
cell cycle, they are degraded so the cell isn’t constantly
dividing.
3. **Production of Cdk-cyclin inhibitors,** such as p21, an
inhibitor that plays a role when things go wrong during the cell
cycle. The process has to be stopped and the problem, fixed.
If the problems can’t be resolved, the cell will enter apoptosis.
4. **De-phosphorylation of Rb,** carried out through
_phosphatases_, so E2F can be rebound to retinoblastoma and

remain inactive.

When these **inactivation mechanisms** are **impaired, tumorous cells appear.**

*[PDF p. 196]*

**Lecture 7.2. Meiosis**

**MEIOTIC CELL DIVISION**

It’s a special type of cell division that occurs in **germ cells that** will generate 4
**gametes/haploid cells** with one set of genetic information. There will be 2 consecutive

divisions.

**Difference between mitosis and meiosis**

Consider a cell with 2 chromosomes (homologous). Cells duplicate visible chromosomes,
with 2 sister chromatids per chromosome. We now have 4 copies of the gene (2 AA, 2 aa).
At the end of **mitosis**, we find **2 daughter cells**, each with A and a genes.

In **meiosis**, after DNA duplication and chromosome formation, during **prophase I**, the
homologous chromosomes form the bivalent structure, and **recombination occurs** : the
non-sister chromatids from both homologous chromosomes exchange genetic material,
resulting in chromosomes and chromatids with diverse genes. During **Anaphase I, whole**
**chromosomes migrate towards the centrosomes.** At the end of meiotic division I, we are
left with 2 haploid cells which have their genetic material duplicated, so they directly enter
the subsequent division. As a result, **4 haploid cells are generated**, each with a
combination of maternal and paternal information.

*[PDF p. 197]*

**MEIOSIS**

**1. Meiotic division I**

**-**
**Prophase I:** we find several subphases.

**a)** **Leptotene:** chromatine starts to condensate and form chromosomes. Homologous

chromosomes begin to pair with each other.
**b)** **Zygotene (** _**union**_ **)** : chromosomal pairing occurs, resulting

in the **synaptonemal complex (SC).** This complex is held
together by proteins. It’s important to keep them together
for recombination to occur properly. At this point, we have

**bivalence** .

**c)** **Pachytene (** _**thick**_ **):** the chromosomes are fully visible, thick

and bound together. In this step, **crossing over**
**(recombination)** takes place: homologous chromosomes
exchange genetic material.
**d)** **Diplotene (** _**diplo=double**_ **):** once recombination has taken place, chromosomes tend

to separate and decondense, but they remain bound by **chiasmata** . In the areas
where there has been recombination, chiasmata is produced during pachytene, but
can’t be seen due to the proximity of chromosomes. It’s in diplotene, when
chromosomes separate a bit, that chiasmata are observed.
**e)** **Diacinesis:** chiasmata disappear, the nucleolus and nucleus envelope disappear

and histones begin to form.

_Meiosis takes 64 days in the testis of males. It starts_
_at the base and the cells suffer a morphological_
_change that transforms the new cells into sperms._

**-**
**Metaphase I**
Homologous chromosomes align in the equatorial plane. **Bivalents** are **randomly oriented**
**and assorted**, which gives place to 2^`23 options/permutations. This generates **genetic**
**variability**, along with recombination.

**-**
**Anaphase I**
Individual whole chromosomes migrate to the poles of the cell, breaking the bivalent

structure.

**-**
**Telophase I**
Nuclear membrane begins to form, but not completely, since the cells are going to undergo a
subsequent division because of the duplicated haploid genetic info.

*[PDF p. 198]*

**- Cytokinesis**

**2. Meiotic division II**

**Prophase II** goes on as a normal prophase, followed by **Metaphase II, Anaphase II,**
**Telophase II and Cytokinesis** . It results in 4 haploid daughter cells.

**GAMETOGENESIS**

**Gametogenesis** is the process by which **mature germ cells** are produced.

- In the case of **males**, **meiosis starts at puberty,** and it’s a **continuous process** that takes
64 days.

- In **females** (watch video on female gametogenesis), meiosis **starts when the embryo is**
**developing,** around the 5th month of gestation. But, these cells enter into meiosis and **stop**
the process at Diplotene of Prophase I, and **remain halted until ovulation** . Until puberty, all
the **oocytes** are stuck at the **diplotene phase** .
As a result of the **release of hormones** such as estrogens, these oocytes will form a follicle
that is released and completes the **first meiotic division:** the 2 resulting cells are haploid.
One of them gets all the cytoplasm, and becomes the **viable cell** or **secondary oocyte**
(needs a big cytoplasm area for this whole process), and the other is bound to die (known as
**Polar body I** ).
The secondary oocyte, which is viable, continues the process. It once again gets **stopped in**
**Metaphase II:** there is a **second meiotic arrest** . **Only if the oocyte becomes fertilized, it**
**will complete meiosis** .
After **fertilization**, the **secondary oocyte** will divide again to produce **2 daughter cells** .
One will be removed ( **Polar body II** ) and the other will **combine** its **genetic material** with
that of the sperm’s to form a **zygote** (the pronuclei fuse).

*[PDF p. 199]*

**Lecture 7.3. Levels of chromatin packaging**

The genome is a long thread of DNA. In the nucleus, the genome is wrapped around
proteins, which creates a fiber known as **chromatin** . During the cell cycle, chromatin
changes its level of packaging (highest during metaphase), forming **chromosomes** . During
interphase, chromosomes can’t be observed under light microscope.

This **packaging** is **not done randomly:**

**“Chromatin for each chromosome occupies a specific territory inside the**
**nucleus, with many domains for the different genes”**

**Levels of packaging**

**1.** **1st level of packaging**
8 different **histone** molecules **(2H2A, 2H2B, 2H3 and 2H4)** form a **nucleosome** when DNA
wraps around them. Histone **H1** acts as a **linker**, giving stability between different
nucleosomes. It’s the basic level of organization of chromatin **(10 nanometer);** _(modelo del_
_collar de perlas)_

**2.** **2nd level**

DNA is found wrapped around histones, forming a **solenoid** _(solenoid model),_ which gives
place to the **30 nanometer fibers.**

**3.** **3rd level**

30 nanometer fiber continues to wrap and coil, giving place to the **chromosomes** seen
during mitosis **(100 nanometers).**

**The human cell has 46 chromosomes.**

Chromatin in interphase is seen as a random
assembly of threads, but in truth, is not so

random: the chromatin for each chromosome

occupies a specific territory inside the nucleus.
There are two pairs of chromosomes (maternal
and paternal), which are homologous.
The two **homologous chromosomes** don’t
necessarily occupy neighboring areas of the

nucleus.

Ex: chromosome 16 (maternal and paternal version). These **homologous chromosomes**
contain the **same genes in the same order**, but these may be different versions of said
genes ( **alleles** ). They may differ in a specific region of the nucleotide sequence, and each

will confer **different characteristics.**

Chromosome 16=MC1R, codifies for hair colour.

During **DNA replication, each chromosome is copied**, giving place to **two sister**
**chromatids** (each visible chromosome will be seen with two arms that contain identical
information).

*[PDF p. 200]*

**Nucleosomes**

**Histones** have **tails**, whose amino acids will undergo
modifications that will be crucial for cell replication. DNA wraps

around these histones to form **nucleosomes** .

The **levels of packaging** correlates with **gene activity and**

**translation.**

**Chromosome structure**

Chromosomes have a **centromere**, the point of attachment of **microtubules** . When
chromosomes are stained through **G banding**, it reflects the packaging levels: clearer bands
are less compact, whereas darker bands undergo higher packaging.
The **short arm** is known as the **“p” arm,** and the **long arm** as the **“q” arm** (although
chromosomes don’t always have a long and short arm, they can be found with similar
lengths).
The banding pattern allows to name the bands by number.

**How chromatin undergoes packaging**

1st, **chromatin forms loops**, giving place
to the solenoid model. These loops are
clustered into **compartments** . The largest
type of compartment in the hierarchy is
called **TAD (topologically associating**
**domains)**, they are **domains of**
**chromatin that associate in space.**
Therefore, chromatin regions within that

domain **interact with each other more**

**frequently than with other regions of**
**the genome.** This has functional
consequences: everything found inside
this TAD interact among each other more
frequently. **Interaction usually means**

**function.**

The whole **genome** is therefore
**compartmentalized into functional**

**domains.**

Many TADs cluster and come together to form exclusive compartments.
Genes in each compartment will be active or not depending on the interactions in TAD,

within each domain.

**Inactive compartments** are closer to the periphery of the nucleus, **closer to the nuclear**

**lamina.**

*[PDF p. 201]*

**Compartments** come in **two different**
**flavours: ACTIVE (A compartments),**
**and INACTIVE (B compartments).**

Genes within each TAD: most of them

will be active, or most of them inactive.

The **function of each cell** is

**determined by the activity and**
**expression of each independent**
**gene.** This is due to cell differentiation
during embryo development. That’s
why the cells in our body, although
containing the same genome, have
different morphology and functions.
**Cells differentiate** into the various tissues due to **differential gene expressions,** different
patterns of activation.

This all occurs through the **regulation of gene expression**, carried out by **enhancers and**
**promoters.** The enhancer has to come into contact with the promoter to activate the
expression of the gene, and this occurs thanks to the formation of **loops** . That’s why genes
are found in different **TADs**, which **contain genes and their respective enhancers.**

Some proteins are responsible for the **borders between TADs.** A disruption in this border
can cause disease because it interferes with gene expression, by causing the formation of
bigger TADs and therefore more interactions.
In vertebrates, the **boundaries** are made by the protein **CTCF**, a protein that **behaves as an**
**insulator. It forms a dimer that will flank compartments at the boundaries.**

*[PDF p. 202]*

**Lecture 7.4. General features of the human genome**

- The human genome is **3Gb long** and contains thousands of genes.

- The **G-C content** (percentage of guanine and cytosine bases) **directly correlates with**
**functional regions of the genome.**

- The genome is full of repeated copies of **mobile elements.**

**Graph about the G-C percentage**

**1st graph:** The graph is not symmetrical. There are
regions of the genome with very low G-C percentage,
while others are very rich in Gs and Cs. The average is
below 50 ( **42%**, but there are regions with G-C content
higher than 50)

**2nd graph:**
About relative **gene density.** We find a very
distinctive trend: the **higher the G-C content**, the
**more gene-dense** that region of the genome. G-C
regions associate with more genes, because
genes are G-C rich. The rest of the regions of the
genome which aren’t genes have less G-C
implications.

**Repeats**

There are sequences which are repeated in the genome thousands of times.
**Repeats** come in two flavours: **tandem** or **dispersed** .

**1.** **Tandems**

We classify tandem repeats by the size of the unit which is repeated:

   - **Satellites** are tandem repeats made up of large units
of **100 bp**, which are repeated up to several
Megabases. For example, “alpha-satellite” contains
DNA that makes up the centromeres.
   - **Minisatellites** are shorter units **(10-100 bp).**
   - **Microsatellites** are short units made up of **1 - 9 bp,**
repeated to make up the sequence. They are also
known as **STRs (short tandem repeats).**

*[PDF p. 203]*

**2.** **Dispersed 0**

They are dispersed because they are **mobile elements, transposons** (jump from one
region of the cell to another. Have been doing it for thousands of years). Two different types:

   **DNA transposons:** a sequence of the genome that makes a copy of itself and
integrates it in a different region of the genome ( _transposase_ ). They are relatively
short. We have **300 thousand** copies of **DNA transposons** dispersed in our

genome.

   **RNA transposons:** they work via an intermediate. The
sequence is translated into messenger RNA, which goes
into the cytoplasm, generating a protein which will then
bind to the **mRNA** and move back to the nucleus,
introducing it into another area of the genome through
**retrotranscription** (carried out by a
_**retrotranscriptase**_ ).

**a)** **LTR-retrotransposons:** LTR stands for **long-terminal-repeats.** They are

specific sequences of **retroviral genomes** (ex: HIV), specifically the
sequences that make up the **tail ends** of these retroviral genomes, which are
flanked by target site duplications. _(These regions codify for RNA which is_
_translated into a retrotranscriptase, which will carry out retrotranscription of_
_the RNA fragment (forming DNA) and reinsert it in a region of our genome)._
We find many LTRs in our own genomes because we have been **infected** by
viruses over millions of years. **8% of our genome** is made up of these
**LTR-retrotransposons.**

**b)** **Non-LTR retrotransposons:** They are not derived from retroviruses. We find:

**- LINE** (long): Quite rich in **G-C (42%)** and contain target site duplications.
They are very important because they are the ones that **code for** the
**proteins** that **carry out retrotransposition.** When LINE are transcribed to
mRNA, they codify for 2 proteins: **ORF1** (protein with DNA binding ability) and
**ORF2** (protein with endonuclease and retrotranscriptase activity), which are
responsible for retrotransposition. We more or less carry **850,000** LINE
retrotransposons ( **21%** of our genome). **LINE 1.**

          - **SINE** (short): Even more rich in **G-C (56%),** also contains target site
duplications. They **don’t encode for any proteins,** so to carry out
retrotransposition they will have to make use of LINE/autonomous
_retrotranscriptases_ . They are non-autonomous. We have around **1,500,000**
SINE retrotransposons, which make up about **13%** of our genome. **ALU.**

*[PDF p. 204]*

**Lecture 7.5. DNA replication**

Scientists wondered at the ability of organisms to create copies of themselves, and of cells
to produce identical copies of large molecules.

**Fundamental aspects**

   DNA replication is a **semiconservative process**

   Replication begins at specific **replication origins,** and once it has begun, it
proceeds **bidirectionally**
   - DNA synthesis progresses **from 5´ to 3´end,** and it’s a **semi discontinuous**

**process.**

**DNA replication is semiconservative**

Each daughter cell carries one original strand from the parental cell and one newly
synthesized strand (experimentally demonstrated at the end of 1950s).

**Replication begins at an origin and occurs bidirectionally**

_(experiments made on E-Coli)_
The **replication origin site (Ori C)** is where
replication begins, and forms **replication forks,**
which move **bidirectionally** along the DNA

strand.

Most of the observations made on bacteria can

be extrapolated to humans because **DNA**
**replication is a highly conservative process.**

   - **Origin of replication in** _**E. coli**_ **:** characterized by having repetitive sections of
nucleotides (in tandem), which are found next to binding sites for DnaA protein.
These sites are recognized and bound to by **Dna-A.** This allows the recruitment of a
protein called _**helicase**_ (Dna-B), which will allow the separation of the double strand.
The single strand of DNA is then bound to **SSB** _(single stranded DNA binding_
_proteins)_, which stabilizes the strand.

   **Origin of replication in eukaryotes:** This process is
more complex in nucleated cells due to the size of the

*[PDF p. 205]*

genome, number of chromosomes and the linear sequence…
In eukaryotes there are **multiple origins of replication.** There are **no specific DNA**
**sequences** associated with these origins of replication. Additionally, these origins of
replication are **rich in A and T bases** _(because they form double bonds and not_
_triple, which makes them easier to denature)_, around which the **origin of replication**
**complexes (ORC)** associate. ORCs include, among other auxiliary proteins:

        An hexamer complex (similar to that formed by bacterial DnaA)

        A helicase complex formed by six subunits of the Mcm2-7 protein

        An SSB known as “replication protein A”

        Two different polymerases, DNA-pol-alpha and DNA-pol-gamma

**DNA synthesis proceeds from 5´to 3´ and is semi-discontinuous**

A new DNA strand is always synthesized in this direction **(from 5’ to 3’).** The synthesis of
the **leading strand** is therefore a **continuous** process.
But the synthesis of the opposite strand (the **lagging strand** ), as it is oriented in the
opposite direction, has to be done throughout fragments which **begin at primers** (RNA
primers) and are synthesized from 5´to 3´ in short segments. These segments are known as
**Okazaki Fragments** . In bacteria, they are 1000 to 2000 nucleotides long while in
eukaryotes, 150-200 nucleotides long.

**Enzymes that carry out DNA replication**

**DNA polymerases** were discovered thanks to the work of Arthur Kornberg, who discovered
_DNA-polymerase I._ Since then, many other elements have been discovered in bacteria,
finding that _E. Coli_ contains at least four other distinct DNA polymerases.

There are 2 basic requirements for DNA polymerization:

   - All _DNA polymerases_ require a **template** .
   - A **primer** with a **free 3´ -OH end** is needed for the reaction to begin. These primers
are RNA nucleotides, of about 5 nucleotides, synthesized by _RNA polymerase_
( _**primase**_ ) which is also part of the replication process.

**Reaction catalyzed by DNA polymerase I**

This is an energy favourable
process, catalyzed by
_pyrophosphatase._

**DNA polymerase catalytic mechanism**

*[PDF p. 206]*

In the catalytic site of the _polymerase_, there are **2 Mg2+ ions:**

- The presence of the **upper Mg2+ ion** helps in the execution of the nucleophilic attack of
the hydroxyl group

- The **lower Mg2+ ion** helps in the displacement of the pyrophosphate.

**DNA replication is a very accurate and specific process.** Therefore, there are
many mechanisms and checkpoints that carry out surveillance of the process.

        - Nucleotide selection is based on its **ability to base-pair** (formation of H
bonds) and the **geometry of the base pairs,** specifically the catalytic sites.

Nucleobases can be found in different tautomeric forms. The selection of

bases in the incorrect tautomeric form can lead to pairing with the wrong

base.

        Active sites accommodate both A-T and G-C base pairs, as they have similar
geometries.

        The geometry of incorrectly paired bases can exclude them from the active
site of the enzyme.

_**DNA polymerases**_ **make mistakes,** and insert one incorrect nucleotide for every 10 to the 4
correct ones, due to unusual tautomeric forms. These sorts of errors are reduced by
enzymatic mechanisms: exonuclease activity removes the mispaired nucleotide, and the
polymerase begins again. This activity is known as **proofreading** .

**DNA polymerase I, II and III**

Many other _DNA polymerases_ were discovered, but _**DNA polymerase I**_ was found in _E. Coli_
